INITIAL POST-HEARING BRIEF OF  
CENTRAL HUDSON GAS & ELECTRIC CORPORATION 
 
I. 
INTRODUCTION  
A. 
Overview 
On July 31, 2023, Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation (“Central Hudson” or the 
“Company”) filed these rate cases in the standard manner but with sufficient information to 
permit the case to be a negotiated multi-year rate plan to replace the rates approved in the Rate 
Plan1 established in Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429.  The rate filings aimed to balance the cost 
to customers during the rate year ending June 30, 2025 (“Rate Year”) with the value provided 
through preserving the safety and reliability of the Company’s electric and gas distribution 
systems.  
 
1  
Cases 20-E-0428 et al. - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Electric Service, Order Adopting Terms of Joint 
Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan (Nov. 18, 2021) (“2021 Rate Plan”). 


--- Page 10 ---
 
2 
 
Six items are driving the Company’s need for rate relief and account for more than 80% 
of Central Hudson’s proposed electric and gas increases.  Tr. 3288.  The increased electric rates 
are driven by the replacement of infrastructure that is beyond its useful life; increased labor 
expense; continued and enhanced low income, energy efficiency and heat pump programs; 
increases due to capitalization and financing costs; and timely recovery of costs associated with 
responding to major storms and extreme weather.  Id.  The investment in modern electric 
infrastructure within the proposed rate plan will serve to maintain system reliability, improve 
system resilience, and allow for greater interconnection of local renewable resources and 
advance the state’s goals and policies set forth in the Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act (“CLCPA”).  The gas rate increase is driven by gas capital investments to replace 
obsolete infrastructure and eliminate leak prone pipe (“LPP”) at a level of 15 miles per year.  Tr. 
3289.  The investment in gas infrastructure will improve system reliability and safety and reduce 
greenhouse gas (“GHG”) emissions, thereby also advancing the goals of the CLCPA.  The rate 
filing included several rate moderators designed to keep the requested rate increases as low as 
possible and to facilitate a negotiated outcome. 
Despite the Company’s desire to achieve a mutually acceptable negotiated multi-year rate 
plan, following the submittal of rebuttal testimony Staff indicated that it would not be pursuing 
settlement “based on the totality of the circumstances as well as the positions that have been 
presented in testimony.”2   
 
2  
Cases 23-E-0418 et al. - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Electric Service, Revised Procedural Conference 
Transcript at 8 (Jan. 5, 2024).  Ultimately, four limited settlement stipulations aimed at reducing cross-
examination were reached on the eve of the evidentiary hearings. 


--- Page 11 ---
 
3 
 
The Company’s rate filings have come on the heels of the COVID-19 Pandemic3 and the 
Company’s transition to a new SAP Customer Information System (“CIS”) and several parties 
inappropriately seek to turn these rate cases into a second referendum on the SAP-CIS transition.  
There are a number of reasons why Your Honors should resist the suggestion to go down that 
false path.   
The simple fact is that the challenges the Company faced in replacing its aging legacy 
CIS and launching its new SAP-CIS are not – and should not be – the focus of these rate cases.  
Indeed, certain costs related to the SAP-CIS transition and remediation have been excluded from 
the calculation of Rate Year revenue requirements.  Tr. 3287.  More importantly, with the help of 
its union and management employees, third-party experts, and contractors in correcting the SAP-
CIS transition issues, Central Hudson has put those issues largely behind it.  Tr. 3286.  At this 
time, customers are receiving accurate and timely bills within normal operating parameters.4  Tr. 
3038.  Moreover, Central Hudson’s five key performance indicators (“KPIs”) (% Of Current 
Bills, % Bills Requiring Adjustments, Actual Bills Replaced with Estimates, Call Service Level, 
and % Of Calls Abandoned) have improved relative to the peak of the SAP-CIS issues and the 
 
3  
Like so many things, Central Hudson’s rate filings were influenced by and reflect the fact that they were 
prepared in the aftermath of the COVID-19 Pandemic.  The lingering effects of the COVID-19 Pandemic are 
evident throughout the filing in the context of increases in arrears balances, changes in employee expectations, 
historic inflationary pressures, supply constraints, increases in interest rates and pressure on the Company’s 
credit ratings and financial integrity.  
4  
The Company analyzed the 114 comments received at the in-person Public Statement Hearings held in 
Poughkeepsie, Newburgh, Kingston, and Catskill.  Based on the analysis included in Exhibit 181 (CEP-3R), out 
of the 114 speakers that provided comments only 7 (or approximately 5%) had unresolved issues, primarily 
open PSC Complaints, at the time they provided their comments.  The analysis shows that the majority of 
billing issue comments were regarding past billing issues and so the comments appear to be a lagging indicator 
of customer frustration.  Given that a vast majority of commenters did not have unresolved billing issues, the 
comments cannot be used as a barometer of ongoing billing issues. 


--- Page 12 ---
 
4 
 
metrics are close to levels associated with the period prior to the SAP-CIS launch.5  Tr. 3286; 
Exhibit 180 (CEP-2R). 
There are several reasons why looking backward is inappropriate.  These rate cases, like 
all rate cases, should be focused forward rather than backward as they are intended to establish 
the critical financial and other resources the Company needs to provide customers safe, adequate, 
and reliable service during the Rate Year.  Tr. 3937.  Unfortunately, however, parties’ overall 
approach to these rate cases has in many ways been both overtly and implicitly focused on the 
past, rather than looking forward to the Rate Year.  Regrettably, Staff is among those parties, at 
least on certain issues.  Indeed, Staff is using the SAP-CIS transition issues not only for many of 
Staff’s recommended revenue requirement reductions but also for highly punitive Negative 
Revenue Adjustment (“NRA”) penalties, which would be tripled or quadrupled under certain 
circumstances and are so severe that they would consume more than one-half of the Company’s 
allowed return on equity (“ROE”).6  Staff seeks such penalties even when recommending the 
removal of 122.5 full-time equivalents (“FTEs”) (Tr. 4055; Exhibit 302 (SAP-4 Corrected)) or 
approximately 48% of the 254 incremental FTEs requested by the Company, many of which are 
critical to achieve the performance targets.  Further, many of the positions rejected by Staff are 
within the customer experience area and are necessary to implement monthly meter reading and 
collections activities which are critical to decrease the Company’s arrears balances.  Tr. 3326.   
 
5  
It is important to note that progress towards resolving the SAP-CIS issues was significantly impacted by the 
Columbia Utilities default with the New York Independent System Operator, which generated an unexpected 
billing impact to over 25,000 customers.  Tr.  2995. 
6  
Staff’s proposed NRAs for customer service performance indicators are so punitive that the NRAs associated 
with the customer service performance indicators (“CSPIs”) alone, carry a maximum basis point (“BP”) penalty 
of 288 BPs.  Tr. 3054.  That figure does not include the NRAs for gas safety and electric reliability, which bring 
the Company’s total corporate annual NRA exposure to 375 corporate BPs, approximately 40% of either Staff’s 
or the Company’s recommended ROE in these cases.  Not only are Staff’s NRA recommendations entirely 
arbitrary – because Central Hudson’s metrics measure it against its own, often high level of performance and 
not any objective standard – but they are blatantly discriminatory and unconstitutional.    


--- Page 13 ---
 
5 
 
Even more to the point, Staff makes no secret that the impetus for its dramatic escalation 
of penalties lies directly with the SAP-CIS billing issues.7  It would be bad enough if those 
penalties were imposed for service that was actually inadequate, but, as the Company 
demonstrates, Central Hudson is an efficient, well-run utility that excels in the critical areas of 
gas safety and electric reliability, as Staff concedes.8  With respect to its customer service 
performance, at this time customers are receiving accurate and timely bills within normal 
operating parameters and, as noted, the five KPIs are close to levels associated with the period 
prior to the SAP-CIS launch.    
Finally, and of particular concern and alarm, is the fact that – even without consideration 
of the punitive NRAs – Staff’s recommendations would place the Company’s credit rating in 
grave jeopardy, to the point of an unlawful confiscation.  The Supreme Court has held that the 
allowed return, “should be sufficient to assure confidence in the financial integrity of the 
enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract capital.”9 
In addressing the elements of a confiscatory rate, the Commission determined that “[t]he 
correct standard for assessing whether rate decisions are confiscatory is whether their end result 
reflects a balancing of shareholder and consumer interests.”10  Indeed, Staff’s own witness 
defined such “reasonable terms in this situation as being able to attract capital at a rate that is 
 
7  
Moreover, in many cases Staff measures the Company’s performance against the Company’s prior performance 
and, where the Company’s performance is superior to the statewide average for other utilities (as it often is), 
Central Hudson would be exposed to significant financial penalties when other similarly situated New York 
utilities would not incur a single dollar of NRA penalties.  See, e.g., Tr. 2722. 
8  
See, e.g., Tr. 2755, 2769 (acknowledging exceptional gas safety performance); Tr. 2613-2614 (recognizing that 
the Company has been able to achieve its SAIFI metrics in all but one of the last three years and its CAIDI 
target in nine of the last ten years).  The Company missed its SAIFI target in 2021 only because of an increase 
in minor storm events that year.  Tr. 2613. 
9  
Fed. Power Com. v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591, 603 (1944) (citations omitted). 
10  Case 94-C-0869 - Request of Advance Communications, Inc., for Revision to New York Telephone Company’s 
Reports Provided to Group Bridging Service and Interactive Information Network Service Providers, Order 
Denying New York Telephone Company’s Petition Upon Rehearing at 3 (Sept. 22, 1997).  


--- Page 14 ---
 
6 
 
comparable to other utilities across the country and within New York State.”  Tr. 2483, lines 17-
21.  Here, however, the cumulative effect of Staff’s revenue requirement and deferral treatments 
would upset that Constitutional balance.   
By Staff’s own admission, the end result of Staff’s proposed recommendations would 
almost certainly jeopardize Central Hudson’s credit rating, resulting in an inability to attract 
credit on the same terms and costs as its peers.  Staff is advancing a case that will produce a CFO 
Pre-WC to debt metric of just 9.3%.  Exhibit 272 (ASH-13).  Although Staff tries to claim 
otherwise, there is simply no escaping the fact that Moody’s Investor Service has warned that a 
downgrade could result if CFO Pre-WC to debt is sustained below 14% and/or the New York 
regulatory and political environment were to become incrementally more challenging or there is 
a further deterioration in the stability and predictability of regulatory decisions.  Therefore, the 
Constitutional requirement that the return “should be sufficient to assure confidence in the 
financial integrity of the enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract capital” would be 
transgressed by knowingly and deliberately exposing Central Hudson to a credit downgrade to 
bond ratings several notches below where the Commission has determined they should be.11 
If the confiscation wrought by Staff’s case were not apparent enough, one only needs to consider 
the potential financial effects of the drastic increases to NRA penalties to which no other utility 
either in New York or nationally is exposed.   
 
11  That is not the end of the financial damage done by Staff’s proposals.  Setting aside Staff’s extreme NRA 
recommendations, Staff’s proposed $20 bill credits for estimated bills, adjusted bills and CDG adjusted bills 
will have an adverse financial impact on the Company.  The estimated impact is $1.4 million for the estimated 
bill credit and $1.0 million for the adjusted bill credit.  Tr. 3330.  This is an annual total of $2.4 million or a 14 
BP reduction to the authorized ROE.  Tr. 3330.  Consequently, despite Staff’s claim that its revenue 
requirement will produce a 9.2% ROE, it will only produce an ROE of 9.06% - thereby penalizing Central 
Hudson without any showing that the bill credits are reasonable or warranted. 


--- Page 15 ---
 
7 
 
In deciding these rate cases, therefore, it is important to rely on the record.  It is equally, 
if not more important, to understand the serious consequences that will arise from ignoring the 
factual record in favor of a backward looking and punitive approach to ratemaking.  If Central 
Hudson were to be downgraded, the costs to customers would be high and rate relief necessary to 
restore an appropriate bond rating would take years.  Nor would the effects be limited to Central 
Hudson.  The consequences of such a punitive rate order would have far reaching results, not 
only for the Company alone but for all other utilities in New York as well.   
The evidence submitted by the Company in these proceedings is overwhelming and 
Central Hudson urges the Commission to adopt its recommended electric and gas revenue 
requirements, and other proposals discussed herein, as a matter of public interest. 
B. 
Procedural History 
On July 31, 2023, Central Hudson filed tariff leaves and supporting testimony and 
exhibits for new rates and charges for electric and gas service to be effective July 1, 2024.  The 
Company proposed to increase electric and gas revenues by $139.5 million and $41.5 million, 
respectively, for the Rate Year.  The Company’s initial filing also included additional forecasted 
financial information for the 12-month periods ending June 30, 2026 and 2027, in the interest of 
reaching a multi-year settlement, which would allow for rate levelization and lower customer bill 
impacts.   
Consistent with established Commission practice, Administrative Law Judges (“ALJ”) 
James A. Costello and Ashley Moreno were appointed to conduct a rate proceeding to review the 
Company’s filing.  On August 29, 2023, ALJs Costello and Moreno held a procedural 
conference that was immediately followed by a technical presentation to all parties by the 
Company discussing various aspects of the rate filings.  On September 8, 2023, the ALJs issued 
a ruling that adopted a schedule for the submission of Staff and intervenor testimony as well as

On December 21, 2023, a procedural conference was held to discuss whether the parties

settlement conference, a limited number of issues were able to be resolved via stipulation. 
Each stipulation has a similar introduction and consistent legal “boiler plate” language. 
The signatories to each stipulation agree that the resolutions reached therein are just and 
reasonable and otherwise in accordance with the New York State Public Service Law, the 
Commission's regulations and applicable Commission orders.  The parties to the stipulations also 
request that Your Honors adopt the stipulations, without modification, in any Recommended 
Decision or Your Honors’ Report and that the Commission adopt them in its final decision.   
The Commission’s Opinion, Order and Resolution Adopting Settlement Procedures and 
Guidelines in Case 90-M-025514 sets forth the standard to be used in assessing the 
reasonableness of a rate settlement (the “Settlement Guidelines”).15  The Settlement Guidelines 
require settlements such as the stipulations to be “just, reasonable and in the public interest.”16

--- Page 18 ---
 
10 
 
In determining whether a proposed settlement is in the public interest, the Commission has 
consistently considered the following factors: 
 The settlement's consistency with law and the regulatory, economic, social and 
environmental policies of the Commission and the State;  
 Whether the agreement compares favorably with the likely result of full litigation 
and is within the range of reasonable outcomes;  
 Whether the settlement strikes a fair balance among interests of ratepayers, 
investors and the long-term soundness of the utility;  
 Whether the settlement reflects the agreement of normally adverse parties;  
 The existence of a rational basis for decision;  
 The completeness of the record; and  
 Whether the settlement is contested.  
The last two factors simply guide the Commission in its assessment.17  To assist Your Honors in 
evaluating the stipulations, when discussed throughout this brief, the Company summarizes the 
parties’ litigation positions, presents a rational basis for the key features, and demonstrates that 
each stipulation is in the public interest.  
Evidentiary hearings were held over ten days between January 24, 2024, and February 6, 
2024, and involved cross-examination of various witnesses produced by the Company and Staff.  
The hearing record of these proceedings consists of over 4,500 pages of evidentiary transcripts 
and 645 exhibits.

1. 
Use of Net Regulatory Liabilities for Rate Moderation 
The Direct Testimony of the Company’s Policy Panel indicated that the Company 
projected having $22 million and $12 million for electric and gas, respectively, of net regulatory 
liabilities available to moderate customer bill impacts within the context of multi-year settlement 
negotiations.  Tr. 3298.  Specifically for electric, these net regulatory balances exist solely due to 
the Company removing regulatory assets of $60 million associated with deferred storm response, 
energy efficiency, and heat pump programs through a ten-year amortization, in lieu of a more 
immediate recovery.  Tr. 3298. 
The Staff Policy Panel, however, suggested a use of additional moderation even in a 
litigated case and proposed immediate use of one-third of net regulatory liability balances 
available while agreeing that the $60 million of regulatory assets be recovered through a ten-year 
amortization.  Tr. 3946-3947. 
The Company continues to believe that use of the net regulatory liability balances for 
moderation is better suited for use in a multi-year rate plan when it can serve to support rate 
stability and levelization for customers.  Additionally, the use of the significant net regulatory 
liability balances will have the impact of further weakening the Company’s CFO Pre-WC/Debt 
metric, posing yet another risk to the financial integrity and credit rating metrics of the 
Company.  It is more appropriate to utilize the use of net regulatory liability balances within the 
context of a multi-year rate plan, when they can be utilized for levelization across rate years 
while balancing the impacts on the Company’s financial integrity with customer bill impacts.

D. 
Compliance with CLCPA 
The Company’s rate filing contains a number of proposals that support the GHG 
emissions reduction goals of the CLCPA, with many of these reductions accruing to the benefit 
of disadvantaged communities.  As discussed in more detail below, the Company’s rate filing is 
consistent with the requirements of the CLCPA and related policies established by the 
Commission. 
The CLCPA requires that New York’s GHG emissions be 40% below 1990 levels by 
2030 and 85% below 1990 levels by 2050.18  Further, the CLCPA requires that: 1) by 2030, 70% 
of statewide electric generation shall be generated by renewable energy systems; and 2) by 2040, 
the statewide electric grid shall be zero emissions.19  CLCPA Section 7(2) requires state agencies 
to consider whether their administrative approvals and decisions “are inconsistent with or 
interfere with the attainment of the statewide greenhouse gas emissions limits” established in 
ECL § 75-0107.20  CLCPA Section 7(3) requires state agencies to ensure that their decisions will 
not “disproportionately burden disadvantaged communities” and to “prioritize reductions of 
greenhouse gas emissions and co-pollutants in disadvantaged communities.”21 
 
18  Environmental Conservation Law (“ECL”) § 75-0107(1).   
19  Public Service Law § 66(1). 
20  Chapter 106 of the Laws of 2019 at § 7(2). 
21  Chapter 106 of the Laws of 2019 at § 7(3).

As the Commission has repeatedly determined, its application of the CLCPA “cannot be 
done in a vacuum but, rather, must be balanced against and consistent with the legal mandates of 
the Public Service Law, which requires the provision of safe and reliable service at just and 
reasonable rates.”22  Essentially, “[t]he CLCPA does not override the Commission’s 
responsibility to ensure reliability, public safety and reasonable rates in favor of emissions 
reductions.”23  “The Commission’s core mission is to ensure the continued provision of safe and 
adequate utility service at just and reasonable rates.  Indeed, the Commission views this objective 
as paramount during the transition to cleaner energy systems.”24 
 
The Company participates in various generic proceedings established by the Commission 
to implement policies and programs designed to achieve the CLCPA’s objectives.  For example, 
in 2020 the Commission commenced a gas planning proceeding to ensure that gas utilities align 
their planning and operational practices with the CLCPA.25  The Gas Planning Proceeding 
requires gas utilities to file long-term plans that include demand forecasts incorporating energy 
efficiency, electrification, demand response and non-pipes alternatives, as well as reporting GHG 
emissions for all proposed solutions to meeting gas supply and demand.  The Company filed its 
initial long-term plan (“Long-Term Plan”) on February 7, 2024, in Case 23-G-0676.  A technical

On May 12, 2022, the Commission also initiated a proceeding to monitor progress made

These processes initiated by the Commission in the Gas Planning Proceeding and the 
CLCPA Implementation Order, as well as regulations forthcoming from the Department of 
Environmental Conservation, will enable a more comprehensive evaluation in future rate cases 
with respect to consistency with the CLCPA.  For now, prior Commission precedent provides the 
best available guidance for determining how rate case filings may be consistent with CLCPA 
Sections 7(2) and 7(3).

1. 
The Company’s Filing is Consistent with CLCPA § 7(2) 
CLCPA § 7(2) requires the Commission to determine whether a proposed rate filing is 
consistent with the state’s emissions reduction goals.  The Commission has found that a rate plan 
complies with the CLCPA’s emissions reduction goals to the extent it “appropriately balance[s] 
the interests in reliability, public safety, and reasonable rates with emission reductions and clean 
energy objectives” and serves as “an important step in the ongoing process of achieving the 
CLCPA’s greenhouse gas limits, one that will be built upon in future rate cases and other 
Commission proceedings.”28   
As an initial matter, the Company has made significant progress toward GHG emissions 
reductions, and estimates that its initiatives implemented between 2020 and 2030, including the 
initiatives proposed in this rate filing, will result in the Company exceeding greenhouse gas 
emissions reductions of 40% by 2030.  Tr. 2790; Exhibit 97 (CLSP-1).  The initiatives proposed 
in this case will enable the Company to achieve GHG emissions reductions consistent with the 
CLCPA.   
Under the Commission’s standard articulated above, the Company’s rate filing as a whole 
complies with CLCPA § 7(2).  In particular, the Company’s rate filing establishes just and 
reasonable rates and enables activities that promote reliability, resiliency and public safety while 
at the same time containing a number of provisions and items that take an important step in 
achieving the CLCPA’s clean energy goals and greenhouse gas emissions reduction targets, such 
as:29

Terms of Joint Proposal at 83 (Jan. 20, 2022) (“Niagara Mohawk Rate Order”). 
29  All of these items are described and supported in more detail in their respective sections of this brief.

 Increased funding for energy efficiency and heat pump programs (Tr. 3292);

rate orders previously approved by the Commission as consistent with CLCPA § 7(2).  Tr. 3985-
3986.  Further, the Staff Clean Energy Panel indicated that the Company’s energy efficiency,

2. 
The Company’s Filing is Consistent with CLCPA § 7(3) 
Pursuant to CLCPA § 7(3), the Commission must find that the Company’s rate filing 
does not disproportionately burden disadvantaged communities.34  The Commission has 
previously provided that “it will adopt a [rate plan] consistent with the CLCPA where the 
proposed rate plan allows the utility at issue to continue providing safe and reliable service and 
the impact of the proposed rate plan is consistent with ratemaking and revenue allocation 
principles.”35  In determining whether a rate plan is consistent with CLCPA § 7(3), the 
Commission also looks at impacts related to capital projects, as well as whether the Joint 
Proposal contains low income protections.36

Based on the above criteria, the Commission should find that the Company’s rate filing

(CLSP-2).  This demonstrates the significant benefits the Company’s rate filing would provide to 
customers in disadvantaged communities.  For example, the Company estimates that 
approximately 84% of the greenhouse gas emissions reductions associated with the Company’s 
LPP replacement program will occur in disadvantaged communities.  Id.  Further, the

REVENUE ISSUES 
A. 
Revenue / Sales / Customer Forecasts 
The Company and Staff entered into a Stipulation Regarding Sales Forecast and Price 
Out Revenues (“Forecast Stipulation”).  See Exhibit 513.  The Forecast Stipulation resolves all of 
the outstanding issues between Staff and the Company related to the Company’s sales forecast 
and pre-rate relief price out revenues for the Rate Year.  To assist Your Honors in assessing the 
 
39  NYSEG-RG&E Rate Order at 58. 


--- Page 29 ---
 
21 
 
Forecast Stipulation, this section summarizes the parties’ litigation positions, presents a rational 
basis for the stipulation, and demonstrates that the stipulation is in the public interest. 
1. 
Electric Forecasts 
The electric sales forecast presented in the Company’s initial filing was developed 
utilizing various econometric or time series models for different service classes using actual 
monthly billed customer and sales data for the period January 2004 to December 2022.  Tr. 
1799;Exhibit 120 (FRP-4).  The Company used econometric models to develop forecasts for 
certain customer classes and time series models (e.g., historic constant or historic trend) for 
others.  Tr. 1803.  The Company’s small industrial demand and non-demand customer forecasts 
were developed by maintaining historic test year levels, and the Company forecasts S.C. No. 13 
individually due to the small number of customers in the class.  Tr. 1803.  Electric sales volumes 
were forecast using econometric models for all classes except S.C. No. 13, the three lighting 
classes, and interdepartmental sales.  The Company also made certain post-model adjustments to 
its electric forecast to account for increased adoption of EVs, photovoltaic (“PV”) net metering, 
and heat pumps.  Tr. 1816. 
The Company used Statistically Adjusted End-Use (“SAE”) variables to forecast electric 
residential and commercial sales.  As described in the Forecasting and Rates Panel testimony, the 
SAE approach integrates structural changes in end-use saturation and efficiency trends and 
addresses the interaction of different economic factors by constructing end-use variables for 
heating, cooling, and other (base use).  These end-use variables consider different drivers, 
including weather, price, and economic drivers, as well as housing square footage and thermal 
shell integrity for residential customers.  Tr. 1806.  SAE variables incorporate the effects of 
changes in technology, building and appliance standards, and programmatic efforts on sales, 
which have offset customer and economic growth.  Tr. 1806.  As such, SAE variables allow the 


--- Page 30 ---
 
22 
 
Company to account for reasons for electric sales decline that is not possible in a traditional 
regression model using economic variables.  Tr. 1806. 
To develop the electric revenue forecast, the Company allocated its electric customer and 
sales forecasts to a service class or sub-class based on an average annual distribution of actual 
revenue account customers and sales to service class or sub-class for the three years of 2020, 
2021 and 2022.  Tr. 1822.  Billing demands were based on historical load factor trends, and the 
split of on-peak/off-peak usage for residential time-of-use (“TOU”) was based on the actual 
annual split realized for 2022.  Tr. 1822.  The forecasts were then priced at rates approved in the 
Company’s previous rate case.  Tr. 1822.   
Staff opposed the Company’s customer and sales forecasts and developed their own 
forecasts.  Staff testified that it was concerned with the Company’s use of SAE variables, arguing 
that SAE variables resulted in a non-transparent, uninterpretable model because it was not 
possible to evaluate the individual inputs in the composite SAE variables.  Tr. 3593-3594.  Staff 
also argued that the SAE variables posited the existence of a fixed relationship between variables 
that are not included within the model.  Tr. 3594.  Staff testified that, in preparing its own 
forecasts, it incorporated SAE variables by separating out the underlying components and using 
them as explanatory variables in Staff’s econometric model.  Tr. 3599.  Staff stated that the 
Company’s revenue price-out approach was reasonable (Tr. 4241) but indicated that it was not 
able to include post-model adjustments for EVs, PV net metering, or heat pumps due to timing 
issues and would provide an update prior to the evidentiary hearing in this proceeding.  Tr. 3631-
3632, 4243. 
In rebuttal, the Company testified that it continued to support the use of SAE variables, as 
several organizations, including the New York Independent System Operator and the U.S. 


--- Page 31 ---
 
23 
 
Energy Information Administration, had used an SAE approach in projecting future energy usage 
and demand in light of emerging technologies and policies, such as the CLCPA.  Tr. 1882-1883.  
The Company’s rebuttal also identified some generic issues it had evaluating Staff’s forecasts, 
particularly in tracing the linkage between the forecasts of the Staff Forecasting Panel with the 
revenue price-out of the Staff Rates Panel.  Tr. 1883.   
On December 19, 2023, Staff submitted supplemental testimony from the Staff Updated 
Forecast and Price Out Panel, which presented updated and corrected Rate Year sales and 
customer forecasts, and associated revenue price-outs, and addressed issues Staff identified with 
respect to the Company’s post model adjustments for EVs, PV net metering, and heat pumps.  Tr. 
3636.  Staff disagreed with the Company’s use of cumulative adoption of EVs and PVs, arguing 
that the historical data already captured prior adoption for EVs and PVs and that a post-model 
adjustment should only account for future adoption incremental to the existing trend captured in 
the models.  Tr. 3642.  Additionally, Staff reflected available data through September 2023 in its 
post model adjustments.  Tr. 3642-3643.  The Company’s Supplemental Testimony of the 
Forecasting and Rates Panel responded to Staff’s supplemental testimony on December 29, 2023, 
addressing Staff’s forecast and price-out corrections as well as its post model adjustments.  The 
Company disagreed with Staff’s focus on solely incremental PV after September 2023, as this 
failed to reflect the cumulative of PV installed from October 2023 through the end of the Rate 
Year.  Tr. 1926.  The Company also expressed concern with Staff’s EV adjustment, questioning 
whether two data points (2021 and 2022) and a pro-rated portion of 2023 data was sufficient to 
develop a slope to measure incremental EVs.  Tr. 1927-1928. 
The Company and Staff entered into the Forecast Stipulation in order to resolve all issues 
between them related to the electric sales forecast and limit the scope of the evidentiary hearing.  


--- Page 32 ---
 
24 
 
The stipulated sales forecast, as well as the associated revenue price out, is set forth in Schedule 
A to the Forecast Stipulation.  See Exhibit 513.  As stated therein, the Forecast Stipulation 
reflects Staff’s testimonial position for the electric forecast in this proceeding, with two minor 
updates to revenues for S.C. No. 8 and S.C. 13.  The Company submits that this stipulated sales 
forecast is reasonable and should be adopted by the Commission.  The Forecast Stipulation is 
supported by the Company and Staff, typically adverse parties and the only parties to submit 
testimony regarding the electric sales forecast in this proceeding and is not opposed by any party.  
The forecast is supported by the record and represents a reasonable outcome that could have been 
reached by Your Honors had this issue been fully litigated in this proceeding.  Accordingly, the 
electric sales forecast set out in the Forecast Stipulation meets the public interest standard set 
forth in the Commission’s Settlement Guidelines and should be adopted by Your Honors and the 
Commission without modification.  
2. 
Gas Forecasts 
The Company prepared its gas customer and sales forecast using the same general 
methodology described above for the electric forecast, including the use of SAE variables.  The 
Company used econometric models to forecast residential and commercial heating and non-
heating class customers, and assumed no growth in the gas industrial customer forecast.  Tr. 
1804.  Gas sales were forecast using econometric models for all firm service classes, excluding 
interdepartmental and S.C. No. 11.  Tr. 1804-1805.  The Company’s gas sales forecast was 
allocated by service class between heating and non-heating sub-classes for the purposes of block 
billing distribution, and were spread between blocks based on an O-give analysis.  Tr. 1824.  The 
Company’s revenue forecast excluded S.C. No. 11 revenues from the Danskammer Generating 
Station (“Danskammer”), which is discussed in more detail below, and included an estimate of 
interruptible gas sales and transport service for S.C. Nos. 8 and 9 based on historic usage for the 


--- Page 33 ---
 
25 
 
prior 36 months.  Tr. 1825-1827.  The Company also made post model adjustments to its gas 
revenue price out to reflect the adoption of heat pumps.  Tr. 1804. 
Staff opposed the Company’s gas customer and sales forecasts on similar grounds to its 
opposition to the electric forecasts, as discussed above, including its concern with the use of SAE 
variables.  Staff also recommended adjustments to the Company’s gas revenue forecast, 
including opposing the Company’s exclusion of Danskammer revenues, as discussed below, and 
recommended adjustments to the revenue forecast for S.C. No. 6 high volume customers.  Tr. 
4243, 4258.  The Company continued to support the use of SAE variables for its gas forecasts in 
its rebuttal testimony, and testified that it had the same issues evaluating Staff’s gas models as it 
did for electric.  The Company also opposed Staff’s use of an economic variable specific to the 
Catskill region of the Company’s service territory to develop its gas forecasts for Central Hudson 
commercial non-heating customers and sales.  Tr. 1884.  In supplemental testimony, Staff made a 
post model adjustment to its revenue price out for heat pumps, which was not opposed by the 
Company in its response. 
The Forecast Stipulation between the Company and Staff proposes to resolve all issues 
between them with regard to the gas sales forecast, and sets forth the stipulated forecast on 
Schedule B thereto.  See Exhibit 513.  For the same reasons discussed above with respect to the 
electric forecast, the Company submits that the gas forecast set forth in the Forecast Stipulation is 
reasonable and should be adopted by Your Honors and the Commission without modification. 
B. 
Revenue Tracking Mechanisms 
The Company proposed to continue, or to continue with modifications, several revenue 
tracking mechanisms in this proceeding.  Specifically, the Company proposed to continue its 
tracking and deferral mechanisms for Lost Revenues (Finance Charges and Reconnection Fee 
Revenues), Net Lost Revenue, Platform Service Revenues, and its electric and gas Revenue 


--- Page 34 ---
 
26 
 
Decoupling Mechanisms (“RDMs”).  Tr. 1510-1512; Exhibit 90 (ATP-11).  The Company also 
proposed to continue with modifications its tracking and deferral mechanisms for Danskammer 
Gas Revenues and Legacy Hydro Revenue.  Exhibit 90 (ATP-11).40   
The Company proposed to continue its electric and gas RDMs with minor modifications.  
The Company’s current electric RDM uses a revenue per class model and is applicable to S.C. 
Nos. 1, 2 Non-Demand, 2 Primary Demand, 2 Secondary Demand, 3, 5, 6, 8, 13, and 14.  
Tr. 1867.  Under the RDM, electric revenues are compared to a delivery revenue target on a 
monthly basis.  Tr. 1867.  At the end of a semi-annual RDM period, total delivery revenue 
excess/shortfalls are refunded/surcharged to customers through service class or sub-class specific 
RDM adjustments applicable during a corresponding semi-RDM adjustment period (six months 
beginning February 1 or six months beginning August 1 immediately following the semi-annual 
RDM period).  Tr. 1867.  Monthly adjustments are made to the delivery revenue target to reflect 
the revenue impact of municipal streetlighting customers that own and maintain their facilities, 
customers participating in the EV TOU whole house rate, S.C. No. 6, and the Residential 
Managed Charging Program.  Tr. 1868.  In compliance with the Commission’s March 16, 2022 
Order Establishing an Allocated Cost of Service Study Methodology for Standby and Buyback 
Service Rates and Energy Storage Contract Demand Charge Exemption issued in Case 15-E-
0751, the Company proposed to modify the electric RDM to include S.C. No. 10 – Buyback 
Service.  Tr. 1869-1870.  No party opposed this modification.  Therefore, the Company submits 
that the Commission should adopt this proposal. 
 
40  The tracking mechanisms for Lost Revenues, Legacy Hydro Revenues, Danskammer Gas Revenues are 
discussed in Sections III.G.5, XIV.A.4, and XIV.G, respectively. 


--- Page 35 ---
 
27 
 
The Company’s gas RDM also uses a revenue per class model and is applicable to S.C. 
Nos. 1 and 12 combined and S.C. Nos. 2, 6, and 13 combined, as well as S.C. No. 11, subject to 
certain deadbands and thresholds established in the Company’s prior rate case.  Tr. 1868.  
Monthly gas revenues are compared to a delivery revenue target, adjusted by the Weather 
Normalization Adjustment, with any revenue excess/shortfall refunded to/recovered from 
customers over a semi-annual RDM adjustment period (six months beginning February 1 or the 
six months beginning August 1).  Tr. 1868-1869.  The Company proposed to modify the recovery 
period for the RDM adjustment from semi-annual to annual.  Tr. 1870.  The Company noted that 
over the past 3.5 years the S.C. No. 2 RDM Adjustment factor has swung from a credit to a 
surcharge every six months and that a longer recovery period would allow a more gradual change 
from a credit to a surcharge over time, mitigating bill impacts on customers due to the seasonal 
nature of the gas business.  Tr. 1870.  No party opposed this modification.  Therefore, the 
Company submits that the Commission should adopt this proposal. 
Staff recommended an additional modification to both the electric and gas RDMs to 
remove language on Leaf 163.5.4 of the electric tariff and Leaf 129 of the gas tariff that requires 
the Company to adjust its actual billed revenue by multiplying the actual billed sales in the month 
of a rate increase by the new rates.  Tr. 4273.  This results in the Company’s revenues being 
overstated in the month in which a rate change occurs.  Tr. 4273.  The Company proposed to 
include a forecast amount in its revenue requirements to offset this overstated revenue.  Tr. 737-
738.  Staff rejected the Company’s proposed revenue requirement adjustment and instead 
recommended that the Company remove the language from the RDM that causes this issue.  Tr. 
4273-4274.  The Company accepted Staff’s proposal to eliminate this language and the 
corresponding modification to revenue requirements.  Tr. 1911.

III. 
EXPENSE ISSUES 
A. 
O&M 
1. 
Areas of Agreement 
There are several expense items for which there is no or only minor differences between 
the Company’s and Staff’s litigation positions.  Those items, including Staff’s and the 
Company’s recommended revenue requirement for each item, are set forth in Appendix 1, 
Schedule B hereto.41  Because there is no or little difference between Staff’s and the Company’s 
recommendations for these items, they are not being addressed in this brief.  
2. 
Site Investigation and Remediation (“SIR”)  
In the context of a potential multi-year deal, the Company’s Policy Panel proposed a 
potential change to the traditional calculation of SIR as a rate moderator.  To be clear, that 
proposal was explicitly couched as a potential settlement offer (Tr. 3299-3300): 
The fourth potential source of moderation could be realized by basing the 
Environmental Site Investigation and Remediation (“SIR”) rate allowance on a 
forecast of spend in the Rate Year instead of the traditional methodology, which 
uses an average of the prior three-year actual spend through the Test Year as the 
basis for determining the revenue requirement.  This change would result in a 
combined change to the electric and gas revenue requirements of $4 million.  
Within the context of multi-year settlement negotiations, Central Hudson is open 
to discussions regarding additional moderation that can be utilized to reduce the 
bill impact to customers. 
 
Incorrectly stating that the Company proposed this methodology as “an alternative” in a 
one-year litigated case, Staff’s SIR Panel (“SSIRP”) argued that the costs forecasted for the Rate 
Year should not include costs for remediation work which will not occur during the Rate Year.  
Tr. 3497.  Based on the Company’s response to DPS-291, Staff argues that the SIR allowance 
 
41  The differences between Staff’s and the Company’s recommendations for the Legal and Maintenance of 
Buildings and Grounds expense items shown on Appendix 1, Schedule A are also minor and not addressed in 
this brief. 


--- Page 37 ---
 
29 
 
should be limited to the total projected costs during the Rate Year of $0.986 million.  The Rate 
Year electric and gas projections are $0.789 million and $0.197 million, respectively.  Id. 
Tellingly, Staff does not dispute that it is proposing to abandon the traditional projection 
methodology of a three-year historical average for this element of expense and to set rates equal 
to projected Rate Year spending.  Tr. 787.  Spending for SIR tends to fluctuate from year-to-
year, depending on the status of specific remediation projects and given that projects run into 
costly increases during active remediation.  Id.  The use of a historic average in this instance 
allows customers to pay for the expense of costly remediation projects in a consistent manner 
year-over-year.  Moreover, collection based on the historic average in this instance operates as a 
reserve, eliminating potential volatility related to this cost element in the future.  Id.  Indeed, if 
the situation were reversed, and the Rate Year SIR expense were estimated to be several millions 
more than the three-year average, there is little doubt that Staff would be advocating the use of 
the average to moderate rates.  The traditional three-year average methodology should be 
continued.  
3. 
Vegetation Management 
The Company’s vegetation management programs include a Transmission Right-of-Way 
(“ROW”) Program and a Distribution Line Clearance Program.  The Transmission ROW 
Program covers seven ROW maintenance activities: ROW floor maintenance, mowing and 
access roads, side trimming, danger tree removal, hot spot trimming, ROW edge reclamation, 
and Legal and Environmental.  Tr. 3567.  The Company requested approximately $3.60 million 
for its transmission ROW vegetation management for the Rate Year.  Tr. 2087.  Staff had no 
concerns with the Company’s proposed budget and found the request to be reasonable.  Tr. 3567-
3577. 


--- Page 38 ---
 
30 
 
The Company’s electric vegetation Distribution Line Clearance Program is designed to 
reduce outages caused by trees or limb failures.  The program establishes greater side clearances, 
reduces vegetation overhanging electric infrastructure, and includes the removal of tall growing 
species from within the ROW.  The two main components of the program are the contractor 
scheduled on-road and off-road trimming activities.  In addition, the program includes routine 
maintenance activities such as danger tree activity, trouble orders or priority work orders, 
flagging activities and hazard tree removals, referred to as the Hazard Tree Program.  Tr. 3549-
3550.  The Company requested $28.50 million in the Rate Year for all vegetation Distribution 
Line Clearance Program activities, an increase of approximately $4.00 million over existing 
rates.  Tr. 2093-2094.  The Staff Electric Vegetation Management Panel supported all but $2.24 
million of the requested $28.5 million.  Tr. 3550.  Staff’s adjustment related to the number of 
incremental bucket tree crews the Company plans to utilize for its Hazard Tree Program in the 
Rate Year.  Tr. 3558-3559.  The Company’s revenue requirement includes expense for seven 
incremental bucket tree crews while Staff only supports three additional crews.  Tr. 3560.  In 
rebuttal testimony, the Company continued to support the need for seven incremental bucket tree 
crews.  Tr. 2139-2140. 
In addition to the number of incremental bucket tree crews, the Company and Staff also 
disagreed on future reporting requirements related to vegetation management.  Staff 
recommended that the Commission establish quarterly and annual reporting requirements for the 
Distribution Line Clearance Program that included detailed updates on a number of vegetation 
management activities, including separate reporting for danger tree and hazard tree removals.  
Tr. 3563-3567.  As part of its rebuttal filing, the Company raised concerns that such additional 
reporting requirements created an unneeded administrative burden because the requested 


--- Page 39 ---
 
31 
 
information was already being provided to Staff during quarterly meetings.  The Company also 
noted that the requested reporting was inconsistent with the way the Company manages 
emergency storm response for both major and minor storm events.  Tr. 2141-2144. 
The Stipulation Regarding Electric Capital and Operations (the “Electric Stipulation”) 
(Exhibit 516) resolves Staff’s and the Company’s differences with respect to the number of 
incremental bucket tree crews for the Company’s Hazard Tree Program.  Specifically, pursuant 
to the Electric Stipulation, to further harden Central Hudson’s system, the Company will increase 
the number of its Hazard Tree Program bucket truck crews from five to eight.  Staff and the 
Company agree that the revenue requirement impact of approximately $1.683 million associated 
with these three incremental crews will be reflected in Central Hudson's electric revenue 
requirement.  The Electric Stipulation also resolves the Company’s and Staff’s disagreement 
related to vegetation management reporting by requiring the Company to work with Staff to 
provide quarterly reporting on all Distribution Vegetation Management Programs. 
The Company and Staff, typically adverse parties, settled their dispute regarding the 
number of incremental bucket tree crews by agreeing to utilize Staff’s litigation position.  The 
Electric Stipulation as it relates to this issue is supported by the record and falls within the range 
of testimony filed by the Company and Staff.  The agreed upon incremental bucket tree crews 
and overall level of Distribution Line Clearance expense are consistent with the Public Service 
Law and Commission precedent.  For these issues, the Electric Stipulation also strikes a fair 
balance between the interests of customers and the Company.  Given these factors, it is 
reasonable to conclude that had this issue been fully litigated, Your Honors and the Commission 
would have reached a similar result.  Accordingly, this portion of the Electric Stipulation meets 


--- Page 40 ---
 
32 
 
the public interest standard set forth in the Commission’s Settlement Guidelines and should be 
adopted by Your Honors and the Commission without modification. 
4. 
Labor Expense 
The Company and Staff strongly disagree regarding labor expense for the Rate Year.  As 
shown in the testimony of the Company’s Revenue Requirement Panel, the direct Labor expense 
is $124.6 million.  Exhibit 241 (RRP-3R).  In contrast, the Staff Accounting Panel testified to a 
Labor expense of $108.6 million.  A careful review of the record demonstrates that the 
Company’s Labor expense position as set forth in the Company’s rebuttal testimony should be 
adopted.42    
a. Incremental FTEs 
One key driver of the labor expense disagreement between Staff and the Company relates 
to the number of incremental FTEs needed for the operation of the Company.  Staff’s initial 
reduction in total FTEs reduces labor expense by approximately $6.0 million for electric and 
$1.7 million for gas.  Tr. 4055.  As the Company’s Workforce, Compensation and Benefits Panel 
testified and set forth in its Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R), Staff’s proposed adjustments to headcount 
are severe, with Staff proposing to remove 122.5 FTEs (Tr. 4055; Exhibit 302 (SAP-4 
Corrected)) or approximately 48% of the Company’s requested staffing increase of 254 
incremental FTEs (205 in the bridge period and 49 in the Rate Year).43  Tr. 4055.  Furthermore, 
79 of these positions or approximately 66% of Staff’s proposed reductions are within customer

Where Staff and the Company agree on FTE positions, we will not address those

provides justification for the other FTEs by individual position.   
One clear and powerful demonstration of the Company’s actual need for the incremental 
FTEs disallowed by Staff, one that speaks for itself, is that the Company has already hired 38 of 
these positions (see Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R)) because they “were necessary to maintain 
Company operations and the delivery of service to customers.”  Tr. 393, lines 10-13.  Staff’s 
denial of a significant number of the incremental customer service positions sought by the 
Company is greatly troubling and counterintuitive because cutting back on incremental FTEs in 
this area would significantly impede the Company’s ability to reinitiate critical collections 
activities, provide improved customer service, and implement monthly meter reading while still 
meeting targeted customer service performance levels.  In many instances, the reductions 
proposed by Staff seem targeted toward removing incremental human resources that are 
necessary to support the Company’s most important initiatives in these critical areas.  Tr. 3327.   
In certain respects, Staff’s significant reduction in recommended incremental headcount 
in the customer service center, monthly meter reading and billing areas appears punitive in 
nature, since Staff’s reductions remove human resources required to provide customer service at 
a level that would avoid triggering the dramatic increase in the potential CSPI NRAs Staff seeks 
to impose on the Company in these rate proceedings.  
An illustrative and telling example of the incongruity of seeking to impose draconian 
NRAs while at the same time denying the Company needed human resources to achieve CSPI 


--- Page 43 ---
 
35 
 
targets to avoid those NRAs is illustrated by Staff’s proposed disallowance of 52 incremental 
FTEs needed by the Company to restart and manage residential customer collections activity.44  
Tr. 3020; Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  The Company requires additional collectors, contact center 
personnel and billing department personnel in order to carry out residential collections efforts, 
address customer inquiries and concerns, match customers with assistance programs and help 
customers through the collection process.  Central Hudson is not currently engaging in 
residential collection activities but is preparing to do so as a needed method to address growing 
residential customer arrears.  These incremental FTEs will be critical to achieving the CSPI 
targets as call volume and customer complaints are likely to rise with an increase in residential 
collection activities. 
As the Company’s Customer Experience Panel testified, the number of residential 
customers in arrears has greatly increased from a pre-pandemic level of 21,000 to a current level 
of 66,000.  Tr. 3020.  The Staff Consumer Services Panel conceded during cross-examination 
that termination and collection activities is a primary tool that utilities have to control arrears 
aside from customer communication and outreach.  Tr. 4549.  It appears that Staff’s concern 
about growing arrears balances (Tr. 4330-4331, 4403) is insufficient to overcome Staff’s 
unwillingness to allow the Company the incremental FTEs necessary for the Company to prepare 
for and handle likely customer impacts of restarting collection activity.   
Staff’s allegations that the Company failed to provide a clear timeline as to when it will 
resume residential collections and service terminations (Tr. 4403) is contradicted by Staff’s own 
testimony which notes the fact that the Company provided in discovery a targeted date of March

b. Vacancy Rate 
Staff seeks to apply an overly aggressive and unreasonable vacancy rate based on flawed 
reasoning.  Staff asserts that the Company’s methodology to project labor expense, which uses 
annual salaries, overstates such expense because it does not consider positions that have been 
vacant during the year or a portion of the year.  Tr. 4058-4059.  As a result, the Staff Accounting 
Panel recommends the Commission apply a 3.5% vacancy rate adjustment to total labor costs to 
reflect the reduced wages the Company will not incur when positions are vacant during the year.  
Tr. 4059.  The basis for the 3.5% adjustment is information provided by the Company in 
response to DPS-380 (Exhibit 298 (SAP-1)), which projected an attrition rate of approximately 
3.5% by year end.  Staff’s proposed vacancy rate adjustment should be rejected for several 
reasons. 
First, Staff’s recommended adjustment is based on a false equivalency – that the 
Company’s attrition rate and vacancy rate are one and the same.  Tr. 4059.  Attrition and vacancy 
are decidedly different measurements.  The attrition rate measures the rate at which employees 
depart an organization, whether voluntarily or involuntarily, whereas the vacancy rate measures 


--- Page 50 ---
 
42 
 
the percentage of vacant positions in an organization over a specific period.  As Staff conceded 
during cross-examination, employee departures do not necessarily create permanent vacancies.  
Tr. 4161-4162.  Moreover, Staff’s use of an attrition rate for its proposed adjustment is fatally 
flawed because it does not take into consideration when the departures occur or how long it takes 
to fill a position.  Tr. 4162-4166.  As a result, even if all of the Company’s departures occurred 
in December (resulting in a single month of vacancy), Staff acknowledged that its vacancy rate 
adjustment would remove an entire year’s worth of expense for those departed employees.  Tr. 
4163.  Likewise, even if it took the Company a month to fill the position for each departed 
employee, Staff ultimately agreed that its vacancy rate adjustment would be applied to an entire 
year’s worth of expense.46  Tr. 4164-4166. 
The unreasonableness of falsely assuming that a departed employee results in a vacated 
position for an entire twelve-month period may be best explained by a simple hypothetical: 
If the Company employed 1,000 employees on January 1st of a given year, lost 35 
employees during the year, and rehired 35 new employees that same year, the 
headcount at year-end would still be 1,000 and the attrition rate would calculate out 
to be 3.5% (35 full-time equivalents (“FTEs”) terminated divided by 1,000 FTEs at 
year-end).  
Tr. 777.  Applying Staff’s methodology to this simple example would result in the Company 
being allowed to recover expense for only 965 employees, which would be unjust and overbroad 
because it does not consider what the vacancy rate was at any point during the year or for how 
long the vacancy lasted.  Tr. 777-778. 
 
46  During cross-examination, Staff seemed unsure of how its proposed vacancy rate would actually be applied, at 
times suggesting that the adjustment would only eliminate employee expense for “the time period that they are 
gone.”  Tr. 4165.  While such an approach would be consistent with Staff’s pre-filed testimony that the vacancy 
rate is meant to reduce wages that the Company will not incur “when positions are vacant during the year” (Tr. 
4059), Staff ultimately agreed that the application of its vacancy rate would be reflective of a year’s worth of 
data.  Tr. 4166.  Staff’s confusion regarding the intent and application of the vacancy rate adjustment alone 
warrants its rejection. 


--- Page 51 ---
 
43 
 
Second, as explained by the Company’s Revenue Requirement Panel, if the vacancy rate 
is meant to protect customers from paying for Company labor expense not actually incurred, it 
should be premised on the Company’ actual employee headcount to the headcount levels 
provided for in rates.  Tr. 778.  When viewed in this context, no vacancy rate should apply 
because the Company’s actual headcount has historically exceeded the headcount allowed for in 
rates.  See Exhibit 247 (RRP-7R).  The Company expects this trend to continue in the Rate Year.  
Tr. 779.  Furthermore, because the Company has a history of managing its business as needed 
and hiring the employees to do so, regardless of rate funding to support those hires, Staff’s 
proposed reduction to the Company’s incremental FTE request already has the same effect as a 
vacancy rate (Tr. 779) and any additional vacancy rate would be duplicative of Staff’s FTE 
adjustments.  Staff’s proposed vacancy rate is also redundant to the 1% productivity adjustment 
imputation included within the Company’s proposed revenue requirement as both have the effect 
of reducing the Company’s labor expense.  Tr. 779-780. 
Finally, application of the vacancy rate to the forecast of incremental new hires is 
particularly unwarranted.  As explained by the Company in the Direct Testimony of the Revenue 
Requirements Panel, the Company factored in an assumption that staggered the hiring of new 
employees over time.  Tr. 694-695.  As such, the Company already included a reduction to labor 
expense for new hires that accounts for the fact that the incremental employees may not be 
present for the entire Rate Year.  Tr. 780.  Applying a further reduction is a double count of a 
timing difference that the Company already factored into its labor expense projection.  Tr. 780. 
c. Labor Distribution Rate (% of labor charged to expense or capital) 
Staff seeks to deviate from the long-established calculation methodology for determining 
the labor distribution rate.  Tr. 781.  Going back at least as far as Cases 92-E-1055 and 92-G-
1056 (over a 30-year span), the Company relied upon the historic test year labor distribution, 


--- Page 52 ---
 
44 
 
adjusted for projected changes in the bridge period and Rate Year to determine the labor 
distribution to be used in the projection of the Rate Year.  Tr. 781, 806.  This methodology was 
approved by the Commission in multiple rate cases over this time span.  Tr. 781, 783-784.    
The Staff Accounting Panel, with little or no justification, seeks to radically alter course 
and deviate from the well-established precedent by adjusting the labor distribution percentage 
calculation methodology to reflect a three-year historical average of actual labor cost 
distributions from 2020-2022.  Tr. 781.  Staff’s stated reason for pursuing a three-year average in 
these proceedings is that the Company’s method “does not account for year-to-year fluctuations 
that can occur in types of work that determine the ratio of labor to expense, capital and other 
affiliates.”  Tr. 4059-4060, lines 22-1.  Staff’s statement lacks any merit as the Company 
included adjustments to the historic year labor distribution to account for known or anticipated 
changes in the Rate Year.  Tr. 781-782.  Staff’s direct testimony provides no additional rationale.  
Tr. 782.  In response to a Company interrogatory (CH to DPS (052) included in Exhibit 243 
(RRP-5R)), Staff posits that it took exception to the Company’s proposed distribution because a 
major component of the Company’s forward-looking adjustments is directly linked to proposed 
incremental FTEs, which remain uncertain.  Tr. 782.  As the Company’s Revenue Requirement 
Panel made clear in its rebuttal testimony, taking exception to a forward-looking adjustment does 
not in and of itself justify use of a historical average.  Tr. 782-784.  Staff had the information 
available to it to recalculate the forward-looking normalization adjustment based on their 
recommended incremental FTEs but chose not to do so.  Tr. 783.  Staff instead relies on a three-
year historical average in what appears to be a deliberate results-oriented approach as the use of 
the forward-looking normalization adjustment in this instance increases the labor charged to 
expense whereas in years past it reduced the labor charged to expense.  Tr. 783-784.  “Staff did 


--- Page 53 ---
 
45 
 
not provide any detail to substantiate its claim that the activities of the past three years, on 
average, would be an accurate indication of the future.”  Tr. 782-783, lines 22-2.  Staff’s use of a 
historical average does not reflect how the Company’s existing workforce allocated their time 
between expense and capital, and it ignores the projected effect that incremental employees will 
have on the overall labor distribution.  The Company’s labor distribution rate therefore should be 
adopted.  
d. Wage Increases  
i. 
Union Employees 
The Company’s union wages are governed by collective bargaining agreements and are 
generally not in dispute for the Rate Year between the Company and Staff because a five-year 
collective bargaining agreement is in effect through April 30, 2026 and covers the vast majority 
of union employees.  Tr. 345.  The exception relates to employees in System Operations, where 
the union and the Company will begin negotiating a new contract in March 2024.  As Staff 
acknowledges, the System Operations employees’ contract will expire on March 31, 2024, so 
wage increase rates for April 1, 2024, and beyond are unknown.  Tr. 4056.  Without knowing the 
outcome of the new contract negotiations, the Company proposes a wage increase of 4.5% 
(consistent with the overall labor increases utilized for all other employees) for System 
Operations employees.  Tr. 346.  The Staff Accounting Panel disagrees and proposes only an 
increase of 2.25% in 2024 and 3.0% in 2025 - allegedly to be consistent with the increase 
received by the IBEW Local 320 union in the last contract negotiation.  Tr. 4057.  Notably, 
Staff’s proposed increase of 2.25% in 2024 would represent a reduction from the System 
Operations employees 2.5% wage increase granted on April 1, 2023, under the union contract 
effective through March 31, 2024.  Tr. 345.  It is odd to assume, as Staff seems to do, that the 
union and the System Operations employees would accept a smaller increase (2.25% versus 


--- Page 54 ---
 
46 
 
2.5%) under a new contract for the period April 1, 2024, through March 2025.  Staff asserts that 
its proposal merely reflects the wage increases in the Company’s other contracts.  Staff 
overlooks or ignores the fact that the Company’s large union population has a collective 
bargaining agreement for a multi-year term with annual increases set several years ago – in a 
vastly economic climate and labor market.  Tr. 401.  Application of a wage escalation rate that 
was determined years ago in the IBEW Local 320 union contract to a future union contract for a 
new group of employes, one that is very different (System Operations) is impractical, unrealistic 
and ultimately unreasonable.  The Company’s proposed use of a 4.5% increase for System 
Operations employees is supported by the best evidence available today and should be adopted.  
ii. 
Non-Union Management Employees  
The Company has adopted a strategy to compensate its employees with base salaries at 
the 50th percentile of overall compensation for comparable jobs in the Northeast United States.  
To do so, the Company employs the services of Mercer, a nationally recognized compensation 
consultant, to establish the range of those salaries.  Tr. 343.  The Company proposed a 4.5% 
overall compensation increase for non-executive management employees.  Tr. 346.  The 
percentage increase was based on Mercer data and reflected the mid-point of Mercer’s proposed 
merit increase recommendation in the range of 4% to 5%, which is shown on Schedule B of 
Exhibit 66 (WCBP-4).     
Staff reviewed the WorldatWork organization’s 2023 forecast set forth in Exhibit 340 
(SPP-4), noting that under ordinary circumstances, Staff would recommend a reduction of 0.5% 
from the Company’s proposed 4.5%.  Tr. 4013.  This would represent a reduction from the 
Company’s proposal down to the WorldatWork forecast.  
Yet, contrary to the Company’s Mercer study recommendation and data, and Staff’s own 
review of WorldatWork data, the Staff Policy Panel inexplicably imposes a far greater reduction, 


--- Page 55 ---
 
47 
 
reducing the management wage increase to 2.25% in 2024 and 3.0% in 2025.  Tr. 4014.  As 
noted by the Company’s Workforce, Compensation and Benefits Panel, utilizing Staff’s below-
market wage increase figures of 2.25% in 2024 and 3.0% in 2025 would perpetuate the problem 
of the Company not being market competitive.  As a result, the Company would continue to face 
challenges attracting and retaining talent which would put the Company’s ability to perform its 
core functions at risk.  Tr. 400. 
Staff’s primary justification for this significant deviation from the available expert 
compensation data is highly subjective.  Staff simply states an opinion that “given the 
disturbances from the billing issues and rate pressures as previously discussed, we cannot justify 
requiring ratepayers to pay for the requested management wage increases during the rate year.”  
Tr. 4013-4014, lines 24-4.  Tellingly, Staff does not allege that either non-union management 
employees (or executive employees) mishandled the SAP-CIS transition.  There is simply no link 
between the billing issues and non-union management (or executive) wages as Staff seems to 
imply. 
Staff ignores Mercer’s market-based expert recommendation.  Instead, Staff arbitrarily 
reduces the management compensation increases to match those provided to Central Hudson’s 
employees represented by the IBEW Local 320 union, increases reached through collective 
bargaining.  Tr. 4014.  Staff points to no evidence demonstrating that labor market conditions are 
identical for employees governed by the Company’s collective bargaining agreements and 
management employes.  Indeed, it would be inappropriate to base non-union management 
compensation on a comprehensive collective bargaining agreement that was reached by 
settlement, a settlement that involved more than just wage increases but also included give and 
take on several other items that do not apply to non-union management employees.  Staff’s 


--- Page 56 ---
 
48 
 
proposed significant reductions to the Company’s management wage increases lack empirical 
support, are contrary to the Company’s market-based Mercer study and are not supported by 
Staff’s own WorldatWork review.  In sharp contrast, the Company has demonstrated that the 
base rate increases for management employees it proposes are supported by actual market data 
and should be adopted. 
iii. 
Executive Employees 
Similar to non-executive management employee compensation, the Company utilized the 
same market median compensation approach noted above and assumed an overall compensation 
increase of 4.5% from the March 31, 2023 level to forecast the Rate Year base payroll.  Tr. 346.  
The only difference is that the Company utilized Frederic W. Cook & Co., Inc. (“Cook”), a 
nationally recognized executive compensation consultant to establish the median level of salaries 
for the Company’s executives.  Tr. 344.  Seemingly not content with its earlier treatment of 
management employees, the Staff Policy Panel doubles down on subjectivity and deviates even 
further from the market-based compensation data in the record to recommend a zero percent 
wage increase for executives.  Tr. 4014.  For the same reasons noted above relating to Staff’s 
reduction to management base wage increases, Staff’s proposal for a zero percent wage increase 
for executives should be rejected. 
iv. 
Temporary Employees 
Staff and the Company also disagree on temporary employee wage increases.  The 
Company recommended a 4.5 % increase for temporary employees.  The Staff Accounting Panel 
drops the increase down to a mere 1.4% based on a historical average that Staff apparently 
utilized because temporary employees “have not regularly received wage increases over the past 
six years.”  Tr. 4057, lines 16-18.  Staff’s proposal would punish the Company for doing a good 
job in keeping wages low – when market conditions allowed it.  Such is no longer the case, as 


--- Page 57 ---
 
49 
 
reflected by the 8.1% increase in wages in 2023 after six years with no increase.  Tr. 4057.  
Staff’s use of an average completely masks the 8.1% increase.  What Staff also seemingly fails to 
recognize is that temporary employees include the Company’s intern pool.  The intern pool is 
where the Company recruits new employees, and the Company must therefore be market 
competitive to continue to attract top talent.  Tr. 402-403.  Staff’s proposed 1.4% increase should 
be rejected and the more reasonable 4.5% proposed by the Company adopted.     
e. Labor Reconciliation Mechanism 
The Company disagrees with Staff’s proposed establishment of a downward-only labor 
reconciliation mechanism.  Tr. 4154.  The record demonstrates that the Company has a proven 
history of hiring the employees needed to safely and effectively run the business.  Tr. 784.  As 
noted previously, the Company has already hired a considerable number of FTEs that it is 
seeking rate recovery of in these proceedings.  Adding a downward only labor reconciliation 
mechanism adds unnecessary complexity to managing the business in a one-year litigated case.  
Tr. 784.    
In addition, Staff’s mechanism is fundamentally flawed because it could result in the 
Company having to return to customers expenses that were actually and reasonably incurred.  Tr. 
785.  Staff’s proposed mechanism gives no consideration to the timing of when employees may 
or may not have been employed at the Company.  Tr. 785.  As the Company’s Revenue 
Requirement Panel noted, Staff’s calculation “assumes that an employee not on property at the 
end of the Rate Year was not on the property for the entire Rate Year.”  Tr. 785, lines 6-8.  If an 
employee was on property for 11 months but left in the 12th month, the Company could be 
forced under Staff’s proposal to return to customers a full year of Labor expense for the departed 
employee, even though the Company bore that expense for the 11 months that the employee was 


--- Page 58 ---
 
50 
 
on the premises.  Staff’s proposed downward-only labor reconciliation mechanism is 
unnecessary, fatally flawed and should not be adopted.  
f. Variable Compensation 
i. 
Non-Executive 
The Company proposed for the first time in these rate cases an incentive program for 
non-executive, non-union employees (Management).  The Company appreciates Staff’s 
agreement and support of the Company’s proposed Management Variable Compensation 
Program. Tr. 401, 4062-4068.  As a result, this program is not at issue between Staff and the 
Company.  Tr. 4068.  
ii. 
Executive 
Similar to the incentive variable compensation plan for management employees, the 
Company seeks to recover costs for an executive incentive pay program.  Like the incentive 
compensation plan for management, the Staff Accounting Panel found that the Company’s 
proposed incentive program satisfied the Commission’s requirements for variable pay programs.  
Tr. 4068-4073.  Surprisingly, despite this finding, Staff recommends the Commission deny 
recovery for the Company’s executive incentive compensation plan noting that “due to the 
overall circumstances in this proceeding, the Staff Policy Panel has made a recommendation to 
exclude the executive compensation program.”  Tr. 4073, lines 19-23.  
It is unusual for the Staff Policy Panel (normally a panel that provides an overview of 
other testimony)47 to ignore the analysis of another Staff Panel.  Notably, the Staff Policy Panel 
does not refute the finding by the Staff Accounting Panel, which used the Commission’s 
approved methodology for evaluating executive variable compensation, that the Company’s 
 
47  As Staff counsel noted during the hearing, “[t]he purpose of [the Staff Policy Panel] testimony is not to provide 
information in the record.”  Tr. 4021. 


--- Page 59 ---
 
51 
 
executive incentive compensation fully met the Commission’s test for allowing incentive 
compensation to be recovered in rates.  
Knowing that executive incentive compensation cannot be denied under the 
Commission’s existing approval standard, the Staff Policy Panel inappropriately looks to expand 
the Commission’s well-established standard for when incentive compensation is available to 
include new and highly subjective factors.  The Staff Policy Panel seeks to rely on “disturbances 
from the billing issues and immense rate pressures.”  Tr. 4004, lines 15-16.  The Staff Policy

Operations Audit of Central Hudson conducted in Case 21-M-0541 (“2021 Management Audit”), 
where the auditor identified weaknesses in various aspects of the Company’s management.  
What the Staff Policy Panel neglects to add is that the management audit process is a cooperative 
one where the auditor, Company and Staff look to identify areas of improvement.  Tr. 904.  It is 
not surprising, therefore, that areas of improvement are identified.  This is neither unusual nor 
unexpected.  The Company’s acceptance of the various audit recommendations and the 
development of an implementation plan is the expected outcome of every management audit.  It 
is not a sign of future poor management.  Moreover, as the Staff Policy Panel concedes, the 
Company’s audit implementation plan has not yet been finalized.  Tr. 4008-4009.   
The Staff Policy Panel takes a leap of logic and then concludes that “Overland’s audit
