ratepayers.”  Tr. 4012, lines 6-8.  Tellingly, the incentive compensation program being 
referenced by Overland was not the Executive Incentive Compensation Program being sought in 
this proceeding.  The Staff Policy Panel acknowledges that the Company’s Executive Incentive 
Compensation Program for which the Company is seeking cost recovery in this proceeding is 


--- Page 60 ---
 
52 
 
comprised of goals related to safety, reliability, customer service and the environment, which are 
consistent with Commission policies.  Tr. 4012.   Staff’s references to Overland’s management 
audit findings are misplaced and do not support Staff’s objection to executive incentive 
compensation.   
Staff’s remaining rationale for denying executive incentive compensation is billing 
system issues and rate pressures.  Once again, the implicit conclusion is that the Staff Policy 
Panel is improperly seeking to penalize the Company (and its executives) on a going forward 
basis for past billing difficulties associated with the transition to a new SAP-CIS.  This is 
inappropriate as the record is clear that the Company has acknowledged that it had billing 
difficulties during a complex transition to a new SAP-CIS system, has taken responsibility for its 
past actions and taken corrective action such that the SAP-CIS billing system errors are at or 
close to billing issue rates pre-go live of the new system.  Tr. 3324.  The Company’s Executive 
Incentive Compensation Program meets the Commission’s settled standard for incentive 
compensation and should be granted in these proceedings.    
g. Employee Benefits 
i. 
Medical Benefit Inflation Rate 
The Company provides Employee Benefits that are comprised of Medical Insurance 
(including Dental and Vision), Group Life Insurance, Savings Incentive Plan (“SIP”), Employee 
Stock Purchase plan (“ESPP”) and other Employee Benefits.  Tr. 696.  The Company and Staff 
disagree over medical benefit costs.   
Given the rapid rise in healthcare costs, the Company relied on incremental studies 
performed by an expert consultant to inform the Company’s position on medical benefit costs in 
these rate cases.  Tr. 696.  More specifically, the Company utilized an actuarial study by Mercer 
to forecast future medical claims costs.  Tr. 381; Exhibit 79 (WCBP-12).   


--- Page 61 ---
 
53 
 
Excluding 2020 (pandemic year), the three-year average historic cost for medical claims 
is $13.2 million and the three-year average expense growth rate is 12%.  The Company 
anticipates that the trend of increasing medical costs will continue due to the nature of the high-
cost claims and subscribers’ health conditions, growth in the Company’s staffing level and 
number of subscribers in the Company’s medical benefit plan, and higher than historic 
inflationary factors.  Tr. 381.   
Staff Witness Gadomski opposes the Company’s proposal to escalate healthcare costs 
using a separate inflation index from that which is used to escalate costs items in the general 
inflation pool.  Tr. 3538.  Staff Witness Gadomski notes the Commission’s long-standing 
requirement (Case 92-M-0184) to use Gross Domestic Product - Price Index (“GDP-PI”) for 
certain utility expense projections.  Mr. Gadomski recommends that the inflation rate be 
calculated using the most recently published consensus short-term and long-range forecasts of 
the GDP-PI, which are included in the October 10, 2023, issue of the Blue Chip Economic 
Indicators.  Tr. 3537-3538.  Should Mr. Gadomski’s approach be adopted, Staff’s rate allowance 
as proposed would require modification.  Staff’s calculation reflects an erroneous mathematical 
computation that results in Staff’s cost per employee actually decreasing over the bridge period 
and in the Rate Year, as opposed to growing at the rate of inflation as proposed by Mr. 
Gadomski.  Staff applied this declining rate to the group of incremental employees and thus 
failed to accurately reflect Mr. Gadomski’s recommendation in the development of revenue 
requirements.  As set forth in Appendix 1, Schedule A, this erroneous calculation results in Staff 
understating the rate allowance for electric by $628,000 and for gas by $142,000. 
Given the consistently large increases in medical costs, the Company believes that a 
separate inflation index is appropriate and should be used.  


--- Page 62 ---
 
54 
 
ii. 
Tracking Adjustments 
There are multiple elements of expense that have tracking adjustments that are tied to the 
components of labor, including headcount, labor expense and/or the labor distribution.  The 
Company and Staff appear to agree on the theory that these are appropriate tracking adjustments.  
The Company, however, notes that it opposes the tracking adjustments made by Staff on 
the grounds that it disagrees with Staff’s positions on the underlying components of labor.  As 
such, once final headcount, labor expense and labor distribution are determined in these 
proceedings, the tracking adjustments that Staff noted should be recalculated.  Tr. 785.  
h. Pensions and OPEBs 
The Company follows the Commission’s Statement of Policy and Order Concerning the 
Accounting and Ratemaking Treatment for Pensions and Postretirement Benefits Other Than 
Pensions, issued on September 7, 1993 in Case 91-M-0890, and Generally Accepted Accounting 
Principles (“GAAP”).  Tr. 1505-1510.  
The development of revenue requirements for Pension for the Rate Year includes a credit 
of $7.3 million and $2.1 million for electric and gas, respectively.  These credit amounts 
represent the net amount after a 1% allocation to affiliates for the Supplemental Executive 
Retirement Plan.  Tr. 699-700.   
The development of revenue requirements for Other Post-Employment Benefits 
(“OPEBs”) for the Rate Year includes a credit of $5.8 million and $1.6 million for electric and 
gas, respectively.  These projections are based on an estimate developed by the Company’s 
actuary.  Tr. 700.  
Staff and the Company agree on these figures and with reflecting the January 2024 
Mercer update for Pensions and OPEBs.  The update, subject to tracking adjustments, will be 
reflected in the Company’s Brief on Exceptions.  Tr. 4084. 


--- Page 63 ---
 
55 
 
i. Employee Training, Safety and Education 
The Rate Year projection was developed by initially reviewing the historic year expense 
to identify appropriate normalization adjustments.  Normalized historic year expenses were then 
escalated at inflation to project the Rate Year.  Tr. 700-701.  Additionally, an estimated cost per 
employee based on normalized historic year expense was calculated, escalated at inflation to 
arrive at a Rate Year cost per employee, and then applied to the projected number of incremental 
employees in the Rate Year.  Lastly, incremental expense for new training programs and 
enhancements to existing programs, as further discussed by the Workforce, Compensation and 
Benefits Panel, was included in the development of revenue requirements.   
Staff opposes the Company’s proposed Employee Training, Safety and Education 
budgets based on the size of the increases, further alleging that the Company did not explain the 
size of the increases.  Staff also takes issue with the Company’s request because the Company 
did not reach its employee training spending targets in the 2021 Rate Plan.  Tr. 4085-4087. 
Initially, Staff recommends modifying the forecast for the Rate Year by using Staff’s 
proposed update to the general inflation indices applied to the normalized historic test year costs.  
Tr. 4088.  This represents a decrease of $4,766 for electric and $2,063 for gas.  
In a more significant change, Staff only allows 50% of the new business initiative 
training to be included in the Rate Year forecasts.  This adjustment reduces Central Hudson’s 
Rate Year forecast by $211,840 for electric and $109,960 for gas.  Tr. 4090. 
Staff’s reduction of the business initiative training funds should be rejected.  The 
Company continuously balances the training needs of the organization with cost and resources 
needed to conduct the training programs and has determined that it is in the best interest of the 
employee learning experience to provide in-person training activities when feasible.  Tr. 408.  
Staff’s proposed 50% reduction in funding for the new business initiative training programs 


--- Page 64 ---
 
56 
 
(which have been deemed essential) significantly affects employee development, knowledge, and 
productivity.  Tr. 408.  Indeed, the Company has already initiated seven of these programs, with 
a projected Rate Year cost of $402,100.  Tr. 408.  Staff’s proposed allowance of $321,800 in the 
Rate Year does not even provide for recovery of the seven programs that have already been 
initiated and leaves no incremental dollars for the Company to execute the remaining initiatives.   
Staff’s further justification for the proposed 50% reduction indicates that the programs 
can be conducted in-house or by having the trainer come to Central Hudson.  However, due to 
the highly specialized nature of many of the new business initiatives, the Company requires 
external subject matter experts for effective facilitation.  Tr. 407.  Moreover, Staff’s assumption 
that the Company is sending employees offsite to attend the training programs is incorrect.  Tr. 
409.  Staff’s position is also inconsistent with its disallowance of two incremental resources 
required to conduct additional trainings in-house.  Tr. 409-410.  For these reasons, Staff’s 
adjustment should be denied. 
5. 
Productivity 
The Staff Accounting Panel noted that “Central Hudson included productivity 
adjustments that reduce the projected Rate Year electric and gas expenses by $1.168 million and 
$343,000, respectively….[reflecting] the Commission’s traditional one percent productivity 
factor applied to labor, employee benefits (specifically fringe benefits), pension, OPEBs, and 
payroll taxes….”  Tr. 4126-4127, lines 22-6.  Staff, however, then makes three adjustments to 
the Company’s effort to follow Commission precedent.  First, Staff would impose an additional 
1% productivity adjustment.  Tr. 4127-4128.  Second, Staff recommended that Pension and 
OPEB costs be reflected in the productivity calculation as zero, rather than as a negative 
expense.  Tr. 4129-4131.  Third, Staff updated the other components in the productivity 
calculation to reflect Staff’s Rate Year projections for those items.  Tr. 4131-4132.  Those 


--- Page 65 ---
 
57 
 
include updates to labor, employee benefits and payroll taxes.  The net effect is a Rate Year 
productivity adjustment forecast of ($2.176) million for electric and ($641,000) for gas, which 
represents reductions to revenue requirements of $1.039 million for electric and $307,000 for 
gas.  See Appendix 1, Schedule A.  The Company agrees that the productivity adjustment should 
be updated to be applied to the values ultimately determined in the Recommended Decision.  But 
the first two Staff proposals are insupportable.   
Staff contends that the productivity adjustment needs to be doubled because: 
The Rate Year revenue requirements reflect greatly increased costs for additional 
employees and related benefits of approximately $12.063 million between 
existing rates and Staff’s Rate Year projections.  The additional employees should 
allow the Company to increase its efficiency as it achieves better outcomes, 
however the Company has not quantified any efficiencies.  Additionally, the 
Company has reflected costs related to implementing its management audit 
recommendations but has not reflected savings that will materialize after the 
recommendations are implemented. 
 
Tr. 4128. 
Staff’s first rationale for doubling productivity is both misguided and duplicative.  First, 
as explained in the direct testimony of multiple Company witness panels, and as put forth in 
Exhibit 64 (WCBP-2), the vast majority of incremental employees requested in these 
proceedings are needed to perform incremental work.  In other words, the Company will perform 
more work with the additional employees than it would with its current workforce and, therefore, 
the addition of the incremental employees does not make the Company’s current employees 
more productive.  Tr. 799.  Because the application of the additional 1% to those existing 
employees assumes just that, it cannot be supported and Staff presents no testimony or study to 
show why this should be the case.  Given that the base upon which the 1% productivity 
imputation is calculated would include the incremental employees – whatever that number 
ultimately is determined to be – means that 1% productivity is also imputed to those new 


--- Page 66 ---
 
58 
 
employees.  Simple math, therefore, would counsel that productivity has already been imputed 
for these new employees, in addition to all of the existing employees.  Tr. 799.  So this 
adjustment would double count productivity. 
Staff’s second reason for imputing double the traditional productivity adjustment (from 
1% to 2%) is equally unavailing.  This argument for doubling the productivity imputation is 
based on Staff’s claim that, as discussed in the testimony of Staff’s Management Audit Panel, the 
Company’s proposed revenue requirements did not explicitly include any cost savings or benefits 
that will result from the implementation of the recommendations from the 2021 Management 
Audit.  Tr. 4129-4130.  It is particularly noteworthy, however, that although the Staff 
Management Audit Panel proposed a Rate Year management audit savings imputation, nowhere 
did that panel recommend a doubling of the productivity adjustment.  The Staff Accounting 
Panel also conceded during cross-examination that it did not undertake an analysis of the 2021 
Management Audit recommendations to determine which specific recommendations would lead 
to labor efficiencies.  Tr. 4180.    
Moreover, although the issue of cost savings will be discussed more thoroughly in the 
Management Audit section of this brief, the fact remains that the Company did assess the costs 
and savings for each of Overland’s recommendations related to the 2021 Management Audit.  Tr. 
833-843; Exhibit 229 (MAP-3R).  As the Commission noted in rejecting a similar such 
conjectural claim for an additional 1% productivity, “the question is not so much over whether [a 
given investment] is likely to create savings, but over what the appropriate amount of those 
savings will be and when they will occur.”48  Exhibit 229 (MAP-3R) contains a summary of the 
 
48  Case 16-G-0257 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of 
National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation for Gas Service, Order Establishing Rates for Gas Service at 35 
(Apr. 20, 2017) (“National Fuel Rate Order”).   


--- Page 67 ---
 
59 
 
Company’s most current assessment of each recommendation, including identification of costs 
and savings that are yet to be determined.  To the extent that any recommendation of the 2021 
Management Audit is likely to result in Rate Year savings, the costs and benefits will be 
explored in the Management Audit section of this brief.  Staff, however, has failed to show that 
any recommendation not specifically accounted for by its Management Audit Panel will be 
implemented and produce benefits in the Rate Year.  As such, Staff’s argument for a 2% 
productivity adjustment is a patent overreach and argues for savings that are based on conjecture.    
Staff also argues that Pension and OPEB costs should be reflected in the productivity 
calculation as zero, rather than as a negative expense.  Tr. 4130-4131.  Staff, however, 
recognizes that, although “the standard productivity adjustment is not intended to capture savings 
associated with these particular items or any particular program initiated by the Company… the 
Commission’s long-standing practice has been to use this calculation as a proxy.”  Tr. 4129, lines 
18-23.  Here Staff is playing a “heads I win, tails you lose” game. 
Despite Staff’s claim that savings in Pension and OPEB are not part of the “intention of 
the productivity imputation,” the fact remains that Staff, nevertheless, concedes that that negative 
values were included in the calculation in the past (Tr. 4129-4130), as ostensibly, positive values 
would also have been included, as well.  Moreover, regardless of Staff ‘s excuse that it was 
“unsure if the negative expense would be temporary or short-term diversions” (Tr. 4131, lines 3-
4), Staff and the Company are in agreement that negative expense has been included in the 
determination of the productivity calculation since Cases 14-E-0318 and 14-G-0319.  Tr. 800.  
This means that for the term of three multi-year rate plans, covering nine rate years, the 
productivity calculation methodology has reflected the Pension and OPEB negative expense.  
The Company does not consider this length of time “temporary” or “short-term” and notes that 


--- Page 68 ---
 
60 
 
Staff’s proposal appears to be little more than an artifice to increase the base upon which the 
productivity imputation is calculated, thereby increasing the productivity imputation.  Id.  
Although Staff now argues that reflecting negative expenses in the proxy calculation is 
“counterintuitive to the intention of a productivity adjustment,” (Tr. 4129-4130, lines 23-2), 
query why reflecting positive expenses is any less counterintuitive given Staff’s confession that 
the standard productivity adjustment is not intended to capture savings with Pension and OPEB 
costs.  Tr. 4129.  So these expenses should either be always reflected as zero or both positive and 
negative numbers be reflected as they fall.  Staff’s approach makes no sense.   
6. 
Uncollectible Accounts Expense 
The Company proposed to use the same Uncollectible Accounts Expense established in 
its 2021 Rate Plan of $3.730 million for electric and $1.323 million for gas but with full deferral 
accounting for net write-offs and collection agency fees during the Rate Year, with no threshold 
limitation.  Tr. 716-717.  The Company’s Customer Experience Panel explained that “the 
suspension and measured restart of collection activities has significantly distorted the data that 
would be necessary to develop an accurate forecast of uncollectible expense [and a]t this point, 
the Company cannot reasonably predict the level of net write-offs that will occur in the Rate 
Year.”  Tr. 3018.    
Staff instead offers an “updated projection” for uncollectible expense based on a pre-
COVID three-year average of net write-offs as a percentage of delivery revenues subject to bad 
debt covering the calendar years ended December 31, 2018 – December 31, 2020.  Staff then 
develops a ratio for this three-year average of 1.14% and applies this ratio to projected pre-rate 
relief delivery revenues to arrive at a Rate Year uncollectible accounts expense of $5.054 million 
for electric and $1.542 million for gas, an increase of $1.324 million for electric and $219,000 
for gas.  Tr. 4095.  Staff failed to update these projections to align with the revenue changes put 


--- Page 69 ---
 
61 
 
forth in their correction filing on January 16, 2024.  Staff also failed to factor in the impact of 
uncollectible expense associated with the proposed rate increases, which is customary in 
ratemaking when using the methodology proposed by Staff.  Staff further argues that a 
reconciliation is unnecessary because it claims the resumption of collection should help lower 
uncollectible expense and holds out the illusory claim that the Company can always file a 
petition seeking deferral.  Tr. 4096.  Staff is being unrealistic. 
 
First, Staff uses stale data – going back to 2018 to estimate uncollectible accounts 
expense – that is not reflective of the uncollectible expense the Company is likely to experience 
in the Rate Year.  This is especially true given COVID-19, and the state’s termination moratoria, 
both of which resulted in a dramatic uptick in this expense.   
Second, Staff ignores that the Company’s use of a historical uncollectible expense 
amount as a Rate Year proxy was specifically tied to its request for a reconciliation mechanism 
for uncollectible expense.  Uncertainty surrounding the timing and amount of uncollectible 
write-offs remains and is driven by a variety of factors including: the Company’s 
commencement of its full collection activities, the number of customers enrolling in deferred 
payment agreements, and future economic conditions.  Tr. 1546-1547.  Based on this 
uncertainty, a deferral is appropriate so that any over- or under-collected amounts compared to 
what is provided for in rates can be set aside for return to or collection from customers at a future 
date.  Tr. 1547. 
Finally, Staff’s argument that the Company can always request a deferral if Staff’s 
projection goes awry is cold comfort given the hurdles to bringing an independent deferral 
petition.  To bring such a separate petition, the Company is required to show, inter alia, that the 


--- Page 70 ---
 
62 
 
amount in excess of the amount allowed in rates would be in excess of 5% of net income.49  For 
Central Hudson, this would mean an expense would need to exceed the level in rates by an 
amount equal to nearly 50 BPs.   
7. 
Gas Materials and Supplies and Stores Expense 
Central Hudson forecasted a Rate Year Materials and Supplies expense of $2.999 million 
for electric and $558,138 for gas.  Tr. 4107.  Staff accepts the Company’s Rate Year expense for 
electric but objects to the expense for gas.  Tr. 4107-4108.  Staff rejected the Company’s 
proposal to normalize this expense over three years and then inflate it.  Instead, Staff applied 
inflation factors to the historic test year cost through the bridge period and Rate Year, which 
results in a Rate Year forecast of $382,608.  This is a reduction to the Company’s forecast of 
$175,530.  Tr. 4110. 
Staff’s adjustment should be rejected.  The expense incurred in the historic test year does 
not capture the cyclical costs that the Company anticipates incurring in the Rate Year, and as 
such Staff’s adjustment will not allow the Company to recover a cost of doing business that can 
be reasonably assumed to occur.  Tr. 793-794.  A three-year average, adjusted for inflation 
would better capture this cyclicality and should be used, instead.    
For the same reasons it adjusted Gas Materials and Supplies expense to reverse the 
Company’s proposed normalization adjustment, Staff removed the normalization adjustment of 
$59,108 to Stores – Gas.  Tr. 4111.  This reduces the Company’s forecast by $62,784, resulting 
in a revised Rate Year forecast of $49,184.  Tr. 4111.  This adjustment should similarly be 
rejected on the same grounds.

As discussed throughout this section, the Company disagrees with including several items

9. 
Regulatory Commission Assessments 
This expense item relates to the general assessment charged by the New York State 
Department of Public Service to each utility in New York State to fund the Department’s 
operations.  Tr. 4096.  As explained by the Staff Accounting Panel, there are three letters 
associated with the Regulatory Commission Assessment sent by the Department to utilities.  
First, an initial statement is mailed in February, prior to the start of the state fiscal year that the 
assessment applies to; second, a revised statement is mailed in August; and third, a final 
statement of assessment is mailed in October of the following year, after the close of the state 
fiscal year.  Tr. 4096-4097. 
To project the Rate Year expense for this item, the Company applied a three-year historic 
average growth rate of 10% to the initial assessment of $3.427 million received from the 
Department in a letter dated February 10, 2023, for the fiscal year end March 31, 2024.  Tr. 717.  
This resulted in a Rate Year projection of $3.017 million for electric and $847,520 for gas.  Tr. 
4097.  The Staff Accounting Panel disagreed with this methodology and recommended setting 
the rate allowance by inflating the latest known final statement of assessment received from the 
Department, dated October 20, 2023, which results in a reduction to the Company’s Rate Year 
forecast of $324,156 for electric and $91,179 for gas.  Tr. 4097-4098.   


--- Page 72 ---
 
64 
 
In rebuttal testimony, the Company agreed with using the October 2023 final assessment 
as the basis for the Rate Year expense but disagreed with Staff’s use of inflation as the applied 
growth rate.  Tr. 786.  Based on experience, increasing the Regulatory Commission Assessment 
by the inflation rate alone will not produce an accurate forecast.  As explained by the Company’s 
Revenue Requirement Panel in direct testimony, over the last three years, the general assessment 
has increased on average by 10%.  Tr. 717.  That three-year average included estimated 
assessments, and as such Staff took exception to the Company’s growth rate calculation.  
Utilizing the growth in the final assessments for the last three years of available data results in a 
three-year average growth rate of approximately 11%.  To the extent Staff has concerns with 
using the Company’s proposed 10% average growth rate because it included estimated 
assessments, Your Honors and the Commission should utilize the 11% average growth rate based 
on the last three years for which final assessments are available or deem the 10% used by the 
Company as reasonable given that even the updated analysis of the growth rate approximates that 
originally put forth by the Company. 
10. 
Information Technology O&M Adjustment 
Staff inexplicably recommends a tracking adjustment for Information Technology (“IT”) 
O&M expense based on the Staff Common Capital Panel’s $8.58 million reduction to Rate Year 
IT capital programs.  Tr. 4098-4099.  As explained by the Staff Accounting Panel, consistent 
with the Staff Common Capital Panel’s recommendation to remove 18.8% of IT capital 
spending, it reflected a reduction to the Company’s Rate Year IT O&M expense equivalent to 
18.8% of the projected growth in IT O&M as compared to previously approved rates, which 
resulted in reductions of $488,236 to electric and $118,816 to gas.  Tr. 4099.  Staff’s baseless 
adjustment should be rejected. 


--- Page 73 ---
 
65 
 
First, there is nothing in the record to support Staff’s unfounded assumption that there is a 
one-to-one correlation between IT capital expenditures and IT O&M expense.  The Company’s 
workpapers that supported the IT O&M included in the development of revenue requirements, 
included a worksheet that linked incremental IT O&M to specific IT capital projects.  Tr. 788-
789.  Those workpapers demonstrate that there is no direct one-to-one correlation between IT 
capital and O&M expense and thus applying an 18.8% reduction in a blanket manner (as Staff 
suggests) ignores the detailed information that supports known changes included in the Rate 
Year projection. 
Second, Staff’s analysis and methodology for determining its IT adjustment contradicts 
the provisions of the Commission’s Statement of Policy on Test Periods in Major Rate 
Proceedings issued November 23, 1977 (“1977 Policy Statement”), which defines a historic test 
year and notes that the “forecasted material should be developed from the historical base.”  Tr. 
789.  The Company put forth a projection based on a test year normalized for known and 
projected changes as per the 1977 Policy Statement, but Staff’s IT O&M adjustment, which is 
tied to Staff’s IT capital adjustment, used rates established as part of the settlement outcome in 
Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429 as the base year.  Tr. 789.  Therefore, Staff’s adjustment is 
fatally flawed and should be rejected. 
11. 
Major Storm Reserve 
As part of the Electric Stipulation (Exhibit 516), the Company and Staff agreed that the 
Company would continue to utilize a Major Storm Reserve as set forth in Schedule B to the 
stipulation.  Exhibit 516 ¶4, Schedule B.  Schedule B to the Electric Stipulation establishes how 
the Major Storm Reserve will operate, including applicable thresholds for expense related to 
major storms and expense related to pre-staging and mobilization costs incurred in reasonable 


--- Page 74 ---
 
66 
 
anticipation that a storm will affect the Company’s electric operations to the degree of meeting 
the criteria of a major storm, but which ultimately does not do so.  The only matter in dispute 
between the Company and Staff related to the Major Storm Reserve is the rate allowance to be 
recovered in revenue requirement. 
A Major Storm Reserve was established for incremental expenses incurred for weather 
events that qualify as chargeable to the reserve, and an allowance of $14.8 million was included 
in the development of the Company’s electric revenue requirement.  Tr. 722.  The allowance was 
developed by employing a 10-year average projection methodology, normalized for an 
accounting entry that was recorded outside of the historic year as a result of timing and for 
changes to the pre-staging thresholds as put forth by the Electric Capital and Operations Panel.   
Id.  As the Panel noted, it is essential that this reserve allowance be enhanced, as the Company 
has been significantly under reserved throughout the current 2021 Rate Plan and has accrued a 
significant regulatory asset.  Tr. 722-723.     
Although Staff agreed with the Company’s methodology to use a ten-year average, Staff 
disagreed on including what Staff termed “superstorms” in the ten-year average.  Tr. 4102.  
Claiming that they were costlier than the average storm, Staff removed Storm Event 8493A 
Snow and Wind Nor’Easter and Storm Event 7542A Winter Ice Storm “Landon” from its 
calculation of a ten-year historical average.  Tr. 4102.  These storm costs were approximately 
$14.734 million and $24.240 million, respectively.  Id.  Staff offers no reason for its adjustment 
other than that the costs for these storms were significant.  Thus, Staff summarily disregards 
$4.064 million of revenue requirement.  Tr. 4103. 
First, given that we are discussing a reserve for “major storms” the removal of two of the 
most major storms by Staff should raise eyebrows, at the very least.  Staff’s removal of 


--- Page 75 ---
 
67 
 
“superstorms,” which Staff agrees are extreme weather events (Tr. 4173-4174), is curious given 
that the opening stanzas of the CLCPA proclaim that “[t]he adverse impacts of climate change 
include: an increase in the severity and frequency of extreme weather events, such as storms, 
flooding, and heat waves, which can cause direct injury or death, property damage, and 
ecological damage…”50  If severe storms are the norm, Staff’s removal of them should not be 
countenanced.  
Staff also apparently forgets that its own Operating Expense & Payroll Tax Panel in 
Cases 17-E-0459 and 17-G-0460 stated: 
Major storms are volatile and unpredictable, but over time, the Company should 
not be left with a storm reserve that is significantly over or under funded.  The 
Commission generally adopts an averaging approach of historic actual costs to 
determine a reasonable rate allowance. 
Tr. 790.  In fact, such storms are not outliers.  A review of storm data back to 2010, almost 
thirteen years of data, shows that the Company has had multiple storms that resulted in 
restoration costs in excess of $10 million and two storms that exceeded costs of $20 million.  Id. 
Staff’s practice of removing storms they deem as “outliers” or “superstorms” from the 
historical average directly contradicts Staff’s stated goal for the reserve and will continue to 
create challenges.  In fact, underfunding the reserve in the past is precisely why the Company has 
accrued a regulatory asset in excess of $60 million for major storm restoration.  As the 
Commission noted in a similar context: 
[T]he 2020 Rate Plan modified the joint proposal filed in that case by, among 
other things, significantly reducing the annual base rate cost recovery for EE 
programs and vegetation management programs, and by extending the 
amortization of certain regulatory assets.  As a result of those rate moderation 
 
50  CLCPA, Section 1(1)(a) (emphasis added).

68 
 
efforts, many necessary costs to the Companies were deferred for future 
collection.  Those costs cannot continue to be deferred indefinitely…51 
Staff’s effort to lower the revenue requirement though the artifice of removing “superstorms” is

after October 2023 through the end of the current rate plan and continues to estimate the amount 
to be collected through the RAM.  Tr. 724.  Given the uncertainty in these assumptions, the 
Company proposes to update this calculation later in these proceedings to reflect an update of 
storm activity and actual RAM collections, if known.  Id.  The Company further proposes that

these storm costs continue to be RAM eligible.  Any collection through the RAM serves to

12. 
Minor Storm 
The Company’s projection for Non-Major Storm Restoration Expense was derived using 
a four-year average (twelve months ended March 31, 2020; 2021; 2022; and 2023) of non-major 
storm expenditures adjusted to March 2023 dollars, which were then inflated by the GDP factors.  
Tr. 724-725.  Use of a four-year average is the methodology used in the Company’s last three 
rate plans.  Tr. 725.  The historic expense level also includes a normalization to reflect a change 
in the prestaging sharing provisions proposed by the Electric Capital and Operations Panel and to 
reflect an accounting entry that was recorded outside of the historic year as a result of timing.  
Tr. 725.  The Panel explained why the cap on prestaging costs should be increased to $3.6 
million from $1.75 million.  Tr. 2077-2078.   
The Staff Accounting Panel disagreed with increasing the prestaging costs thresholds 
from $1.75 million to $3.6 million.  Tr. 4104-4105.  In doing so, Staff attempted to “reverse” the 
corresponding normalization adjustment made by the Company in the determination of the non-


--- Page 78 ---
 
70 
 
major storm rate allowance related to the one storm event that would have exceeded the $3.6 
million threshold proposed by the Company. Staff’s adjustment resulted in a reduction of 
$57,000 to the Rate Year forecast.  Tr. 4105-4106.  Staff’s reduction was in error because it was 
based on a misunderstanding of the Company’s adjustment, which was a necessary corollary to 
the Company’s proposal to increase the threshold. 
Appendix U to the Joint Proposal in Case 20-E-0428 provides that if pre-staging costs for 
an anticipated storm event exceed $1.75 million, the Company is authorized to defer 85% for 
future collection and to charge 15% to non-major storm expense.  During the four-year period 
that served as the basis for the average used in calculating the non-major storm rate allowance in 
this case, the Company exceeded the $1.75 million threshold on one occasion, which resulted in 
$490,359 appropriately being charged to non-major storm expense.  But because under the 
Company’s proposal to increase the threshold, less expense would have been charged to non-
major storm expense, it was necessary to remove 15% of the difference between the $1.75 
million and $3.6 million, producing a reduction of $277,500, from the non-major storm 
restoration calculation.  Tr. 792.   
That adjustment of $277,500, however, was only necessary to align it with the 
Company’s proposal to increase the pre-staging cap.  Tr. 792.  Because the Company included a 
reduction of $277,500 as explained above, a rejection of the proposal to change the threshold 
should result in Staff reversing the Company’s $277,500 reduction to the calculation period.  
Instead, however, Staff further reduced the non-major storm expense by $212,859.  Id.  As a 
result, under the four-year average methodology for this element of expense, the rate allowance 
should have increased by $79,000, as opposed to the reduction of $57,000 reflected by Staff.  Id.

As part of the Electric Stipulation, the Company and Staff agreed that the prestaging 
thresholds for purposes of the Major Storm Reserve would not be increased from $1.75 million 
to $3.6 million.  Exhibit 516, Schedule B.  The Company and Staff further stipulated that the 
Rate Year’s electric revenue requirement will include Non-Major Storm Restoration Expense of 
$7.634 million, which represents the four-year average of non-major storm expenditures 
(exclusive of the Company’s proposed normalization adjustment to reflect a change in the pre-
staging sharing caps), adjusted for inflation.  Exhibit 516.  The Company and Staff also agreed 
that the $57,000 downward adjustment to this expense item will not apply.  Exhibit 516.  The 
Company also agrees to work with Staff to provide quarterly reporting on minor storm activities.   
The Company and Staff, typically adverse parties, settled their dispute regarding non-
major storms by agreeing to an amount the reflects Staff’s litigation position regarding the pre-
staging thresholds but the Company’s litigation position regarding how maintaining the existing 
thresholds impacts the non-major storm cost element.  The Electric Stipulation as it relates to this 
issue is supported by the record and falls within the range of testimony filed by the Company and 
Staff.  The agreed upon non-major storm expense is consistent with the Public Service Law and 
Commission precedent.  For this issue, the Electric Stipulation strikes a fair balance between the 
interests of customers and the Company.  Given these factors, it is reasonable to conclude that 
had these issues been fully litigated Your Honors and the Commission would have reached a 
similar result.  Accordingly, this portion of the Electric Stipulation meets the public interest 
standard set forth in the Commission’s Settlement Guidelines and should be adopted by Your 
Honors and the Commission without modification.

Other Operating Insurance 
This expense category is composed of many different types of insurance, but the dispute 
here concerns primarily the Rate Year expense for all risk commercial property insurance.  Tr.  
4121-4122.  The premiums for this insurance are composed of a rate per million dollars applied 
against an aggregate property value.  The Company then increased this rate by 10% yearly at 
July 1, 2023, and at July 1, 2024.  Id.  Focusing solely on a decline in the July 2021 to July 2022 
rate per million of insured value, Staff proposed to use the latest known costs from July 2023 
premium payments for all risk commercial property insurance, increased by GDP inflation, to 
develop a Rate Year projection.  Tr. 4123.  Staff’s forecast reduces the Company’s Rate Year 
forecast by $136,000 for electric and $34,000 for gas.  Id.  Although appropriate to adjust the 
expense to reflect latest known insurance premium levels, simply applying inflation to premiums 
to arrive at the Rate Year expense projection is not appropriate.  
Although Staff focused on the experience of only one year to justify its claim that this 
expense should only be increased by inflation, Staff pointedly ignored the significant growth in 
insured value from July 2019 to July 2021.  Tr. 795.  Moreover, in response to discovery (CH to 
DPS (038) included in Exhibit 243 (RRP-5R)), Staff confirmed that its review of the information

provided by the Company demonstrated a historic compound average growth in the rate per 
million that exceeded the rate of inflation.  Tr. 796.  So, clearly, simply applying inflation 
understates this cost element.

performance to all customers and thus should be approved.  Tr. 3080.

Average Service Lives 
Although calling the Company’s proposal to maintain the existing ASLs for depreciation 
accounts in order to limit the rate increase “admirable,” Staff Witness Huang then embarked on 
his own exercise to recalculate various of the accounts based on his visual examination of their

accounts.  Tr. 2276.  Similarly, while agreeing with the majority of the ASLs calculated by Mr. 
 
Planning Proceeding in Case 20-G-0131), as will be explained, infra, retaining a 10% reserve deficiency for gas 
assets at the end of an amortization period 20 years from now is a highly questionable policy.  Moreover, the 
usual 20-year amortization of the reserve deficiency is also too long for electric assets, given the changes and 
upgrades to the electric system that are likely to result under the CLCPA. 
54  The Company has quantified the impact of adopting the 2022 Depreciation Study Rates and presented the 
information in Exhibit 162 (ATP-4R).  Additionally, the Company compiled Exhibit 163 (ATP-5R) to quantify 
the differences between the Company’s position of adopting the rates as determined in the Study versus Staff’s

--- Page 89 ---
 
81 
 
Spanos for the 29 gas accounts, Mr. Huang recalculated four of them.  Tr. 2278.  And, finally, 
while agreeing with the majority of ASLs calculated by Mr. Spanos for the 24 common accounts, 
Mr. Huang recalculated five of them.  Tr.  2279.  A closer look at some of the results of Mr. 
Huang’s ASL methodology reveals significant flaws in his approach.   
Mr. Spanos explained that Staff’s ASL proposals rely unduly on either visual 
comparisons to the Company’s historical data or current service life parameters, many of which 
were the result of settlement discussions and positions that the Company accepted from the last 
rate cases.  They are also unduly constrained to 10 years’ movement.  Tr. 280.  This approach 
produced unrealistically long service lives for the property studied or too much emphasis on the 
service life estimates developed solely in settlements.  Id.  Service life estimates (i.e., average 
service life and survivor curves), however, should be based on more than mathematical or visual 
analysis and should represent the mortality characteristics of the property studied and not just a 
comparison to a rate currently utilized.  Id. 
When estimating service lives, it is important to consider factors beyond just the 
statistical life analysis results so that the recommended estimates are consistent with reasonable 
expectations of the mortality characteristics of the property studied.  Tr. 282.  Indeed, the 
National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’(“NARUC”) Public Utility 
Depreciation Practices manual, which is one of the most widely recognized authoritative 
depreciation texts, explicitly states: 
Depreciation analysts should avoid becoming ensnared in the mechanics of the 
historical life study and relying solely on mathematical solutions.  The reason for 
making an historical life analysis is to develop a sufficient understanding of 
history in order to evaluate whether it is a reasonable predictor of the future.  The 
importance of being aware of circumstances having direct bearing on the reason 
for making an historical life analysis cannot be understated.  These circumstances, 
when factored into the analysis, determine the application and limitations of an 
historical life analysis. 


--- Page 90 ---
 
82 
 
 
Tr. 289-290 (emphasis added).    
Staff Witness Huang’s approach, however, relies entirely on statistical analysis and visual 
fitting of the curve to historic information limited to the Company and ignores completely 
changes such as the CLCPA or technological advances that make the past a poor guide to the 
future depreciation forces.55  As Mr. Huang conceded, it is possible that if there is an anomalous 
event that renders the future different from the past, the historical data in the curve it represents 
can be skewed and in error.  Tr. 2315.  He also acknowledged that in that case historic data will 
not represent proper depreciation rates for the future.  Tr. 2316.  Mr. Huang further conceded he 
looked only to Central Hudson data rather than the experience of other utilities for similar 
property.  Tr. 2312.  All depreciation texts, however, are clear that service life estimates are 
forecasts of the future.  In fact, as Mr. Spanos explained, it is widely understood by depreciation 
professionals that sole reliance on the statistical analysis of historical data is inappropriate for life 
estimation.  Tr. 289.  By relying solely on historic data and visual curve fitting of that data, Staff 
ignores the significant forces at work that will render the future different from the past.  As a 
stark example of the deficiencies of Staff’s approach, Mr. Spanos selected several accounts to 
highlight how the survivor curves determined by Staff are not reasonable for future life cycles. 
a. Electric Plant Accounts  
i. 
Account 356.10 – Overhead Conductors and Devices 
(Transmission)  
For these overhead transmission line conductors, Staff chose an 80-R2 survivor curve for 
this account rather than the 70-R2 survivor curve in the Study, arguing that the longer lived 
curve is a “better visual fit.”  Tr. 2275-76; see also Tr. 291.  The average range of service lives

used for this account by other electric utilities, however, is only 50 to 65 years and the average

56  Mr. Huang conceded both that he was unaware of the ASLs employed by other NY utilities (Tr. 2311), that an

true when the ASLs employed for gas services by other New York utilities range from 45 to 65 
years.  Tr. 297-298.  Not only will levels of future retirements be affected by the CLCPA, but it 


--- Page 93 ---
 
85 
 
is very likely that much shorter service lives will be appropriate in future studies as most services 
will need to be upgraded more frequently than previously.  Tr. 298. 
As explained, service life estimates are forecasts of the future.  Because Staff’s analysis 
relies only on visual curve fitting of past results, it fails to consider all forces of retirement.  The 
Study, in contrast, does so and the ASLs presented therein are superior and should be adopted. 
3.

their inadequacy and their inconsistent application.  First, in instances in which the net salvage

approach is required and limits movement to a maximum of ten percentage points.57  Tr. 2329-
2330; Tr. 298.58  Second, Staff, however, does not even apply its “gradual” process consistently, 
changing it seemingly at will.  Tr. 2281-2282; Tr. 299.59  Here, too, a description of 
representative accounts highlights the errors and inconsistencies in Staff’s approach.

recommendation is found on page 22 of Mr. Huang’s testimony, where he states “…I limited my 
 
57  Net salvage factor refers to the component of the depreciation rate that allows recovery of the estimated net 
salvage.  It is expressed as a percentage, based on the original cost of plant.  For example, if a unit of property 
has a total installed cost of $1,000, $0 estimated future salvage, and an estimated cost of removal of $500, the 
net salvage factor is -0.50, or -50% net salvage factor.  Tr. 2269.

86 
 
recommendations to 10 percent movement from currently approved net salvage factors, 
following the principal of gradualism.”  Tr. 2282, lines 3-6.  While “gradualism” may have its 
virtues, it also has its limits.  The average net salvage for the 1965 to 2022 time period is 
negative 152% and the most recent 5-year net salvage average is negative 157%.  Tr.  302.  The 
Study’s estimate of negative 70% is a modest attempt to move the salvage estimate closer to the 
reality experienced since 1965.  Tr. 302.  Staff’s mechanical application of its gradual approach 
to limit salvage to a 60% negative net salvage value appears grossly misplaced given that the 
most recent 5-year net salvage average is negative 157%.  Tr. 302.    
ii. 
Account 356.1, Overhead Conductors and Devices  
The statistical analysis for this account is combined with all other subaccounts (356.15, 
356.20 & 356.25) for distribution overhead conductors and devices.  Without any support 
provided at the individual account level, Staff proposed a different net salvage estimate of 
negative 35% for Electric Account 356.1 during the prior case, which was approved in the 
settlement.  Tr. 308.  In the Study, the Company is recommending a net salvage estimate of 
negative 60% based on a combined analysis of all four subaccounts.  The combined statistical 
analysis for the 1965 to 2022 period and most recent five-year period is negative 123% and 
negative 179%, respectively.  Id.  Despite this, Staff again recommends net salvage estimate of 
negative 45% for Account 356.10 and negative 50% for the other subaccounts in Account 356.  
Other than gradualism run amok, there is simply no explanation or justification for this gross 
disparity. 
iii. 
Account 365.00 – Poles, Towers and Fixtures    
Staff proposes a negative 40% value for this account while the Study used a negative 
50% value.  The average net salvage for the 1965 to 2022 time period is negative 52%.  Tr. 303.  


--- Page 95 ---
 
87 
 
Although the most recent 5-year net salvage average is negative 41%, it is not 40% and the 
longer experience counsels a more negative value than Staff would use.   
iv. 
Account 367.00 – Underground Conductors and Devices  
For this account, the only implication that can be drawn from Staff’s negative 25% net 
salvage estimate compared to the negative 40% proposed in the Study is that it – again – fits 
Staff’s artificial constraint of a maximum 10 percentage point movement in salvage values from 
the existing 15% negative salvage value.  Tr. 304.  That artificially constrained salvage value is, 
however, inconsistent with the net salvage costs experienced by the Company for the overall 
period of 1965 to 2022 and very conservative when compared to the most recent 5-year net 
salvage average (negative 39% and negative 69%, respectively), both of which support the 
Study’s estimate of 40%.   
v. 
Account 369.10 – Services – Overhead  
As with other artificially restricted salvage values, Staff’s negative 75% net salvage 
estimate for this account rather than the negative 100% net salvage estimate in the Study 
is only 10 percentage points greater than the negative 65% net salvage estimate agreed upon in 
settlement in the prior case.  Tr. 305.  Here, too, such an artificial constraint is unfounded when 
the average net salvage for the 1965 to 2022 time period is negative 118% and the most recent 5-
year average net salvage is negative 158%.  Tr. 305.  Seen against these negative salvage 
percentages, the 100% value used in the Study is quite conservative.

i. 
Account 376.00 – Mains 
Staff is recommending a negative 50% net salvage estimate for this Account rather than 
the negative 60% net salvage estimate proposed in the Study.  Tr. 306.  Although the most recent 
5-year net salvage average is negative 49%, the overall net salvage average for the 1965 to 2022

time period is negative 68%.  Id.  The negative 60% net salvage proposed in the Study is lower 
than the overall negative 68% and, under the circumstances, is more appropriate.     
ii. 
Account 380.00 – Services  
The Study proposed a 100% negative net salvage value for this account.  Tr. 307.  Staff’s 
negative 60% net salvage estimate for this account (Tr. 2323) ignores the fact that the salvage 
average for the 1965 to 2022 time period is negative 136%.  Even with the most recent 5-year net 
salvage average of negative 44%, there is no reason to choose a negative salvage value that is 
less than one-half the historical average of negative 136%.  And, oddly, Mr. Huang conceded, in 
this one instance he did not apply his gradual approach.  Tr.  2323-2324.  Here, again Staff’s 
approach is a one-way street where gradualism is applied only where the revenue requirement 
can be reduced.   
 
For all the reasons explained above, the salvage values in the Study should be adopted 
over Staff’s estimates.  Moreover, it must be understood that the salvage values – like ASLs – 
are directly related to the reserve deficiency.  If they are artificially constrained, as they are by 
Staff’s gradualism approach, the reserve deficiency will grow unnecessarily.  To the extent that 
only the excess over 10% of the reserve deficiency is amortized, that is already a gradual 
approach.  Staff’s approach to limit salvage values to a ten percentage point movement will only 
impose additional gradualism onto an already gradual process and should be rejected.     
4. 
Amortization of Reserve Deficiency 
The theoretical reserve is the amount of depreciation expense that should have been 
collected as of a particular date, given the survivor curves, ASLs, and net salvage factors used to 
determine the proposed depreciation rates.  Tr.  2270.  As demonstrated above, the Study’s 
results are superior to Staff’s manipulation of them.  Consequently, the Company is seeking to 
recover the reserve deficiency consistent with the values in the Study.  The Company compiled 


--- Page 97 ---
 
89 
 
Exhibit 163 (ATP-5R) to quantify the differences between the Company’s position of adopting 
the rates as determined in the Study versus Staff’s position, which was predicated on the Study 
with modifications.  Tr. 1538.  Based on the calculation of the reserve deficiency consistent with 
the Study, the Company proposed to reduce the electric and common under-reserve position to 
10% and to eliminate the under-reserve position for gas.  Tr. 1495. 
First, to be clear, the Company disagrees with the depreciation rate adjustments made by 
Staff, and thus maintains that its originally proposed calculation of the underfunded status of the 
reserve in the Study as presented in Exhibit 82 (ATP-3) is accurate.  Tr. 1539. 
 
Second, the Company disagrees with both Staff’s calculation of the depreciation reserve 
and its proposed method for collecting the deficiency if one is found to exist.  Staff’s position is 
to spread recovery of any reserve deficiency above 10% over 20 years for the electric, gas and 
common theoretical reserves.60  Because Staff’s adjustments to ASLs and salvage values 
artificially reduced depreciation rates, Staff argues that the book to theoretical reserve variation 
for electric, gas and common is less than 10% and, thus, does not require any further collection.  
Tr. 1539. 
We have previously demonstrated that Staff’s adjustments to the Study are improper and 
should be rejected.  This is especially true given Staff’s gradualism approach, which simply piles 
on more gradualism to the gradualism already inherent in the amortization of the deficiency.  The 
Company continues to maintain that the reserve deficiency is $135.9 million in total, of which 
$42.7 million for electric, $33.0 million for gas and $0.345 million for common should be 
recovered over a 10-year period.  This translates to the recovery of $4.3 million and $3.3 million 
 
60  Although Mr. Huang identifies the 20-year recovery timeline as “typical,” he concedes that the rate treatment of

annually beginning July 1, 2024, for electric and gas, respectively.  See Exhibit 239 (RRP-1R)

The 10-year recovery period proposed by the Company provides a more reasonable

Company believes that the entirety of the gas depreciation theoretical reserve deficiency should

gas under-reserve will unduly burden future customers in order to recover the reserve beyond the 
projected life of the assets.  Tr. 1540.  The entire gas reserve deficiency and the excess of 10% of 
the electric and common deficiency should be recovered over 10 years.

Based on Central Hudson’s response to DPS-453, question 5(a), Staff claims that the

G. 
Deferrals62 
As an initial matter, the Company disagrees with the general premise asserted by Staff 
that deferrals are not appropriate in the context of a one-year litigated case.  Tr. 4049, 4095-
4096, 4153.  As Staff seems to recognize (Tr. 4049), reconciliation is appropriate for significant 
items that cannot be reasonably forecasted.  In fact, the Company and Staff are in agreement on a 
number of deferral mechanisms continuing in the Rate Year as set forth on Exhibit 160 (ATP-
2R).  The need for a reconciliation should not turn on whether the Commission is dealing with a 
one-year litigated case or a multi-year rate plan.63  Deferral mechanisms are a common tool 
employed by the Commission to protect both customers and the Company against variations in 
hard to forecast expenses.  Put simply, if costs cannot be reasonably forecast, a deferral is 
appropriate.   
Staff’s suggestion that deferral mechanisms are unnecessary since the Company can 
always file a deferral petition (Tr. 4049) is misleading.  The Commission traditionally employs a 
three-part deferral test and unless an expense meets all three prongs of that test – including a 
materiality threshold of 5% of the Company’s net income – a deferral petition is unavailable to 
the Company.  As previously indicated, in Central Hudson’s case, this would mean an expense 
would need to exceed the level in rates by an amount equal to nearly 50 BPs.  Moreover, that 
threshold must be met for each expense since the materiality test cannot be satisfied by 
 
62  In testimony, the Company had requested a deferral mechanism for costs associated with the ERP Phase III 
Assessment and project readiness work.  Tr. 1314, 1513; ATP Exhibit 160 (ATP-2R).  Due to the expected one-
year nature of these rate cases, the Company notes that it is no longer pursuing the ERP Phase III Assessment in 
the Rate Year.  The Company, therefore, is no longer requesting a deferral mechanism for the ERP Phase III 
Assessment in these proceedings. 
63  Case 05-E-1222 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of

Modifications at 97-98 (Aug. 23, 2006) (authorizing a storm deferral mechanism in a one-year case because 
“the potential for the Company to incur substantial costs in the circumstances of severe weather provide proper 
justification for the use of reserve accounting to cover the costs that cannot be adequately forecast in advance 
and must be incurred for service reliability and continuity.”).

combining unrelated expenses.  Staff’s suggestion that the Company could always file a deferral 
petition is nothing more than an empty gesture. 
The remainder of this section addresses deferral items proposed by the Company that 
were disputed in Staff’s testimony and exhibits.  For the reasons set forth below, the Company’s 
proposed deferral mechanism are appropriate, reasonable, and in the public interest and therefore 
should be adopted by Your Honors and the Commission.   
1. 
FERC Jurisdictional Proceedings Regarding Hydroelectric Facilities 
This mechanism allows the Company to defer incremental O&M expenses and the 
revenue requirement effect on incremental capital spending incurred in a Rate Year as a result of 
a Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (“FERC”) proceeding concerning hydroelectric 
facilities when the total impact is greater than 10 BPs of return on common equity for the electric 
department.  Exhibit 90 (ATP-11).  In addition, if the Company implements incremental capital 
related to any such proceeding, the balance will be reduced from actual electric net utility plant 
prior to comparison to the Net Plant target for calculating the net plant deferral.  Exhibit 90 
(ATP-11).   
While Staff had initially indicated it was opposed to this deferral, the Company and Staff 
now agree that this deferral should continue to apply in the Rate Year.  Tr. 1542; Exhibit 159 
(ATP-1R), p. 4.   
2. 
Governmental, Legislative and Other Regulatory Actions 
The Company proposed to continue its deferral mechanism that allows it to defer the 
revenue requirement effect of any governmental, legislative or other regulatory actions as 
described in the 2021 Rate Plan.  Exhibit 90 (ATP-11).  The Staff Accounting Panel opposed its 
continuation arguing that the risk to the Company is limited, as the Company has the ability to 


--- Page 106 ---
 
98 
 
seek new rates or petition the Commission for deferral authority if the impact meets the 
traditional deferral criteria.  Tr. 4149-4150.  Staff’s position is unavailing.   
A reconciliation is appropriate regardless of the duration of the rate plan for significant 
items that cannot be reasonably forecasted such as future governmental, legislative and other 
regulatory actions.  Tr. 1542-1543.  As recognized by Staff, expense related to these types of 
actions is non-discretionary.  Tr. 4182.  Furthermore, historical experience (i.e., Horseheads 
Order, Bulk Electric System, Tax Cuts and Jobs Act, New York State Budget changes) supports 
the Company’s position that future changes in governmental, legislative or regulatory actions 
could significantly impact customers or the Company.  Tr. 1543.  Without sufficient visibility to 
allow for any such changes to be incorporated into revenue requirements, a deferral mechanism 
is appropriate.   
3. 
Existing Variable Rate Debt 
Central Hudson seeks to continue its deferral for long-term interest costs on existing 
variable rate debt.  While the Staff Accounting Panel initially recommended discontinuing this 
deferral, Staff Witness Hale recommended that it continue.  Mr. Hale explained that with interest 
rates rising, it would not be unreasonable for the Company to consider the possibility of 
refinancing its debt and continuation of the true-up will assure there is no disincentive to 
maintaining the variable rate debt as long as it is economically advantageous to do so.  Tr. 2403-
2404; Exhibit 159 (ATP-1R), page 2.  
Accordingly, the Company and Staff agree that this deferral should continue into the Rate 
Year.  
4. 
New Fixed and Variable Rate Debt 
The Company is proposing to modify the deferral for new fixed and variable rate debt in 
the Rate Year.  Historically, the deferral has only been provided in multi-year agreements during 


--- Page 107 ---
 
99 
 
the second and third Rate Years.  Tr. 1518.  However, because the country is in a time of 
significant economic uncertainty with the highest inflation rate in four decades and global events 
that have had a drastic impact on natural gas prices and raw materials, not to mention uncertainty 
as to when and by how much the Federal Reserve may change interest rates in the near term, the 
Company is seeking the true-up of the entirety of its weighted average cost of long-term debt to 
the rate as reflected by the Finance Panel in the Rate Year.  Tr. 1518.  Staff Witness Hale 
opposes a deferral mechanism for fixed rate debt arguing that the Company largely has control 
over when and how often it goes to the market and has the ability to structure their fixed rate 
debt efficiently.  Tr. 2405-2406.  Staff Witness Hale also opines that the Commission typically 
does not approve reconciliation mechanisms for fixed rate long-term debt in a one-year 
proceeding, as it would not provide Central Hudson with the proper incentive to minimize 
issuance rates.  Tr. 2406.  Mr. Hale’s position is misguided. 
With the volatility of interest rates and continued uncertainty in the market, it is 
extremely important that a deferral mechanism remain in place.  Tr. 1003.  Notably, this two-
way deferral would protect customers as much as it would protect the Company.  While Mr. Hale 
indicates (Tr. 2396) that current rates are superior to using forecasted rates, the latest Blue Chip 
Financial Forecasts, which the Company’s position relies on for interest rate forecasts, projects a 
decline in interest rates for 2024 and 2025 which should result in rates on Company issuances of 
approximately 6.25% and 6.0%, respectively.  If interest rates fall, as predicted by the Blue Chip 
Financial Forecasts, customers will pay more for the cost of debt in rates under Mr. Hale’s 
position than they would have if there were a two-way fixed rate debt cost deferral mechanism in 
effect.  Tr. 1003.  For this reason alone, the deferral for new fixed rate debt should remain in 
place. 


--- Page 108 ---
 
100 
 
5. 
Late Payment Fee and Reconnection Fee Revenue 
The Company recommends continuation of the existing symmetrical deferral of actual 
finance charge (late payment fee) and reconnection fee revenues above or below the levels 
included in the final revenue requirement if the impact is greater than 10 BPs of return on 
common equity for either the gas department or electric department.  Exhibit 90 (ATP-11).  The 
Staff Accounting Panel opposes continuation of this deferral mechanism because, according to 
Staff, such reconciliation mechanisms are not generally employed during a one-year rate case 
and the Company always has the ability to petition for a deferral when actual revenues vary 
significantly from the amount established in revenue requirement. 
Once again, Staff’s position is misguided.  As Staff recognizes, the Company is not 
currently assessing late payment fees or reconnection fees.  Tr. 4158.  However, an assumption 
of $4.5 million for electric and $1.3 million for gas, calculated based on historical methodology, 
was embedded in the development of revenue requirements.  Tr. 1544.  This forecast was put 
forth under the premise that it would be accompanied by a deferral, as the forecast for these 
elements of revenue remains extremely uncertain.   
Staff agrees that the level of late payment charges and reconnection fee revenue is related 
to the level of collection and termination activity at the Company (Tr. 4157) – activity which the 
Company has paused over the last four years.  Given the current ambiguity around customer 
decisions regarding payments and DPAs as the Company recommences its full collection 
activities, the timing and amount of finance charge revenues and reconnection fees cannot be 
accurately forecasted.  Tr. 1544.  This is particularly true given that the Company will soon end a 
four year pause with respect to the collection of residential late fee and reconnection fee 
revenues.  Indeed, Staff agrees this pause could create some level of uncertainty with forecasting 
these levels of revenue in the Rate Year.  Tr. 4159. 


--- Page 109 ---
 
101 
 
Given the uncertainty surrounding the level of late payment fee and reconnection fee 
revenue the Company will actually experience, a deferral will appropriately allow any over- or 
under-collected amounts compared to what is provided for in rates to be set aside for return to or 
collection from customers at a future date. 
6. 
Property Taxes 
Staff opposes the Company’s recommendation to continue its property tax deferral 
mechanism in the Rate Year, which allows the Company to defer 90% of any difference between 
actual property taxes and the rate allowance for future recovery or pass back to customers, with 
the Company’s share limited to five basis points each for electric and gas.  Tr. 4152-4153.  
According to Staff, a property tax reconciliation is not necessary in a one-year rate case because 
many of the factors influencing the Rate Year property tax forecast are known and therefore not 
subject to significant forecasting risk.  Tr. 4153.  The Company disagrees. 
Forecasting property taxes is difficult since many elements are largely outside of Central 
Hudson’s control.  Tr. 1545.  The Company has neither insight into, nor influence over 
municipal and school tax rates, other than potentially challenging assessment values and/or 
property tax rates on a reactive basis.  Tr. 1545.  Given the current economic and financial 
condition of New York State, municipalities may find it necessary to increase property tax rates 
to bridge the gap resulting from fluctuations in federal and state funding.  Tr. 1545.  In addition, 
contrary to Staff’s suggestion, not all elements of property tax expense will be known at the time 
of the Company’s next update in these proceedings, as School 2024/2025 taxes and Town 2025 
taxes will continue to be based on projections.   


--- Page 110 ---
 
102 
 
7. 
Right-of-Way Maintenance Distribution 
Staff disagreed with the Company’s proposed modification to this deferral in the context 
of a multi-year settlement, but in the context of a one-year litigated case, the Company and Staff 
agreed that this deferral mechanism should expire.  Tr. 1546. 
8. 
Uncollectible Write-Offs 
Staff recommends that the Commission deny the Company’s request to continue a 
deferral mechanism for uncollectible expense because the resumption of collection activities 
should help minimize uncollectible expense.  Tr. 4095-4096.  Staff also posits that the Company 
could file a deferral petition with the Commission seeking authority to defer costs if it 
experiences materially higher uncollectible expense than the amount allowed in rates.  Staff’s 
arguments are flawed.  Tr. 4096. 
First, it bears noting that the Staff Consumer Services Panel recommends that the 
Commission direct the Company to delay resumption of collection and termination activities 
even further until some unspecified testing is complete.  Tr. 4406.  If the Commission agrees 
with this recommendation, which it should not, the resumption of collection activities will not be 
available to help minimize uncollectible expense as the Staff Accounting Panel contends.  
Second, a deferral mechanism is necessary because Staff recommends setting uncollectible 
expense using a “pre-COVID three-year average of net write-offs as a percentage of delivery 
revenues subject to bad debts.”  Tr. 4094, lines 22-24.  The problem with Staff’s methodology is 
that its three-year average uses calendar years 2018-2020 and, unlike here, in the years prior to 
this three-year period, the Company’s collections and termination activity was not in a state of 
suspension.  It is therefore unclear whether resuming those activities now will result in Rate Year 
uncollectible levels similar to those experienced from 2018-2020.   


--- Page 111 ---
 
103 
 
The uncertainty surrounding the timing and amount of uncollectible write-offs is driven 
by a variety of factors including: the Company’s commencement of its full collection activities, 
the number of customers enrolling in deferred payment agreements, and future economic 
conditions.  Tr. 1547.  Additionally, the Company has proposed inclusion of collection agency 
fees as part of this deferral as it is directly correlated with any recoveries of write-offs to arrive at 
the net write-off amount included in rates.  Recognition of the uncertainty in the uncollectible 
write-off amount, net of recoveries, should also include a recognition of the directly related 
uncertainty in the amount of collection agency fees paid for the recoveries in that net amount.  
Tr. 1547.   
Based on the uncertainty surrounding uncollectible expense, a deferral is appropriate so 
that any over- or under-collected amounts compared to what is provided for in rates can be set 
aside for return to or collection from customers at a future date. 
9. 
Long-Term Gas Planning Proceeding 
The Company recommends that it be authorized to establish a deferral mechanism for 
incremental costs associated with the Gas Planning Proceeding, such as support for Joint Local 
Distribution Company filings and modernization of the gas planning process.  Exhibit 90 (ATP-
11).  Staff Witness Riebel disagrees with this recommendation because the Company has not yet 
made its long-term gas system plan filing nor provided reasonable support for incremental costs.  
Tr. 3663. 
Despite Mr. Riebel’s testimony, this deferral is reasonable and appropriate because the 
Gas Planning Proceeding is known and on-going and the Company filed its Long-Term Plan on 
February 7, 2024 in Case 23-G-0676.  Now that the filing has been made and the process to 
review the Long-Term Plan is underway, the Company will undeniably incur costs in the Rate 
Year related to its Long-Term Plan filing.  The Company will also most certainly incur 


--- Page 112 ---
 
104 
 
additional costs related to Joint Utility filings in Case 20-G-0131.  However, the exact costs 
remain unknown.  Given the uncertainty in the expenditure amounts that may be required under 
these existing proceedings, a deferral should be provided. 
10. 
Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act 
On August 16, 2023, Governor Hochul signed the into law the Roadway Excavation 
Quality Assurance Act, codified as Labor Law section 224-F (the “Act”).  Pursuant to the Act, as 
of September 15, 2023, certain roadway construction work by utility company contractors and 
subcontractors must comply with prevailing wage requirements.  Tr. 1235.  As indicated in the 
Company’s October 2, 2023 Update filing and further discussed in the Accounting and Tax 
Panel’s Rebuttal Testimony, Central Hudson is proposing deferral treatment for the revenue 
requirement effect (O&M and rate base, including a return) of the incremental impacts of the Act 
on the Company.  Tr. 1238.  The Staff Accounting Panel opines that Central Hudson did not 
provide testimony to justify the deferral and thus it should be denied until it has evaluated the 
impacts and presented a more thorough proposal.  Tr. 4153-4154.  During cross-examination, the 
Staff Accounting Panel made clear that it was not testifying that the Act would have no 
additional cost impact on the Company, just that it had not evaluated what those impacts might 
be.  Tr. 4186. 
Given when the Act was signed and became effective, it is understandable that the 
Company was unable to provide testimony regarding the impacts of the Act prior to the filing of 
Staff’s testimony.  The Company cured Staff’s claim of inadequate information through its 
rebuttal filing.  As explained by the Company’s Gas Capital and Operations Panel, the Company 
has determined that a majority of its gas capital work will fall under the Act’s definition of a 
“covered excavation project.”  Tr. 1236.  The Company has engaged with its existing contractors 


--- Page 113 ---
 
105 
 
and, to ensure compliance with the new law, they have sought increased funding for any 
excavation work with a permit date after September 15, 2023.  Id. 
Based on the Company’s preliminary review, the Act will increase Central Hudson’s gas 
capital program, particularly with regards to Distribution Improvement projects and the 
elimination of LPP, by up to approximately 27%.  Id.  Confidential Exhibit 215 (GCOP-2R) 
shows a breakdown of the potential changes to Distribution Improvement capital projects based 
on preliminary pricing received from the Company’s current contractors.  Although this exhibit 
only shows the potential impact to Distribution Improvement projects, there will be impacts 
across other gas programs as well.  Tr. 1236-1237. 
Similarly, the Company’s Electric Capital and Operations Panel testified that initial 
estimates indicate that the Act will increase Rate Year routine line clearance flagging costs by 
approximately $1.1 million and Hazard Tree flagging activity by $440,000.  Tr. 2144-2145.  
Further information on the Act’s anticipated impacts on these activities were provided in Exhibit 
199 (ECOP-4R).  
Now that the Company has provided testimony on and support for the Act’s impact on its 
electric and gas budgets, a deferral mechanism is wholly appropriate and should be authorized.
