H. 
Rate Adjustment Mechanism 
The Company recommends continuing the RAM in the Rate Year and increasing the 
threshold from 2.5% of total operating revenues to 2.4% of total revenues, inclusive of 
surcharges and commodity costs.  Tr. 1865-1866.  The RAM allows the Company to implement 
a surcharge or credit to collect or recover certain deferred costs.  Tr. 4143-4144.   
The Staff Accounting Panel opposes continuing the RAM in the Rate Year because 
through the rate setting process expense forecasts will have been reset for the time period of July 
1, 2024 through December 31, 2024, the time period for which Central Hudson would make the 
first measurement of its RAM eligible deferrals.  Tr. 4144.  According to Staff, the Company 
would be able to pursue a significant balance of regulatory assets or liabilities built up between 
July 1, 2024 and June 30, 2025, in its next rate case.  Tr. 4144.  Staff did not address the 
Company’s request to increase the RAM threshold. 
Staff’s comments and concern with continuing the RAM in the Rate Year are unsound.  
First, Staff ignores the relationship between carrying charges and the RAM and how the RAM 
allows for more timely collection or return of carrying charges to customers.  Tr. 1545.  Staff 
also fails to recognize that recovery using the RAM deferral is only implemented when the 
annual under-collected deferrals meet or exceed the minimum threshold.  Tr. 1545.  Therefore, 
the RAM would not be implemented if there were insufficient deferral balances to warrant 
collection and the RAM would continue to be capped at a maximum threshold to mitigate bill 
volatility.  Tr. 1545-1546.  Staff’s position also does not consider the impacts that removing this 
credit supportive mechanism can have on a rating agency’s perception of the regulatory 
environment in New York, which impacts the qualitative portion of rating agency assessments.  


--- Page 115 ---
 
107 
 
Tr. 990, 995, 1546.  The RAM also allows the Company to record the impact of non-cash 
carrying charges in compliance with GAAP.  Without the RAM, regulatory assets would have a 
negative impact on the Company’s earnings as the earnings impact of financing the regulatory 
asset would not be offset by the impact of non-cash carrying charges. 
For these reasons, the RAM deferral and cash recovery mechanism should be continued 
with the updated thresholds proposed by the Company. 
IV. 
RATE BASE ISSUES 
A. 
Electric Capital 
1. 
Electric Capital Projects 
The Company projected total electric capital additions, less cost of removal, of 
approximately $148.8 million, $146.0 million, $148.9 million, $155.2 million, and $155.2 
million for calendar year 2024 through 2028, respectively, totaling approximately $754.2 million 
over the five calendar years.  Tr. 2007.  The Company’s electric capital proposal is presented in 
the following eight categories: 1) Hydroelectric & Gas Turbines (Production); 2) Transmission; 
3) Substation; 4) New Business; 5) Distribution Improvements; 6) Transformers; 7) Meters; and 
8) Storms.  Tr. 2011.  The Staff Electric Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“SEIOP”) 
recommended two slight modifications to the Company’s electric capital proposal.   
First, the SEIOP recommended the removal of dollars associated with the Company’s 
proposed Storms Category of spending.  Staff argued that the Storms Category should be 
rejected because the Company continues to budget future storm related expenditures within other 
capital categories and, according to Staff, this may result in double counting of expenditures, and 
could create additional confusion in the future with respect to the Company’s tracking of capital 
expenditures.  Tr. 2578-2579.  Second, the SEIOP recommended the removal of the Company’s 
proposed Electric Transmission Structure Coating Program within the Transmission Category.  


--- Page 116 ---
 
108 
 
Tr. 2567.  Although the SEOIP agreed in concept with the program, it opines that it is not 
immediately necessary at this time because the Company has been managing its electric capital 
portfolio safely and reliably without the program.  Tr. 2568-2569. 
In rebuttal testimony, the Company’s Electric Capital and Operations Panel disagreed 
with Staff’s conclusion that approving the Storms Category would result in double counting of 
expenditures.  Tr. 2112-2113.  The Company also noted that disallowing recovery of the Storms 
Category may result in the Company not having sufficient funding to complete the planned 
electric capital projects and programs within the Company’s capital forecast that are needed to 
maintain the existing levels of reliability.  Tr. 2113.  With respect to the Electric Transmission 
Structure Coating Program, the Electric Capital and Operations Panel opposed Staff’s 
recommendation to postpone it until the next rate case because doing so could result in cost 
impacts to customers.  Tr. 2111. 
The Electric Stipulation (Exhibit 516) resolves Staff’s and the Company’s differences 
with respect to the Company’s electric capital expenditures in the Rate Year.  Specifically, 
pursuant to the Electric Stipulation, the electric capital expenditures included in Central 
Hudson’s electric revenue requirement for the Rate Year will reflect Staff's testimonial position 
as shown on Exhibit 318 (SEIOP-2), which is also included as Schedule A to the Electric 
Stipulation.  The agreed upon capital expenditures reflect the removal of expenditures within the 
Rate Year associated with the Company's proposed Storms Category and the Company's 
proposed Electric Transmission Structure Coating Program. 
The Company and Staff, typically adverse parties, settled their dispute regarding the 
electric capital expenditures by agreeing to utilize Staff’s litigation position.  The Electric 
Stipulation as it relates to this issue is supported by the record and falls within the range of 


--- Page 117 ---
 
109 
 
testimony filed by the Company and Staff.  The agreed upon electric capital expenditures are 
consistent with the Public Service Law and Commission precedent.  For this issue, the Electric 
Stipulation also strikes a fair balance between the interests of customers and the Company.  
Given these factors, it is reasonable to conclude that had this issue been fully litigated, Your 
Honors and the Commission would have reached a similar result.  Accordingly, this portion of 
the Electric Stipulation meets the public interest standard set forth in the Commission’s 
Settlement Guidelines and should be adopted by Your Honors and the Commission without 
modification. 
2. 
Hydroelectric & Gas Turbines 
The only dispute related to hydroelectric and gas turbines appears to be related to the 
allocation of the revenues generated from these facilities.  This issue is discussed in Section 
XIV.A.4 below. 
3. 
CATV Make-Ready Reconciliation Mechanism 
In 2023, Central Hudson experienced an unprecedented increase in applications for 
attachment to distribution poles.  Tr. 2038.  Due to the insufficient lead times associated with 
these non-discretionary projects, inclusion of these projects in capital and expense forecasts is 
not possible and any incremental expenditures would require reprioritization, which may impact 
planned distribution improvement projects necessary for the provision of safe and reliable 
service.  Tr. 2038.  For this reason, as well as uncertainty regarding the number and size of 
potential projects, the Company requested a deferral mechanism for these costs. 
Despite understanding the Company’s concerns regarding the uncertainty and volume of 
the number of applications, and the capital and expense costs that would be associated with these 
applications, the SEIOP recommended denying the Company’s deferral request.  Tr. 2576.  
According to Staff, the Company can reasonably forecast this expense for the Rate Year and the 


--- Page 118 ---
 
110 
 
Company should be able to consider prioritization of the work, which should result in immaterial 
rate impacts during the Rate Year.  Tr. 2576.  Staff’s recommendation should be rejected because 
it is based on flawed assumptions. 
Staff’s assertion that the Company can reprioritize the capital plan should additional 
expenditures be incurred above the CATV Make-Ready project forecast is unrealistic.  A single 
entity notified the Company of plans that required surveying 34,322 poles and completing the 
associated make-ready work within a 1.5-year period.  Tr. 2115.  In the Company’s response to 
DPS-660, (see Confidential Exhibit 197 (ECOP-2R)), the Company demonstrated that 
supporting this individual buildout alone would require a capital investment of $7.9 million.  
This would account for approximately 15% of the overall forecasted expenditures within the 
Distribution Improvement Category for 2024 and would hinder the Company’s ability to execute 
its capital plan without deferring these incremental costs.  Tr. 2115.  CATV Make-Ready 
projects typically occur in areas where the replacement of infrastructure would be otherwise low 
priority absent the need to support a broadband initiative.  These projects are non-discretionary 
and offsetting capital expenditures that exceed the CATV Make-Ready project forecast requires 
the postponement of projects that support other key initiatives, such as CLCPA Phase 1 projects, 
or projects designed to improve system reliability or operating issues.  Tr. 2115-2116.  For these 
reasons, the Company’s proposed CATV Make-Ready reconciliation mechanism should be 
adopted. 
4. 
Public Service Law Section 119-d (Maintenance of Utility Poles) 
Reconciliation Mechanism 
The new Public Service Law § 119-d requires that on or before April 1, 2024, the 
Commission shall promulgate rules and regulations to require, in addition to other maintenance 
procedures, the trimming of vines on utility poles throughout the state by utility company or 


--- Page 119 ---
 
111 
 
cable television company that owns such utility pole where such vine growth is likely to disrupt 
safe and reliable service.  Tr. 2145.  Currently, the Panel is not aware of any regulations 
promulgated by the Commission to implement Public Service Law § 119-d.  The Company’s 
current program/work activity associated with vine removal/trimming is triggered only by 
dispatch orders associated with customer inquiries or pole inspection findings.  Tr. 2145.  Should 
the new law and implementing regulations result in additional vine clearing activity, incremental 
funding will be required.  The Company proposes that it be authorized to defer any such 
incremental funding for future recovery.  Given that the Company is unable to forecast the 
impacts of this new law and implementing regulations at this time, deferral accounting treatment 
for these incremental costs are appropriate. 
B. 
Gas Capital 
The Company’s Gas Capital and Operations Panel (“GCOP”) presented the then 
proposed gas capital expenditure levels for calendar years 2024 through 2028, containing capital 
spending levels of $72.01 million in 2024, $80.01 million in 2025, $81.97 million in 2026, and 
$83.87 million in 2027.  See Exhibit 14 (ECOP-1) (providing the Company’s Five-Year Capital 
Plan summary).  The gas capital expenditures for the Rate Year were $79 million.  Exhibit 14 
(ECOP-1); Tr. 2184.  In contrast, Staff’s Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel (“SGIOP”) 
recommended gas capital expenditures of just $65.03 million in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2185.  Staff 
recommended a gas capital budget of approximately $62.6 million for calendar year 2024 and 
$67.4 million for calendar year 2025.  Id.  These amounts represent reductions of approximately 
$9.39 million for 2024 and $12.58 million for 2025.  Id.  Staff’s Exhibit 330 (SGIOP-2) contains 
a comparison of the Company’s and Staff’s proposed gas capital expenditures for 2024 through 
2027. 


--- Page 120 ---
 
112 
 
Staff noted that Central Hudson’s budget categories are:  1) Production; 2) Transmission; 
3) Regulator Stations; 4) New Business; 5) Distribution Improvements; and 6) Meters.  Tr. 2185.  
Staff agreed that the Rate Year capital projections for the Production and New Business 
categories are not in contention.  Tr. 2185, 2194-2195.  As discussed in detail below, the 
contested Staff adjustments to the Company’s capital budgets are not well supported. 
Furthermore, as discussed earlier, the Company’s GCOP noted in their rebuttal testimony 
that, since the filing of direct testimony, there has been a significant legislative development (the 
Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act) that will impact the Company’s gas capital 
program.  
1. 
Gas Transmission Projects 
Before delving into a specific discussion of Staff’s approach to the Transmission 
Category of gas capital expenditures, it is necessary to explain the underlying error to Staff’s 
methodology, which permeates the entirety of its approach to capital projects.     
Staff’s first error is its use of historic, three-year averages to determine capital 
expenditures for the future.  For example, regarding Transmission, Staff states:  
We recommend an alternate approach to determining the budget for all the 
remaining projects in the Transmission category based on a three-year historical 
average of expenditures in calendar years 2020, 2021, and 2022.  The three-year 
average is used because three years covers the most recent actual spending but 
also takes into account some historical data.   
 
Tr. 2190, lines 12-19.  Although the use of an historic average takes into account historic data, it 
neither takes into account the significant inflation that has occurred since 2020 (see Exhibit 544), 
nor does it factor in the even more dramatic run up in prices for construction materials caused by 
supply chain issues, shortages and other price escalation pressures.  By using an average of 
earlier years, including much of 2020 before inflationary pressures took hold, Staff’s method 


--- Page 121 ---
 
113 
 
ignores actual price increases and the prices that the Company is actually experiencing today and 
into the future.  Cross-examination of the SGIOP pointed out this shortcoming: 
Q. Okay. Let's -- let's break this down to an example, if I could. [H]ypothetically, 
if the cost -- if the construction cost for certain service was $10 in 2020, $20 in 
2021, and $30 in 2022, would you agree the three-year average for that would be 
$20?  
 
A. Yes.  
 
Q. And that $20 is $10 short of the third year in that example.  Is that right?  
 
A. Based on your example, that's correct.   
 
Tr. 2227, lines 15-25.  Moreover, inflating that artificially reduced number by the GDP-PI in the 
October 2023 Issue of the Blue Chip Economic Indicators, as Staff does (Tr. 2190-2191), is 
another artificial constraint that ignores actual price increases that are explicitly recognized in the 
Company’s budgets. 
 
The second fatal flaw in Staff’s approach is that construction activity, hence construction 
budgets, vary from year to year based on the actual infrastructure requirements that must be 
fulfilled to meet the Company’s obligation to provide safe and adequate utility service.  As the 
Company’s rebuttal testimony explains (Tr. 1240-1241), Staff’s approach actually starves capital 
from projects that Staff, itself, concedes are justified or needed (Tr. 2218); indeed, essential in 
many cases.  See, e.g., Tr. 3658-3659.    
The SGIOP contended that expenditure levels should be adjusted downward to $1.92 
million, $3.78 million, $4.12 million, and $1.31 million for calendar years 2024, 2025, 2026, and 
2027, respectively.  Staff’s adjustments amount to a 50% reduction ($11.07 million) to the 
Company’s Transmission projects over this four-year period.  Tr. 1239.  This adjustment is 
drastic and unwarranted. 


--- Page 122 ---
 
114 
 
First, Staff accepted the Company’s proposed expenditure levels for the Pipeline Mega 
Rule project.  Then, to determine expenditure levels for the remaining projects, Staff utilized its 
three-year average of historic spending for the Transmission Category from 2020 through 2022, 
adjusted for inflation.  As shown in Exhibit 330 (SGIOP-2), to reach Staff’s desired expenditure 
level for the entire Transmission Category, Staff indiscriminately adjusted every project line item 
other than the Pipeline Mega Rule project.  For 2024 and 2027, every project line item was cut 
by approximately 71%, and for 2025 and 2026, every project line item was reduced by 
approximately 81%.  Tr. 1239.  Tellingly, Staff did not eliminate or defer any specific project, 
nor were Staff’s project-level adjustments based on an individual project analysis.  Tr. 1239-
1240.  While Staff acknowledges the Company provided cost information on a project-by-project 
basis (Tr. 2219), instead of reviewing that data and making adjustments to those specific project 
budgets, Staff applied an indiscriminate generalized reduction to the overall Transmission 
Category and then used a results-driven approach to adjust the individual projects.  Tr. 1239-
1240, 2220.  That, alone, should warrant rejection of Staff’s adjustments. 
 
Staff’s support for specific projects within the Transmission Category further illustrates 
how insupportable Staff’s adjustment is.  For example, the SGIOP recognized the importance of 
the Pipeline Mega Rule project, for which the SGIOP testified it is “imperative that the Company 
begin working …  immediately.”  Tr. 2189, lines 5-6.  Similarly, Staff Witness Riebel testified 
that “the Poughkeepsie Receival MP/TP Interconnect [is also] an important project for the 
Company to maintain reliable gas service” and “will allow for better reliability in that region if 
an interruption were to occur on one of those lines upstream of the regulator station.”  Tr. 3658, 
lines 11-24.  The intractable problem is that Staff’s funding level cannot pay for both.  Given that 
Staff did not adjust the proposed expenditures for the Pipeline Mega Rule project, that leaves just 


--- Page 123 ---
 
115 
 
$435,000 for the Poughkeepsie Receival project, which represents a 71% reduction from the 
Company’s proposed expenditure level of $1.5 million.64  Tr. 1241.  During cross-examination, 
Staff indicated that its position was not that the Company’s budgets for this project, or any 
project for that matter, was inaccurate by 70%.  Tr. 2220-2221.  Clearly then, the Company 
would not be able to complete the Poughkeepsie Receival project under Staff’s recommended 
budget.  Even worse, given that the Pipeline Mega Rule project and the Poughkeepsie Receival 
project alone are projected to cost approximately $2.5 million in 2024, not only is Staff’s 
proposed 2024 expenditure level for the entire Transmission Category of only $1.9 million 
insufficient for both those projects, but Staff’s proposed funding allowance leaves nothing for the 
remainder of the Transmission projects.  The Company’s GCOP went on to explain the 
importance of several of the projects (many of which are mandated by law) that would be left 
completely without funding under Staff’s drastic approach.  See Tr. 1241-1243.    
Furthermore, Staff failed to provide recommendations on how the Company should 
manage the decreased levels of expenditures.  Staff also failed to comment on whether individual 
projects should be eliminated or deferred.  Staff simply confirmed in its response to CH to DPS 
(063) that “Staff does not intend to remove any particular gas transmission project proposed by 
the Company.  Staff adjusted the overall capital budget for this category.”  See Exhibit 213 
(GCOP-1R), page 10.  If Staff does not think other projects will be eliminated, why did Staff 
remove all funding for them?  In contrast, the Company provided evidence of the need for 
funding the Transmission Category in the 2024-2028 Capital Budget Book (“Capital Budget 
Book”), filed June 30, 2023 in Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429, with a specific justification for 
 
64  At the very least, similar to the Pipeline Mega Rule Project, the budget for the Poughkeepsie Receival project 
should have been itemized separately from the rest of the Transmission projects.   


--- Page 124 ---
 
116 
 
each individual project or program.  Tr. 1244.  Each Transmission project identified therein has a 
justified safety or reliability need that Staff does not rebut.  Id. 
 
For all of these reasons, Staff’s adjustment to the Company’s gas Transmission capital 
budget is not defensible.   
2. 
Regulator Station Projects 
Staff’s approach to capital funding for Regulator Station projects applies the same faulty 
three-year average of historic spending plus inflation that Staff used for Transmission projects.  
Tr. 1247.  Here, again, Staff did not identify specific projects to remove or defer, but uniformly 
applied a reduction in funding across all projects.  For the same reasons that using historical 
expenditures was inappropriate for establishing expenditure levels for the Transmission projects, 
this is an equally insufficient method of determining appropriate expenditure levels for Regulator 
Stations.   
 
Central Hudson proposed a capital budget of $3.30 million in calendar year 2024, $3.59 
million in calendar year 2025, $3.82 million in calendar year 2026, and $4.38 million in calendar 
year 2027.  Tr. 2192.  Based on its three-year averaging approach using calendar years 2020 
through 2022 and adjusted for inflation, Staff recommended budgets for this category of $2.13 
million, $2.17 million, $2.22 million, and $2.27 million in calendar years 2024, 2025, 2026, and 
2027, respectively.  Tr. 2193-2194. 
Contrary to Staff’s assertion that the Company failed to provide adequate justification 
and that the three-year experience in 2020-2022 is a better indication of capital needs (Tr. 2193), 
the Company’s Capital Budget Book provided a specific justification for each individual project 
or program.  Tr. 1247.  In response to DPS-555, included in Staff Exhibit 329 (SGIOP-1) at page 
147, the Company also provided a pro forma analysis of recently completed projects of the same 
theme as the proposed projects, which provides further justification for the requested funding for 


--- Page 125 ---
 
117 
 
each project.  Tr. 1247-1248.  The drivers of these Regulator Station projects are: pressure 
control improvements and recording chart replacements; pilot and line heater installations to 
ensure adequate functionality of regulator stations in cold weather; rebuilding of aged regulator 
stations to reduce the risk of failed infrastructure and under/over pressure events; and 
implementation of SCADA monitoring and future control that will reduce response times 
associated with under/over pressure events and provide enhanced ability to model and plan the 
gas system.  Id.  All of these are important safety and reliability functions.  
Nowhere does Staff explain why the Company’s forecasting methodology is wrong or 
inaccurate but, rather, Staff simply asserts, with no basis, that the most recent three-year average 
provides a better estimate.  Tr. 2193.  As previously indicated, historical spending levels are not 
always a reliable predictor of future activity and historical costs are hardly a reliable indicator of 
future capital costs in an inflationary environment.    
Moreover, the SGIOP’s proposed reductions to the Regulator Station projects are 
inconsistent with Staff Witness Riebel’s testimony that “The Company’s Regulator Station 
rebuild projects proposed in the Company’s rate filing are all important for the reliability of the 
gas system [and that]…[n]ot replacing them would jeopardize their ability to function and could 
put customers at risk of not receiving the gas that they depend on…”  Tr. 3660-3661, lines 22-6 
(emphasis added).  As SGIOP recognized, the budget drivers for this category are “replacement 
of out-of-date regulator stations, pressure control upgrades, the addition of conditioning 
equipment to provide enhanced safety reliability and the installation of coating systems via the 
regulatory station coating program to extend the life of existing stations.”  Tr. 2191, lines 15-21.  
By Staff’s own admission, its recommended budget for the Regulator Station projects, which 


--- Page 126 ---
 
118 
 
reduced the Company’s budgets by 35% in 2024 and 40% in 2025 and thus will not allow all 
projects to move forward, will needlessly jeopardize the reliability of the Company’s gas system. 
In fact, over 90% of the Regulator Station projects within the Five-Year Capital Plan are 
associated with compliance or maintenance of system standards.  Tr. 1249.  This means there 
will be an increased risk of negative impacts to gas safety and reliability if the Company’s 
proposed budget is not approved.  Examples of potential impacts include but are not limited to: 
over pressurization of pipelines; under pressurization of pipelines; and service interruptions due 
to regulator station equipment failures.  Tr. 1249.  As with Staff’s Transmission adjustments, its 
Regulator Station adjustments should be soundly rejected.  
3.

New Business expenditures include gas service to new homes and businesses, 
Underground Residential Development, and residential and commercial conversions.  Tr. 2194.

symmetrical deferral, for the New Business Category.  Tr. 1211-1212.  The SGIOP opposed an 
independent true-up mechanism for New Business (Tr. 2196) and in rebuttal testimony the 
Company agreed to withdraw the proposal.  Exhibit 160 (ATP-2R).  As a result, this is no longer 
an issue in the case. 
4. 
Distribution Improvements 
This category consists of a number of capital projects, including LPP replacement, Leak 
Prone Service Replacements, Large Diameter Gas Welded Pipe Replacements and a number of 


--- Page 127 ---
 
119 
 
other projects that are described in the GCOP testimony.  Tr. 1214-1227.  As shown in Exhibit 
330 (SGIOP-2), Central Hudson has projected total capital costs for these projects of $51.581 
million, $56.374 million, $60.382 million, and $63.691 million for the years 2024 to 2027, 
respectively.  Staff has contended that aggregate expenditure levels should be adjusted to 
$46.259 million, $48.704 million, $52.547 million, and $56.135 million for calendar years 2024, 
2025, 2026, and 2027, respectively.  Tr. 1250.    
Although Staff’s testimony discusses each of the projects separately for the Distribution 
Improvement Category, it is not necessary to address each separately because, as the Company 
explained, Staff used a combination of methodologies for calculating adjusted expenditures.  Tr. 
1250.  The first method utilized was a three-year average of historic spending (plus inflation) 
utilizing years 2020, 2021, and 2022.  Id.  The second method utilized was an adjusted pro forma 
pricing structure (per foot installation cost, plus inflation) also based on a three-year average of 
historic spend utilizing years 2020, 2021, and 2022.  Id.  These inconsistent methods both result 
in inaccurate calculations.   
The pro forma pricing utilized was not based on accurate information.  First, as 
previously explained, Staff’s use of the same stale 2020-2022 data, which does not reflect current 
prices, is indefensible.  Staff’s other adjustment to the Company’s pro forma pricing is no better.  
DPS-281 Attachment 1, included in Exhibit 329 (SGIOP-1) at page 11, shows Staff used work 
order (project) estimates for the Leak Prone Replacement projects to calculate annual 
differences.  Tr. 1251.  The estimates provided in this discovery response, however, were not 
representative of initial work order revisions, but rather later revisions that included project 
specific adjustments.  Id.  As a result, Staff calculated an adjusted pro forma pricing structure 
(utilizing a three-year average) that resulted in a pro forma adjustment of only 4.2%.  Id.  


--- Page 128 ---
 
120 
 
Contrary to Staff’s method, initial work order revisions (estimates) should be used to calculate 
pro forma pricing in order to accurately reflect year-over-year adjustments.  Utilizing the initial 
work order estimates as seen in Exhibit 216 (GCOP-3R), the Panel recalculated the differences, 
resulting in an overall adjustment of 10.4%.  Tr. 1251. 
  
Staff also inappropriately took an inconsistent approach to adjusting the individual 
projects within this category.  Tr. 1252.  Pro forma pricing should be used for all identified 
projects, not limited to identified LPP projects, to capture a more accurate budget.  Tr. 1252.  
Identified projects have an established scope and include project specific footages and road types 
to be used with pro forma pricing.  Confidential Exhibit 218 (GCOP-4R) shows these identified 
projects, including footages, road types, and pro forma pricing.  Finally, although Staff 
calculated inflation rates and included them for the initial pro forma pricing for 2023, Staff did 
not consider inflation rates for identified projects in 2024, 2025, 2026, and 2027.  Tr. 1252.  
Again, for all these reasons, Staff’s adjustments should be rejected.  
5. 
Meters 
Based on the cost of meters and the projected numbers of meters installed, the Company 
is requesting $2.93 million, $3.03 million, $3.21 million, and $3.41 million in calendar years 
2024, 2025, 2026, and 2027, respectively.  Tr. 2213.  Staff, however, recommended just $2.36 
million, $2.41 million, $2.46 million, and $2.51 million in calendar years 2024, 2025, 2026, and 
2027, respectively.  Tr. 2214.  Staff’s rationale for its adjustment is: 
[W]e have received conflicting answers for the forecasted number of meters to be 
replaced annually.  As such, we have used the historical three-year average, from 
calendar years 2020 to 2022, adjusted by inflation, in order to forecast our 
recommended budget...     
 
Tr. 2214, lines 15-18.  Ironically, Staff concedes here that, in order to get an accurate projection 
in the absence of specific information, it must use historic information as a proxy, but, in the 


--- Page 129 ---
 
121 
 
preceding adjustments – even where furnished copious, specific information – Staff still used the 
fallback adjustment it claims it only used here as a last resort.   
 
In any event, Staff’s claim of inadequate information has been remedied.  Staff’s 
testimony asserts that there was a discrepancy between the Company’s responses to DPS-283 
and DPS-584, included in Staff Exhibit 329 (SGIOP-1) at pages 54 and 158, which led them to 
default to the three-year average.  Tr. 2213-2214.  The GCOP agreed in their rebuttal testimony 
that there was a discrepancy in these discovery responses and clarified that the information 
provided in the Company’s response to DPS-283 Part C is based on the forecasted meters to be 
purchased (not inclusive of meters that would be reused or any ancillary equipment that would 
be needed) while the information provided in DPS-584 Attachment 1 Confidential is based on 
the sales forecasted projection for meters to be installed.  Tr. 1253-1254.  Due to this 
discrepancy, the Company provided Confidential Exhibit 220 (GCOP-5R), which shows the 
forecasted meter budget using the appropriate purchase forecast for both meters and ancillary 
equipment.  Id.  The clarification provided in Confidential Exhibit 220 (GCOP-5R) resolves 
Staff’s concern with the discrepancy identified in the discovery responses and the Company’s 
proposed budget for Meters should be adopted.

Lands and Building 
a. Daily Operations 
The Company’s Daily Operations programs are used for emergent needs and unplanned

As the Company described in testimony, the Daily Operations programs are for emergent 
needs and unidentified projects – the Company cannot provide specific descriptions of the work 
to be performed and the associated cost, as the need for that work has not yet arisen.  
Confidential Exhibit 310 (SCP-1), page 75.  Given the emergent nature of the programs, it is 
unreasonable for Staff to eliminate funding because the Company has not specifically laid out the 
projects the proposed funding will be used for.  Further, the Company disagrees that no historical 
information was provided in support of these expenditures.  In response to DPS-001, which is 
included in Exhibit 317 (SEIOP-1), the Company provided information in the 2024-2028 Capital 
Expenditures Workpaper Recategorization workpaper demonstrating that the Company spent 
approximately $438,000 on Daily Operations through September 2023.  Tr. 125.  The Company 


--- Page 131 ---
 
123 
 
is unable to provide information prior to 2023 as tracking of Daily Operations projects began in 
2023 and will be performed on a more granular level beginning in 2024.  Id. 
 
 
The Company’s proposed expenditures for Daily Operations projects are necessary to

projects.  Staff’s proposed reductions will constrain the Company’s ability to address these needs 
and may force the Company to shift capital funding away from important planned capital 
projects.  Therefore, the Company’s proposed expenditures should be adopted.

d. Paving

Company to address postponed paving needs.  Id.

reduction of $362,267 for 2024 and $193,017 for 2025.  Tr. 3831.  To arrive at the proposed 
expenditures, Staff used historical spending from 2020 and applied inflation.  Id.  Staff’s use of 
historical spending is inappropriate in this instance.  As mentioned above, the Company had to 
delay a number of paving projects from 2021-2023 and spent $0 in 2021 and 2022 and $12,500 
as of October 2023.  Redacted Exhibit 309 (SCP-1), page 104.  As a result of this delay, the 
Company now needs to address significant paving needs – many of the paved surfaces around 
Company facilities are in poor condition with visible cracks, patches and potholes.  Tr. 127.  
Staff’s proposed funding does not take into account the fact that the Company postponed paving 
projects, which necessitates a significant increase in funding for 2024 and 2025.  Adopting 
Staff’s expenditure level would cause the Company to further delay remediation of problem 
areas, which could lead to slips, trips, falls and subsequent injuries to employees or visitors.  Tr.

67  Capital Budget Book at 1170-1173. 
68  Id. at 1188. 
69  Id. at 1200-1203. 


--- Page 135 ---
 
127 
 
 
Staff recommended reductions to each of the projects listed above.  Staff reduced the 
Company’s proposed expenditures for the Training Academy - Annex project by $97,000 in 
2024 and $1.53 million in 2025.  Tr. 3835.  The Company’s proposed expenditures for the 
Transportation Building - EC project were reduced by $224,000 in 2025.  Tr. 3838.  Staff 
reduced the Company’s proposed expenditures for the Butler Building Rebuild Project by 
$197,000 in 2025.  Tr. 3840.  Finally, Staff reduced the proposed expenditures associated with 
the Ellenville Office Renovation Project by $20,590 in 2025.  Tr. 3843. 
 
The Company disagrees with Staff’s reductions for a number of reasons.  First, to arrive 
at the “raw cost” of the projects, Staff relied on the estimates for the contractor portion of the 
projects.  Tr. 3833.  The Company included other costs, including but not limited to internal 
Central Hudson labor, to arrive at the “raw cost.”  Tr. 129.  These other costs do not appear to be 
reflected in Staff’s forecasts.  It is not possible for the Company to complete the projects without 
the costs associated with owner-provided services.  Id.  Further, Staff only applied project 
contingency factors to the overhead and Allowance for Funds Using During Construction 
(“AFUDC”) cost estimates.  Tr. 129.  The Company applied the project contingency factor to the 
entire cost estimate to determine the project budget, which is consistent with the Company’s 
Project Management Manual: Procedures and Best Practices.  Id.; Exhibit 317 (SEIOP-1), page 
110.  Staff’s reductions do not provide sufficient contingency for these projects, which reduces 
the probability that a given project will be completed within its budget. 
 
Finally, for the Transportation Building - EC, Butler Building Rebuild, and Ellenville 
Office Renovation projects, Staff applied an 8% contingency factor, instead of the 20% applied 
by the Company.  Tr. 130.  According to Staff, the contingency factors were changed to keep 
project contingency consistent amongst projects.  Tr. 3840.  The Company is confused by this 


--- Page 136 ---
 
128 
 
adjustment.  The contingency factor applied to a project depends on how far along the project is 
in the design phase.  For projects that are further along, such as the Training Academy - Annex 
project, the Company applied an 8% contingency factor, which is consistent with the Central 
Hudson Management guidelines for a project that is in the definitive estimate phase.  Tr. 131.  
However, the projects listed above are in the conceptual estimate phase, and the Company 
appropriately applied a 20% contingency factor to reflect the increased risk with a project in the 
earlier stages of planning.  Tr. 131.  Staff’s adjustments to the contingency factor do not reflect 
the uncertainty associated with these projects, and therefore should be rejected. 
 
For the reasons discussed above, the Company’s proposed capital funding for these major 
projects should be adopted. 
2. 
Office Equipment 
The Company proposed capital funding of $560,000 in 2024 and $689,000 in 2025 for 
the Company’s Office Equipment projects.  These projects include the Office Chair Replacement 
Program, the Primary Control Center project, and the Tannersville - New Facility project.  These 
projects involve the purchase of office chairs and workstations at Company facilities.  To 
develop the proposed costs for these projects, the Company arrived at a per workstation value of 
$5,938, which accounts for all equipment needed to furnish a new space, inclusive of individual 
workstations, common areas (such as conference rooms and breakrooms), appliances and local 
storage.  Tr. 133.  The Company’s proposed cost for office chairs was $547 per chair.  Id.   
 
Staff agreed with the forecasting methodology used by the Company but disagreed with 
the cost per workstation and cost per chair proposed by the Company.  For the Office Chair 
Replacement Program, Staff used a value per chair of $290.  Tr. 3845.  For the Primary Control 
Center and Tannersville - New Facility projects, Staff used a per workstation value of $2,250.  
Tr. 3847, 3850.  The Company disagrees with the values utilized by Staff.  For example, the cost 


--- Page 137 ---
 
129 
 
per workstation value used by Staff is limited to office cubicles and does not account for the 
other items included by the Company, like furniture for common areas, appliances, and storage.  
Exhibit 636.  Staff’s proposed per workstation values will not allow the Company to adequately 
furnish the Primary Control Center and Tannersville - New Facility projects.  Further, the value 
Staff uses for office chairs is not premised on any specific chair but is an “industry-average 
estimate.”  Id.  The Company’s proposed cost per chair is premised on a vendor estimate for a 
chair that has a long-expected life and includes ergonomic features.  Tr. 134.  Selecting an office 
chair using Staff’s proposed funding will likely result in more frequent replacement needs, a 
reduction in quality, and the loss of ergonomic features.   
 
For the reasons discussed above, the Company’s proposed funding for the Office 
Equipment projects should be adopted.        
3. 
Transportation 
The Company proposed funding of $13.824 million in 2024 and $14.115 million in 2025 
for vehicle and equipment replacement.70  Exhibit 9 (CCOP-6).  In support of these expenditures, 
the Company performed an analysis to determine which vehicle/equipment would warrant 
replacement based on the Company’s established replacement criteria.71  Tr. 110.  This analysis 
demonstrates that the Company is significantly behind on its scheduled replacement cycles due 
to extended delivery times associated with supply chain constraints.  Id.  Prior to COVID-19, the 
Company experienced lead times of approximately one year for vehicles built to the Company’s 
specifications; following COVID-19, lead times for the same vehicles are approximately three

years.  Id.  In order to address these supply chain constraints, the Company put together a

by the end of the Five-Year Common Capital Plan.  Id. 
 
Staff reduced the Company’s proposed expenditures by $4.589 million in 2024 and 
$4.676 million in 2025.  To arrive at its proposed expenditures, Staff calculated the average costs

Staff’s use of historical spending to establish the expenditures for this category is

actual vehicle and equipment age/mileage/hours to establish the need for the Company’s 
proposed funding.  Tr. 135; Exhibit 9 (CCOP-6).  The data in this analysis is most appropriately

schedule of replacement needs.  Tr. 135.  Further, this analysis considered supply chain 
challenges in the aftermath of COVID-19.  Tr. 110.  The historical period used by Staff should

resulting supply chain challenges.

developing its forecast.  Tr. 3856.  The SCP agreed that COVID-19 supply chain-related issues 
constrained the availability of new vehicles from 2020-2022.  Tr. 3866.  However, during cross-
examination the SCP admitted that it did not look at the Company’s historic purchase of vehicles 


--- Page 139 ---
 
131 
 
from 2020-2022 in the context of COVID-19 supply constraints.  Tr. 3867.  Given the impact of 
COVID-19 and supply chain constraints on vehicle purchases experienced by the Company, and 
the fact that Staff did not consider those impacts when developing its forecast, Staff’s forecast is 
flawed and should be rejected.  Staff’s proposed reductions will prevent the Company from 
timely replacing essential vehicles and equipment, and therefore the Company’s proposed 
expenditure levels should be adopted.

D. 
Technology Capital 
The Company groups its IT capital projects into the following major categories:  Energy 
Management System (“EMS”) (operational technology); Electronic Data Processing (“EDP”) 
(hardware); Software; Security; and Communications.  Tr. 1301.  Staff seeks a reduction to the 
Company’s recommended EMS, EDP and Communications capital levels based on the outcome 
of Staff’s flawed methodology of using an inflation-based growth rate, which as noted previously 
does not accurately account for historic inflation thereby ignoring the prices that the Company is 
actually experiencing today and into the future.  More specifically, Staff used historic actual 
spending data for calendar years 2020 through 2022 and inflation rates of 3.65% (2023), 2.35% 


--- Page 140 ---
 
132 
 
(2024), 2.20% (2025), 2.20% (2026) and 2.20% (2027), resulting in a 12.87% annual downward 
adjustment to the amount of capital requested by the Company.  Tr. 1357-1358.   
The inflation rates applied by Staff to the historic data are severely understated.  The 
Company demonstrated that an 8% inflation rate, based on the Company’s actual experience, 
more accurately forecasts the vendor portion of the project cost estimate than Staff’s inflation 
rates, which are simply based on the latest forecasts of the GDP-PI.  Tr. 1358.  In arriving at the 
8% inflation rate the Company analyzed renewals for existing Technology vendor partners from 
2021 to 2023.  Tr. 1358.  Confidential Exhibit 250 (TCOP-2R) provides an overview of this 
analysis, which included a robust sample set of vendor agreements for strategic software and 
license vendors that the Company plans to continue to maintain over the next five years.  Tr. 
1358. 
In addition to the infirmities just discussed, establishing future growth based on historic 
actual spend levels adjusted for inflation is not an appropriate forecasting methodology for EMS, 
EDP and Communications projects because there are many technology capital projects in these 
areas that are unique to the Five-Year Capital Plan and not present in historic years.  Tr. 1358.   
1. 
Energy Management System 
EMS projects that are unique to the 2024-2028 period include:  the Outage Management 
System, Grid Modernization, and the Primary Control Center EMS projects.  Tr. 1358-1359.  
These projects are incremental additions to EMS and therefore are not captured within historical 
averages.  Tr. 1359.  Moreover, they are necessary for the provision of safe and reliable service 
and to achieve the benefits associated with the Primary Control Center.  Tr. 1359. 
2. 
Electronic Data Processing 
For the EDP Category, examples of projects unique to the 2024-2028 period are the 
Primary Control Center - IT HW and the Microsoft Roadmap: License/Contract Renewal - 


--- Page 141 ---

MS365 3-Year Renewal (“Microsoft License Renewal”).  These projects are both incremental

a. Emergent Software 
For example, Staff and the Company remain far apart on the treatment of Emergent 
capital funding within the Software Category.  Staff recommends removal of all projects labeled 
“Emergent” asserting that the Company “failed to adequately justify the need to fund these 
projects due to a lack of specific deliverables...”  Tr. 3815, lines 3-4.  Staff’s assertion lacks 
merit.   
The Company implemented an improved capital planning process for Technology 
projects in 2022.  Tr. 1363.  Prior to that date, Staff would have been partially correct as the 
Company lacked the full planning process capability to adequately classify projects as 
“emergent” versus “planned.”  However, that capability was added in 2022 and will continue.  
Exhibit 253 (TCOP-4R) provides a list of Emergent (unplanned) projects from the second half of 
2022 through November of 2023, showing 17 emergent requests in total.  In 2023, $3 million in 
Emergent capital projects have been requested and approved by the Technology Steering 
Committee.  Tr. 1363.   
Using 2023 as a baseline, the Company carried that amount forward into the Five-Year 
Capital Plan and applied a qualitative risk analysis to each Emergent area to reach a reasonable 
forecast of $10.4 million for Emergent initiatives over that five-year period.  Tr. 1363.  The 
Company leveraged a qualitative risk-based approach to forecast the amount of Emergent 
funding recommended for each Software area.  Tr. 1364.  The tab labeled “Emergent Overview” 


--- Page 144 ---
 
136 
 
in Exhibit 253 (TCOP-4R) provides an overview of how this estimating framework was applied.  
Tr. 1364.   
The Company applied a percentage of the total amount of named investments within a 
Software area, based on the strategic focus, relative volatility, and perceived unknowns within 
the area.  Software areas classified as “low risk” were allotted a 1-10% Emergent allowance, 
while areas classified as “moderate risk” were allotted a 11-20% Emergent allowance and areas 
classified as “high risk” were allotted a 21-35% Emergent allowance.  Tr. 1364.  Exhibit 253 
(TCOP-4R) provides more details on the risk analysis and forecasting approach for each 
respective Software area.  Tr. 1364-1365.  Based on the information provided above and in 
Exhibit 253 (TCOP-4R), the Company has fully justified the need for and funding level it seeks 
for these Emergent software capital projects and Staff’s recommended adjustments should be 
rejected.

c. IEDR Fin/OPS Data Analytics 
The Company and Staff are in agreement that the costs of IEDR Phase I and II projects 
should be treated as non-discretionary costs and recovered from customers per the Commission’s 
direction in Case 20-M-0082.  Tr. 1365.  However, there remains disagreement regarding the 
treatment of costs for “a portion of the IEDR project, called ‘IEDR-FIN/Ops Data Analytics’” 
that Staff asserts is “discretionary” and should not be funded or receive deferral treatment.  Tr. 
1365, lines 12-14.  
The IEDR - Fin/Ops Data Analytics project modernizes the Company’s data warehouse 
and reporting systems and consolidates legacy solutions with limited functionality onto the same 
scalable hosted solution used for the state's IEDR program.  Tr. 1365-1366.  This initiative 
would also allow for ease of data migration for future phases of IEDR as they are identified and 
the capture of efficiencies, since data sets migrated by the Data & Analytics project would 
already be present within the new platform.  Tr. 1366.  Merging the data solutions will also 
improve the quality and accuracy of Company data by ensuring that the same data governance 
policies and quality assurance tools are applied to the common platform.  Tr. 1366.  Finally, as 
data sets continue to grow in scope and size, the project will also allow the Company to more 
easily spin up additional servers and data storage to meet the requirements of any IEDR or 
internal Company use cases.  Tr. 1366-1367.   
If Central Hudson only implements the cloud solution for the IEDR Phase I and II 
requirements, all other data sets and Company reporting will be forced to remain in the on-
premises legacy data warehouse/reporting platforms.  Tr. 1366.  This means that the Company 
cannot leverage any benefits or synergies the new IEDR technology would provide and would be 
forced to continue maintaining the separate legacy data warehouse.  Tr. 1367.  Creating the new 
IEDR data solution and not funding the IEDR - Fin/Ops Data Analytics initiative would create a 


--- Page 147 ---
 
139 
 
duplication of data management systems, introduce needless inefficiency, and silo Company data 
in systems only designed to meet one function.  Tr. 1368.  Additionally, the IEDR project’s 
Phase I use cases have already benefitted from having data sets that have been previously 
extracted/replicated, cataloged, and made available within the existing data warehouse system.  
Finally, cutting funding for the Data & Analytics project’s planned integrations to systems not 
yet feeding data will eliminate potential benefits/synergies for future IEDR phases.  Tr. 1367.  
For the foregoing reasons, funding and deferral treatment should be provided for the IEDR - 
Fin/Ops Data Analytics initiative.

Communications 
Staff once again applied its flawed inflation-based growth rate to the three-year average 
of the Company’s historical 2020-2022 IT budgets to determine the forecasted funding for the

are projects that did not occur in prior years and were not captured in the historical averages, 
including the Land Mobile Radio Replacement with Digital Mobile Radio (“DMR”), Grid 
Modernization Projects, and the Router Replacement Program. Tr. 1319-1322; 1331.  Staff’s 
proposed reductions to these projects suffer from the same fatal flaws as Staff’s earlier treatment 
of EMS and EDP projects discussed above and should be rejected.    
Moreover, Staff’s review by budget category alone misses critical safety and reliability 
concerns that could result from Staff’s proposed reductions in the key area of Communications.  
The Company is proposing to replace its aging VHF legacy low band analog system, the 
Land Mobile Radio (“LMR”), with Digital Mobile Radio (“DMR”).  Tr. 1319.  The existing 
system suffers from a long litany of ills.  It is becoming more difficult to maintain with limited 
radio models available, has extensive lead time on repairs, point to point only capability, poor 
audio quality, poor coverage, lack of management software, radio sites served by dedicated 


--- Page 149 ---
 
141 
 
leased land lines soon to be discontinued, and difficulty in maintenance due to the lack of 
technicians familiar with the old technology.  Tr. 1319.   
Staff conceded that the LMR system is “nearing its obsolescence state and needs to be 
replaced.”  Tr. 3860, lines 7-10.  Staff acknowledged on cross-examination the fact that the 
Company’s ability to maintain mobile communications with its line workers and other 
employees, particularly during storm conditions, supports safety and reliability.  Tr. 3860.  
Similarly, the Company also proposes backhaul optical fiber ($1.9 million in the Rate 
Year) in those areas where reinforcement of the Company’s communications network is needed 
to provide redundant communications paths, increase system reliability or to expand the network 
to reach new substations, regulator stations or gateway locations.  Tr. 1320-1321.  These 
important Communication projects are also included in the Communications budget.  
Interestingly, Staff acknowledges that it made its reductions to the Communications 
budget without doing any review on a project-by-project basis of costs necessary to complete the 
Land Mobile Radio Replacement with Digital Mobile Radio project or any analysis of the need 
for the back haul fiber optic cable.  Tr. 3861.  Staff is clear that it proposed cuts to the 
Communications Category without any analysis of the merits of the individual projects in the 
category.  Tr. 3861.    
Staff’s proposed downward adjustment to the Company’s Communications capital 
budget is based on a flawed methodology and ignores the critical importance of communications 
to operating a safe and reliable utility system in today’s environment.  See Tr. 1319-1322, 1331, 
3860-3861.  Accordingly, Staff’s proposed adjustment to the Communications budget should be 
rejected.  


--- Page 150 ---

E. 
Net Plant Reconciliation Mechanism 
The Company’s expiring three-year rate plan included a net plant reconciliation 
mechanism that has become commonplace in multi-year rate plans.  The mechanism was a one-
way reconciliation mechanism that protected customers over the duration of the multi-year plan 
if plant additions were less than forecasted (but provided no similar protection for the Company 
if the net plant additions exceeded projection).  This, however, is not a multi-year rate plan; it is 
a one-year litigated case.  
Staff Witness Huang, however, argues in his depreciation testimony that a new deferral 
must be implemented, with the same provisions as in the 2021 Rate Plan, but with new net plant 
and depreciation expense targets established in these rate cases.  Tr. 2292.  Mr. Huang 
maintained that “[t]his deferral will protect ratepayers from overcollection if the Company does 
not spend the entirety of its approved capital expenditure budget.”  Id. at lines 18-21.  Other than 
the fact that the net plant reconciliation was in the Company’s prior multi-year rate plan, Mr. 
Huang offers no compelling reason why such a mechanism should be continued.  Mr. Huang’s 
proposal suffers from two significant infirmities. 
First, as noted, Staff offers no evidence that would warrant the continuation of such a 
plan in a one-year litigated case. 
Second, the adoption of such a one-way reconciliation clause, especially without any 
record of the Company’s deliberate underspending capital, is not only unwise - it is an unlawful 
exercise of retroactive ratemaking that has long been held to be beyond the Commission’s 
powers.74  Such a one-way adjustment that operates after a rate case has been decided is clearly 
violative of that rule.   
 
74  See Mtr. of National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation v. Public Serv. Comm’n., 97 A.D.2d 674 (3d Dep’t 
1983) (citing Mtr. of Niagara Mohawk Power Corp. v Public Serv. Comm’n., 54 A.D.2d 255 (3d Dep’t 1976)).   


--- Page 151 ---
 
143 
 
For these reasons, Staff’s proposed one-way net plant reconciliation mechanism should 
be rejected.

V. 
CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND SUSTAINABILITY INITIATIVES 
A. 
Disadvantaged Communities 
As discussed above, the Company’s rate filing is consistent with Section 7(3) of the 
CLCPA and provides significant benefits to disadvantaged communities.  PULP expresses 
concern that the Company has not adequately identified the disadvantaged communities in its 
service territory because the Company does not maintain data by census tract.  Tr. 628-629.  
While the Company does not track customers based on census tract, the Company has identified 
the disadvantaged communities in its service territory utilizing the geographic information 
published by the Climate Justice Working Group (“CJWG”), which allows the Company to 
identify customers, projects, and infrastructure located within the specific census tracts identified 
as disadvantaged communities.  Tr. 2829-2830.  This information was used to prepare the 
Company’s analysis regarding greenhouse gas emissions reductions in disadvantaged 
communities, as well as to identify which of the Company’s projects are located in 
disadvantaged communities.  Id.         
B. 
Gas Initiatives 
1. 
Responsibly Sourced Gas 
The Company proposed to procure responsibly sourced gas (“RSG”) when the price of 
RSG is higher than the annual weighted average cost of traditional natural gas, up to an annual 
incremental cap of $200,000.  Tr. 1067-1068.  RSG is natural gas obtained from suppliers that 
proactively manage their methane emissions through an independent third-party measurement 


--- Page 153 ---
 
145 
 
and certification to attest that the gas was produced under specified best practices for methane 
mitigation, as well as best practices for other vital environmental categories like water use, land 
use or community engagement.  Tr. 2800-2801.  The Company has determined from a recent 
pilot project that the incorporation of RSG into its natural gas supply portfolio can result in GHG 
emissions reductions,75 with an estimated 71% of those reductions accruing to disadvantaged 
communities.  Exhibit 97 (CLSP-1); Exhibit 98 (CLSP-2).  Given that this proposal would 
reduce GHG emissions, with most of that reduction accruing to disadvantaged communities, the 
Company’s proposed purchase of RSG is consistent with the CLCPA.  Further, the Company’s 
proposal is consistent with proposals approved by the Commission for other utilities in the 
state.76 
 
Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal, as the exposure to customers would be no 
more than $200,000 and RSG can be a means to decrease GHG emissions.  Tr. 3666-3667.  Staff 
Witness Riebel provided that the purchase of RSG “is a way for a gas utility to help reduce the 
climate impact of its energy choices by reducing measurable emissions while providing 
purchasers with proof that they are making impactful changes to its emission reduction goals.”  
Tr. 3665, lines 11-16.  Dutchess County and CLP oppose the Company’s proposal.  Dutchess 
County argues that the Company’s proposal goes against Commission precedent because it 
would allow the Company to purchase RSG even when not the lowest base supply option.  Tr. 
 
75  By comparing the methane intensity of the RSG with the average methane intensity of traditional natural gas, 
the Company can calculate the CO2e abated.  Tr. 2803. 
76  Cases 22-G-0065 et al. -  Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and

Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plans with Additional Requirements at 134 (Jul. 20, 
2023) (“Con Edison Rate Order”); NYSEG-RG&E Rate Order at 70; Cases 21-G-0073 - Proceeding on Motion 
of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. for 
Gas Service, Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plans, with 
Additional Requirements at 117-118 (Apr. 14, 2022) (“O&R Rate Order”). 


--- Page 154 ---
 
146 
 
449.  Dutchess County appears unfamiliar with the recent Commission precedent regarding RSG.  
As discussed above, the Commission has approved a number of similar programs for other 
utilities in New York and has found the programs consistent with the CLCPA and in the public 
interest. 
 
CLP expressed a number of concerns regarding the Company’s proposal to procure RSG.  
CLP’s primary concern appears to be that there is no regulatory body that oversees the 
certification process.  Tr. 3702, 3712.  As a result, CLP argues that the certification process 
cannot be trusted, particularly as applied to Project Canary,77 which is one of the certifying 
agencies.  Tr. 3712-3713.  CLP relies heavily on an article that asserts that Project Canary has 
failed to detect methane leaks as part of its certification process.  Exhibit 393 (JEDR-02).  
However, the conclusions of this article have been directly challenged by Project Canary, which 
asserts that the article utilized a flawed methodology to declare that Canary S monitoring 
systems (which are not actually designed to detect methane) had failed to identify methane leaks.  
Exhibit 166 (CLSP-2R).  Notably, the article referenced by CLP was not peer reviewed and has 
not been updated to reflect the inaccuracies noted by Project Canary.  Tr. 2835-2836.  Finally, 
CLP argues that the procurement of RSG is not consistent with the GHG emissions reductions 
goals of the CLCPA.  Tr. 3715-3716. 
 
The arguments raised by CLP above have been considered and rejected by the 
Commission in previous utility rate cases.78  It is premature to completely foreclose a potential 
opportunity to reduce GHG emissions from the Company’s gas distribution system.  The CLCPA 
 
77  Importantly, Project Canary is only one of a few certifying entities the Company could potentially purchase 
from.  Tr. 2836, 2925. 
78  See, e.g., Con Edison Rate Order at 129-134; Case 21-G-0073, AGREE Statement in Opposition at 6 (Nov. 19,

--- Page 155 ---
 
147 
 
will require decarbonization of the gas distribution system, and the Company’s RSG proposal 
has the potential to advance the market and inform further development of RSG certification at 
minimal cost to customers.  Tr. 2836.  As the Commission provided in the recent Con Edison 
Rate Order: 
Programs that test potential avenues to reduce GHG emissions, like [RSG], should not be 
ruled out as an interim step in the transition process because it still relies on the use of 
natural gas.  The process of moving the State to a decarbonized gas delivery system is 
just that – a process.  The proposed [pilot] will help determine whether and how [RSG] 
should play a role in that process.79   
 
CLP points to the fact that Colorado declined to include RSG as part of its Clean Heat Plan as 
support for its argument that RSG is not consistent with the CLCPA.  Tr. 3703.  This is not 
relevant to this proceeding.  Central Hudson is regulated by the Commission, and therefore, the 
relevant precedent is the precedent cited above that supports RSG procurement as consistent with 
the GHG emissions reduction goals of the CLCPA and New York.     
 
Because the Company’s RSG proposal will provide greenhouse gas emissions reduction 
benefits at minimal cost to customers, it is consistent with the CLCPA and prior Commission 
precedent and should be adopted. 
2. 
Clean Hydrogen Feasibility Study 
The Company proposed to conduct a Clean Hydrogen Feasibility Study to identify 
portions of its distribution system where hydrogen blending activities could be successful and to 
identify project sites that can utilize hydrogen for both gas heating and industrial process load.  
Tr. 2804.  This study would allow the Company to gain experience with hydrogen technology 
while identifying barriers to implementation, market readiness, and customer interest.  Id.  
Further, the study would identify opportunities for the Company to leverage its existing pipeline 
 
79  Con Edison Rate Order at 134. 


--- Page 156 ---
 
148 
 
infrastructure for GHG emissions reductions, consistent with the CLCPA.  Id.  The Company 
requested recovery of $250,000 in the Rate Year to complete the study.  Tr. 2854.   
Staff agreed with the Company’s proposal as studying hydrogen “to support the State’s 
climate goals is commendable and could allow the Company to make progress towards those 
goals.”  Tr. 3669, lines 12-16.  Staff Witness Riebel recommended that the study be specific to 
the Company’s service territory, and the Company agrees with this recommendation.  Tr. 3669, 
2837.  Dutchess County and Assemblymember Sarahana Shrestha oppose the Company’s 
proposal.  According to Dutchess County, it is premature to expend customer resources on this 
study because: 1) the feasibility of the study leading to the construction of a hydrogen production 
facility in the rate plan is slim to none; and 2) hydrogen requires further study before a hydrogen 
production facility can be sited.  Tr. 449.  The Company struggles to understand this argument.  
The Company’s proposal will allow the Company to study whether the use of hydrogen could be 
successful on the Company’s system, particularly as it relates to industrial customers.  Tr. 2839.  
Further, the CLCPA requires significant emissions reductions by 2030 and 2050 – the Company 
believes it is essential to investigate potential avenues to decarbonization as soon as possible.        
 
Assemblymember Shrestha opposes the Company’s proposal to study hydrogen, as the 
study “goes against the spirit of the CLCPA” (Tr. 75, line 7) because it: 1) prioritizes a short-
term minimal emissions reduction goal at the cost of the more ambitious 2030 and 2040 
emission-free electricity goals; 2) does not involve a phase-out of gas infrastructure and therefore 
does not demonstrate urgency towards the 2050 goal; and 3) seeks to invest in an unvetted 
technology that has known problems and can exacerbate inequity.  Tr. 74-75.  The Company will 
address these arguments in turn.   


--- Page 157 ---
 
149 
 
First, the Company believes that the achievement of the CLCPA emissions reduction 
goals will require decarbonization of the Company’s gas distribution system, and the intent of 
the study is to explore a potential avenue to decarbonization.  Assemblymember Shrestha 
provides no support for her argument that Central Hudson’s study would somehow detract from 
the CLCPA’s clean energy goals.  Further, Assemblymember Shrestha’s arguments regarding the 
total phase-out of gas infrastructure by 2050 are speculative and not supported by Commission 
precedent.  As the Commission recently stated, “[t]he role of hydrogen in decarbonizing the 
natural gas system is not clear at present.”80  The purpose of the Company’s proposal is to 
determine the extent to which hydrogen can be used to reduce GHG emissions going forward.81  
Finally, the Company’s study will be conducted with an intent to understand any potential 
emissions concerns related to NOx emissions, as well as impacts on disadvantaged 
communities.82  Tr. 2839-2840. 
 
The Company’s Clean Hydrogen Feasibility Study should be approved because it will 
allow the Company to investigate a potential avenue for decarbonization of the natural gas 
distribution system.  Further, this proposal is consistent with the Climate Action Council’s Final 
Scoping Plan and other efforts being taken in the state.83

Onsite Solar 
The Company proposed to install solar generation infrastructure at certain Company-

generation infrastructure to offset its own use, which would reduce costs to customers and 
provide GHG emissions reduction benefits.  Tr. 2813; 2843.  The Company proposed a four-year 
capital expenditure amount of approximately $2.861 million, with $30,000 proposed for 2024 
and $180,000 for 2025.89  In order to arrive at these costs, the Company utilized vendor estimates 
and added internal Company labor for project management, overheads, AFUDC and 
contingency, as required to properly fund these projects.  Tr. 126. 
 
88  The Company notes that the total cost of the feasibility study is $200,000.

--- Page 163 ---

Staff generally supported the Company’s proposal but recommended a reduction of 
approximately $590,000 to the four-year capital expenditure amount, which resulted in 
reductions of approximately $7,000 in 2024 and $37,000 in 2025.  Exhibit 311 (SCP-2).  In 
arriving at its proposed capital expenditures, Staff relied solely on the vendor estimates received 
by the Company.  Tr. 3829.  However, the vendor estimates only provide the vendor-generated 
portion of the costs, and therefore do not represent the total necessary costs.  Tr. 126.  In order to 
properly fund these projects, costs for internal Company labor, overheads, AFUDC and 
contingency are necessary.  Id.  Because Staff’s proposed capital expenditures are not inclusive 
of all costs necessary to fund the Company’s projects, Staff’s reduction should be rejected.

finds this recommendation unnecessary.  The Company’s proposal seeks to install modestly 
sized solar generation at its facilities at a modest cost to customers.  Tr. 2843.  Incorporating a 
third-party like NYPA adds administrative complexity to the Company’s proposal, and 
Assemblymember Shrestha has not sufficiently demonstrated why the involvement of NYPA is 
necessary.  Dutchess County opposes the proposal allegedly because it increases costs without 
tangible customer benefits.  Tr. 453.  This characterization is inconsistent with the record in this 
proceeding.  The Company’s proposal to install solar generation on its facilities will provide 
tangible benefits to customers, as it will reduce the Company’s energy costs and result in GHG 
emissions reduction benefits, which is consistent with the state’s clean energy goals.  Exhibit 97 
(CLSP-1); Exhibit 98 (CLSP-2).

G. 
CLCPA Deferral Mechanism 
The Company seeks authorization to defer the revenue requirement effect of any new 
legislation, Commission regulations or Commission orders that require the Company to make 
incremental investments in the Rate Year.  Tr. 2788-2789.  It is possible that to meet the GHG 
emissions reduction and clean energy goals of the CLCPA, new legislation or regulations will 
require accelerated development of projects and programs beyond what is proposed by the 
Company in this proceeding.  Tr. 2789.  The costs of any such new requirements are unknown 
and uncertain at this time and are outside the control of the Company.  Id.  The authority to defer 
these costs will ensure that necessary funding is available to fully comply with CLCPA-related 
requirements on a timely basis.  Id.   
 
Staff disagrees with the Company’s proposal as: 1) the Company’s Five-Year Capital 
Plan already includes capital cost estimates for the suite of CLCPA Phase 1 projects; 2) the 
Company should be able to reasonably forecast its capital and CLCPA-related costs during the 
Rate Year; and 3) the Company already has the ability to file a deferral petition with the 
Commission.  Tr. 4002-4003.  However, Staff ignores the fact that at any time, a new study, 
project, program, or complex tracking system could be required in relation to achieving CLCPA 
goals, and cost recovery for those requirements is not assured without the Company’s proposed 
deferral.90  Tr. 2846.  As an example, the Company was required to incur costs related to the 
development of its Long-Term Plan, pursuant to the Gas Planning Proceeding.  Tr. 2847.  The 
Company had not forecasted these costs and the Commission did not explicitly allow the 
Company to defer the costs associated with this work for future recovery within the Gas 
 
90  On cross-examination, the Staff Policy Panel refused to entertain the idea that a new law or regulation could 
impose additional requirements on the Company.  Tr. 4026-4028.  This refusal to consider future developments 
highlights the shortsighted nature of Staff’s position.  


--- Page 165 ---
 
157 
 
Planning Proceeding.  Id.  Further, it is not clear whether the Company could have received 
deferral authority through a petition, as the costs associated with the Long-Term Plan 
development did not meet the materiality threshold of the Commission’s three-prong test for 
deferral treatment.  Tr. 2847-2848.  This example highlights the problems with Staff’s position 
and demonstrates why the Company’s proposed CLCPA Deferral is necessary.  The Company’s 
proposal will allow the Company to fully comply with any new CLCPA-related requirements on 
a timely basis, and therefore, the CLCPA Deferral should be adopted.
