VI. 
RATE OF RETURN / FINANCIAL ISSUES 
A. 
Overview94 
1. 
The Company’s Rate of Return Must Be Considered Within Constitutional 
Guidelines and Its Financial Integrity Must Not Be Placed in Jeopardy 
a. Constitutional Standards and Overview 
A critical component of ratemaking – going back almost to the beginning of the 
ratemaking process – is the requirement that the financial integrity of a utility must be 
 
93  See https://www.cenhud.com/en/my-energy/heat-pumps/.  
94  Because it is essential to view the rate of return discussion through the lens of the degradation that Staff’s 
recommendations would have on Central Hudson’s financial integrity and how those recommendations comport 
with Constitutional guidelines, the major subheadings in the Company’s initial brief are off by one compared to 
the uniform table of contents.  


--- Page 167 ---
 
159 
 
maintained so that it can raise necessary capital at a reasonable cost.  The seminal Hope Natural 
Gas case established this almost eighty years ago: 
By that standard, the return to the equity owner should be commensurate with 
returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.  That 
return, moreover, should be sufficient to assure confidence in the financial 
integrity of the enterprise, so as to maintain its credit and to attract capital.95  
 
Indeed, Staff’s own witness defined such “reasonable terms in this situation as being able to 
attract capital at a rate that is comparable to other utilities across the country and within New 
York State.”  Tr. 2483, lines 17-21.  Yet, by its own terms, Staff’s recommendations in this case 
would not pass that test.  One measure of a utility’s financial integrity is its bond rating.  Staff 
concedes, however, that rating agency credit metrics produced by its 9.2% rate of return on 
equity and 48% equity ratio, along with other elements of Staff’s case, would fall significantly 
below the credit metrics floor at which the credit rating agencies would downgrade the 
Company’s credit rating.  Were that to occur, Central Hudson’s credit rating would fall below 
that of its peers, it would not be able to attract capital at a rate comparable to its higher rated peer 
utilities, and by Staff’s own admission the reasonableness test of Staff’s case would fail.  Tr. 
2484.  Moreover, such a result would occur even before the potential reduction arising from 
millions of dollars of NRAs and penalties that Staff is proposing, which are not faced by other 
utilities, either in New York or nationwide, and which measure Central Hudson only against its 
own typically positive past results.  As will be demonstrated below, the ROE and equity ratios 
advanced by Staff are well below the national average and the Company’s credit rating today is 
already below the minimum credit rating found by the Commission to be in the best interest of 
the Company and its customers.  Should the Company’s current credit rating be further reduced 
 
95  Fed. Power Com. v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591, 603 (1944) (citing Missouri ex rel. Southwestern Bell 
Tel. Co. v. Public Service Commission, 262 U. S. 276, 291 (Brandeis concurring)).  


--- Page 168 ---
 
160 
 
as a result of Staff’s recommendations in this case, it will have long term consequences for 
Central Hudson and its customers for years to come.  
b. The Commission’s Conclusion that an Optimal Bond Rating for a 
Utility is “A” Has Not Been Changed or Revised 
Even now, the credit rating held by the Company, “Baa1” and “BBB+” by Moody’s and 
Standard & Poor’s (“S&P”), respectively, stand one notch below the “A” credit rating found 
optimal by this Commission.  In the Generic Finance Proceeding (“GFP”)96 that informs many of 
the rate of return methodologies and policies the Commission follows today, however, the 
desirability of New York utilities maintaining an “A” bond rating was affirmed in the Generic 
Finance RD:  
All parties in the Electric/Gas Group, including CPB, Multiple Intervenors, and 
IPPNY, signed a Financial Integrity Agreement.  The Water Industry Group 
adopted this agreement as its own as well.  That agreement essentially readopts 
the Commission's 1982 conclusion that an “A” bond rating is a proper, 
supportable goal.  It further incorporates a preference stated subsequently by the 
Commission in a number of cases, that the primary means of achieving higher 
bond ratings should be by the issuance of equity.  The agreement states that 
regulatory support for high bond ratings would continue to be dependent on its 
rate impacts, and it concludes that utilities with bond ratings greater than “A” 
should be required periodically to demonstrate that their particular financial 
strategy is not harmful to ratepayers.97 
 
The Generic Finance RD concluded: “We concur with the parties who recommend reaffirming 
the Commission's earlier conclusions with respect to an ‘A’ rating for electric utilities and 
extending these conclusions to the other industry groups.”98    
 
96  Case 91-M-0509 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Consider Financial and Regulatory Policies for 
New York State Utilities, Recommended Decision (July 19, 1994), 1994 N.Y. PUC LEXIS 141, *118-119 (the 
“Generic Finance RD”). 
97  Generic Finance RD, 1994 N.Y. PUC LEXIS 141, *120-121.    
98  Id. at 122.  The wisdom of having New York’s utilities maintain an “A” bond rating goes back beyond even the 
GFP.  In a 1982 proceeding which examined financial integrity for electric companies, the Commission 
concluded that an “A” bond rating was a cost-effective financial goal; that, barring unacceptable rate impacts, 
utilities should be provided with rate support for that bond rating; and that the Commission would not interfere 
 


--- Page 169 ---
 
161 
 
 
The support for an “A” bond rating does not end with the Generic Finance RD in 1994.  
In 2011, the Commission found:   
Our actions in this rate proceeding fully support this corporate achievement and 
we fully support movement and growth in Niagara Mohawk's equity position to 
the range recently achieved that supports its "A-" rating.  Specifically, the 
introduction of an RDM for the Company's electric operations represents a 
significant risk reduction, meaning a smaller equity cushion can be used.99 
 
In 2013, as a condition of the Joint Proposal approved by the Commission in Case 12-M-0192, 
the following condition was specifically designed and adopted to protect and monitor the 
Company’s credit quality: “Central Hudson and Fortis will continue to support the objective of 
maintaining an “A” credit rating for Central Hudson.”  Tr. 946, lines 10-11.  And, in 2016 the 
Commission again reaffirmed the desirability of an “A” bond rating, finding: 
In addition, we concur that the equity ratio should be constrained to 48%, since 
this equity level should maintain an "A" bond rating for the Companies.  We 
generally support rate levels designed to maintain an investment grade bond 
rating where practicable.100 
 
c. The Company’s Existing Bond Rating, However, Falls Short of that 
“A” Rating Goal and, Worse, is in Jeopardy of Further Downgrading 
As shown in Exhibit 33 (FP-1), Schedule B, Central Hudson’s senior unsecured debt 
ratings reflected “A” or the equivalent by two or three of the rating agencies in most of the past 
15 years.  In 2015, 2016, and 2019, Fitch, S&P, and Moody’s, respectively, downgraded the 
Company’s senior unsecured debt to the low end of the “A” range.  In 2021 and 2022, however, 
Moody’s and S&P both downgraded Central Hudson’s senior unsecured debt to “Baa1” and 
 
with the pursuit of higher bond ratings by utilities if it were not harmful to ratepayers.  Case 27679, Financing 
Plans for Major New York Gas and Electric Companies, Opinion No. 82-83 (issued October 18, 1982).

“BBB+” respectively, and in early 2023 Fitch changed its outlook to negative signaling a 
potential downgrade from its current “A-” rating, stating: “[t]he Negative Rating Outlook reflects 
Fitch’s concerns that, absent a significantly improved outcome in the Company’s next rate case,

Although a Moody’s Credit Opinion dated May 16, 2023 (Exhibit 266 (ASH-7)) viewed 
Central Hudson’s current Baa1 bond rating as stable, Moody’s both anticipated the Company’s 
filing of this rate case and listed conditions that could warrant a ratings change.  First, Moody’s 
noted: 
The primary credit challenges at this time are pressure on financial metrics 
resulting from the last rate settlement in 2021 that continues to contribute to 
growth in regulatory assets combined with a reduction in regulatory liabilities and 
a reduction in equity capital from 50% to 48% over the next 3 years, at a time 
when the utility is in the midst of a large ongoing capital program.  In 
combination, we expect these factors to lead to cash flow from operations pre-
working capital (CFO pre-WC) to debt ratio in the range of 13-16% in the next 
few years, which position it weakly at its current Baa1 rating level. 
 
Exhibit 266 (ASH-7), page 1.  Second, Moody’s warned: 
A key driver of CHG&E's historical credit quality has been the regulatory support 
that the company has received from the NYPSC, although this support has 
declined in recent years.  The utility's most recent rate case order, issued on 18 
November 2021, continues to contribute to the forecast weakness in financial 
metrics with a growth of regulatory assets combined with a reduction in 
regulatory liabilities. 
 
Id. at 3.  Moody’s then further warned that: “[t]he stable outlook is based on Moody's 
expectation that CFO pre-WC to debt will be sustained in the range of 13-16% over the next 


--- Page 171 ---
 
163 
 
few years,” that an upgrade might occur if CFO Pre-WC to debt were sustained above 18%, and 
that a downgrade would result if CFO Pre-WC to debt is sustained below 14% and/or the New 
York regulatory and political environment were to become “incrementally more challenging or 
there is a further deterioration in the stability and predictability of regulatory decisions.”  Id. at 2 
(emphasis supplied).101  Lest there be any doubt – Moody’s has made it abundantly clear that if 
this rate decision produces sustained CFO Pre-WC to debt levels below 14% and/or adverse 
regulatory treatment, a credit downgrade looms as a very likely outcome.  As we will 
demonstrate, Staff’s proposals, therefore, must be viewed with a jaundiced eye because, if 
implemented, they would cause a credit quality deterioration, jeopardize the Company’s credit 
ratings and financial integrity and would, thus, transgress the Constitutional ratemaking 
protections afforded by Hope Natural Gas.   
d. Staff’s ROE and Capital Structure Along with Other 
Recommendations Will Produce Financial Metrics Consistent With a 
Downgrade and Will Degrade Central Hudson’s Financial Integrity 
Before even discussing the appropriate capital structure and rate of ROE to be adopted in 
this case, therefore, it is essential to view the rate of return discussion through the lens of the 
degradation that Staff’s recommendations would have on Central Hudson’s financial integrity 
and how those recommendations heavily transgress the financial integrity test set forth in the 
Hope Natural Gas case.  By his own admission, Staff Witness Hale’s recommendation of a ROE 
of 9.2% on a capital structure with a 48% equity ratio will produce a CFO Pre-WC to debt metric 
of just 9.3%.  Exhibit 272 (ASH-13).  Given that a CFO Pre-WC to debt metric of 9.3% is 
nowhere close to Moody’s required metric of 14% to retain the Company’s existing bond rating, 
 
101  The mere fact that this case is a one year litigated matter, rather than a three-year settled rate plan, indicates a

B. 
Capital Structure 
1. 
A 50% Equity Ratio is Necessary to Support the Company’s Credit 
a. Current Conditions Require a Thicker Equity Ratio 
In the past two years, energy prices have increased by 132% and based on market 
forecasts, are expected to remain above historic levels for the foreseeable future.  The supply 
cost increases negatively impact cash flows as the Company pays for supply in advance of the 
collection of receipts from its customers.  Tr. 933.  Storm costs also adversely affect cash flow as 
recovery has historically lagged because expenditures have significantly exceeded the rate 
allowance.  Tr. 934.  Furthermore, arrears balances have grown significantly since the Company 
suspended collections activity for all customers in March of 2020 and in accordance with

Given these cash needs, financial ratios are the key determinants used in the quantitative 
assessment by credit ratings agencies.  Tr. 957.  All three major credit rating agencies are 
intensely focused on cash flow to total debt, and debt to earnings before interest, taxes, 
depreciation, and amortization.  Id.  The common theme in the quantitative analyses employed 
by the credit rating agencies when determining bond ratings is the level of debt leverage at the 
subject utility and the amount of debt leverage included in the ratemaking capital structure.  Tr. 
958.  This is because a Company’s equity ratio affects both operating income and debt leverage.

an increase in the ratemaking equity ratio.  Tr. 959.  As Mr. Hale agreed, having a greater 
percentage of capitalization financed by long-term debt produces more financial risk.  Tr. 2485-
2486. 
In Central Hudson’s 2017 rate case, the equity ratio was increased from 48% to 50%, 
which partially mitigated the impact of the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”).  That equity 
ratio, however, was changed in the 2021 Rate Plan from 50% back down to 48%.  Tr. 959.  Mr. 
Hale’s suggestion that the Company is experiencing diminishing impact from the TCJA (Tr.

cited by the credit rating agencies (such as a sizeable capital expenditure forecast) the primary 
pressure remains the TCJA’s impact of permanently reducing the cash flow associated with 
capital expenditures and the impact on the Company’s financial integrity from the TCJA has not

without even considering the negative impact of the potential imposition of millions of dollars in

b. Staff’s Arguments in Favor of a 48% Equity Ratio are Unavailing and

Staff Witness Hale conceded that a stand-alone capital structure is appropriate on which 
to set rates for Central Hudson.  Tr. 2353.  He admitted that the Company’s equity ratio as of

equity ratio, arguing that: (1) all other major electric and gas utilities in New York have managed 
to continue to maintain access to reasonably priced capital with a 48.0% common equity ratio; 
(2) a 50.0% common equity ratio is not necessary to maintain the Company’s current bond 
ratings; and (3) even with an allowed 50.0% equity ratio in Rate Year One of its current rate 
plan, the Company was still downgraded by both Moody’s and S&P.  Tr. 2360.     


--- Page 177 ---
 
169 
 
 
As supposed support for his argument that a thicker equity ratio is unnecessary, Mr. Hale 
contends that despite having a 48.0% common equity ratio during the financial crisis of 2007-
2008, Central Hudson was still able maintain sufficient access to capital.  Tr. 2363-2364.  This 
claim is, of course, beside the point because a company’s creditworthiness is extremely 
important during a financial crisis, and Central Hudson’s stronger creditworthiness as an “A”- 
rated company during the 2007-2008 financial crisis was integral to its ability to access capital.  
Tr. 984.  As explained above, Central Hudson’s creditworthiness has deteriorated since that time.  
As far as Mr. Hale’s contention that all other gas and electric distribution companies have been 
authorized 48% equity ratios, that fact is irrelevant when Central Hudson is facing different 
circumstances, including the clear likelihood of a downgrade if Staff’s case were to be endorsed 
in whole or in large part.  Finally, as far as his contention that a thicker equity ratio is 
unnecessary to maintain the Company’s bond rating (Tr. 2364), a 48% common equity ratio is 
one of the contributing factors to Mr. Hale’s calculation of Moody’s CFO Pre-WC to debt metric 
of 9.3% (Tr. 985), which is far below Moody’s floor metric of 14% necessary to maintain the 
current bond rating.  Mr. Hale acknowledged that his stated belief that a change in the 
Company’s 48% equity ratio is not necessary to avoid a downgrade did not contemplate whether 
there would be a need for a 50% equity ratio if Central Hudson was seeking to improve its 
current bond rating.  Tr. 2487.

operating company level data, rather than holding company data, to determine the group’s 
common equity ratio, alleging that holding company data results in a mean common equity ratio 
of 44.10%.  Tr. 2365.  Mr. Hale’s analysis of holding company capital structures includes

170 
 
utilities.  The relevant capital structure for comparison purposes is at the operating company

Mr. Hale further contends that, although it was reasonable to increase the equity ratio in

not diminished.  Tr. 959.  More important, however, if as Mr. Hale concedes, “other, more 
significant factors” have replaced the TCJA, then there is even more of a reason to increase the 
equity ratio now, not less.    
 
Finally, Mr. Hale’s claim that the effect of a downgrade would be less than the cost of 
increasing the equity ratio is without basis.  He asserts that a downgrade would only raise the 
cost of debt by 10 BPs, which he argues is approximately $2 million less than the cost of 
increasing the equity ratio.  Tr. 2389-2390.  First, Mr. Hale ignores both recent spread analyses 
and the increasing interest rates that are required in times of financial distress.  The Company’s 
Finance Panel noted that during the 2008 financial crisis, Central Hudson, then rated “A2” by 


--- Page 179 ---
 
171 
 
Moody’s and “A” by S&P, was able to issues notes at a spread advantage of 220 BPs over 
Sempra, which was then rated at “Baa1” and “BBB+”.  Tr. 943-944.  Moreover, when Central 
Hudson went to market in March 2023 for $90 million of long-term debt, the maturities priced 
with spreads of 170, 180 and 190 BPs, respectively.  In contrast, in 2018 when the Company was 
rated the equivalent of “A-”, it issued 15-year bonds with a spread of 105 BPs.  The higher 
spread which is directly correlated to the Company’s credit rating causes millions of dollars in 
additional interest costs that  then raise costs to customers now and well into the future.  Tr. 945.  
Finally, Mr. Hale willfully turns a blind eye to the fact that, once downgraded, a prolonged 
period of much higher financial metrics is required for a company to reattain the lost higher bond 
rating.  And during that time, every single debt issuance carries with it higher interest costs.  A 
modest increase to the Company’s equity ratio is a small price to pay for the assurance that 
access to reasonably priced capital is not squandered needlessly.   
C. 
Long-Term Debt 
1. 
The Company’s Proposed Debt Costs are the Most Reasonable Measure of 
the Cost of Future Debt Financing 
The Company’s Finance Panel provided an updated forecast for the embedded cost of 
long-term debt, which reflected debt that the Company issued after the Panel filed its direct 
testimony and the release of the latest interest rate forecast from Blue Chip Financial Forecast.  
The updated cost of long-term debt is adjusted to be 4.50%, as shown in Exhibit 203 (FP-3R) 
and does not include the impacts of a credit rating downgrade.  Tr. 1002.    
In contrast, Staff Witness Hale believes that current U.S. Treasury yields should be used 
to estimate future debt issuance costs.  Tr. 2398.  Although the Company realizes that the 
Commission has generally agreed with Mr. Hale and used the most recent interest rates, the 
economic uncertainty described in Mr. Nowak’s testimony (Tr. 222-224), renders Mr. Hale’s 


--- Page 180 ---
 
172 
 
spot interest rates no more likely to be accurate than the Company’s forecasts using the Blue 
Chip data. 
2. 
The Best Approach to Market Volatility is to Adopt the Company’s 
Proposed Debt Tracker 
Because the Company has no control over the market and given the recent volatility and 
future uncertainty, an interest rate true-up should be adopted for debt issued during the term of 
the rate plan, using deferral accounting treatment to record the expense impact of favorable or 
unfavorable variations in the weighted average cost of the fixed rate debt shown in the 
Company’s exhibits (or the rate ultimately reflected in delivery rates as a result of these 
proceedings).  Tr. 972.  Staff Witness Hale supported the continuation of a deferral mechanism 
for variable rate debt (Tr. 2404) but opposed the deferral for fixed rate issuances.  Tr. 2405.    
 
Mr. Hale argues that the Company largely has control over when and how often it 
goes to the market and has the ability to structure their fixed rate debt efficiently.  Tr. 2405-2406.  
He further avers that the Commission typically does not approve reconciliation mechanisms for 
fixed rate long-term debt in a one-year proceeding, as it would not provide Central Hudson with 
the proper incentive to minimize issuance rates.  Tr. 2406.  As far as control over issuances, 
because of its relatively small size, Central Hudson is a price-taker as a debt issuer in the 
financial markets.  Its goal is to manage volatility, minimize financing risk, and achieve a 
reasonable cost of long-term debt over long periods of time by issuing mostly fixed rate debt, 
managing its maturity schedule, and issuing long-dated maturities when market interest rates are 
low and issuing shorter maturities when market interest rates are high.  Tr. 972.  The Company 
can do this most effectively when there is no interest expense impact driven by differences in 
actual interest rates at the time of issuance compared to those assumed in delivery rates.  Id.  
Because the Company has no control over the market (Id.), Mr. Hale’s incentive-based argument 


--- Page 181 ---
 
173 
 
is misplaced.  Moreover, his opposition to an interest rate deferral may well end up harming 
customers.  The latest Blue Chip Financial Forecasts project a decline in interest rates for 2024 
and 2025.  Tr. 1003.  If interest rates fall, as predicted by the Blue Chip Financial Forecasts, 
customers will pay more for the cost of debt in rates under Mr. Hale’s position than they would 
have if there were a two-way symmetrical fixed rate debt cost deferral mechanism in effect. 
D. 
Cost of Common Equity 
1. 
The Company’s Requested ROE of 9.8% is Conservative and Well 
Supported by Current Market Conditions 
a. Summary of Mr. Nowak’s ROE of 9.8% 
The Company’s Rate of Return expert, Mr. Nowak, developed his 9.8% ROE 
recommendation using a multi-stage Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model and two forms of 
the Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”) applied to a proxy group of companies.  Tr. 143.  He 
has presented the DCF and CAPM results weighted equally, and with the Commission’s 
conventional 2/3 weighting of the DCF and 1/3 weighting of the CAPM.  Id.  The use of a multi-
stage DCF model and two forms of the CAPM is consistent with the approach employed by the 
Commission in prior cases.  Id.  Although an equal weighting of the DCF and CAPM results 
does not conform to the weighting typically employed by the Commission in the past, Mr. 
Nowak explained why placing less emphasis on the DCF model at this time is reasonable and 
consistent with the goals of the Generic Finance RD issued in the GFP.

Based on the quantitative and qualitative analyses and the weighting of the DCF and 
CAPM results, as well as his assessment of the business and financial risk environment of 
Central Hudson relative to the proxy group, Mr. Nowak concluded that the appropriate ROE for 
the Company is within the range of 9.80% and 10.80%.  The Company, however, has 
constrained it requested ROE to 9.80%, which is at the low end of the range of reasonableness 
and is a conservative estimate of the investor-required ROE in order to mitigate the effect on 
rates.  Tr. 145. 
b. Mr. Nowak’s ROE Recommendation Employs Current Market 
Conditions  
In response to the economic effects of COVID-19, the Federal Reserve decreased the 
federal funds rate twice in March 2020, resulting in a target range of 0.00% to 0.25% (which 
remained in effect until March 2022), and increased its holdings of both Treasury and mortgage-
backed securities, and supported increased credit to both businesses and individual borrowers.  
Tr. 167-168.  Starting with the initial stages of the COVID-19 Pandemic, however, inflation 
levels have been driven higher, reaching 9.1% in June of 2022.  As a result of these substantially 
higher inflation rates, the Federal Reserve increased the federal funds rate seven times in 2022, 
and another three times in 2023.  Tr. 170.  The current capital market conditions are heavily 
influenced by monetary policy aimed at mitigating inflationary pressures.  This has caused both 


--- Page 183 ---
 
175 
 
short-term and long-term interest rates to increase substantially since the historical lows of 2020, 
suggesting a higher cost of capital including the ROE.  Therefore, it is important that these 
factors be accounted for in the cost of equity models and the appropriate weight be given to the 
approaches that best account for the current dynamics of the capital market environment.  Tr. 
174. 
 
The primary area of disagreement between Mr. Nowak and Staff Witness Hale 
is that Staff’s approach mechanically develops the assumptions used in its ROE formula without 
considering the effect of market conditions on the models, or the current financial profile 
of the Company.  As will be discussed, the results of Staff’s model and ultimate 
recommendations are not consistent with recently authorized returns in any other jurisdiction, 
especially when considered in the context of the capital market conditions in which the other 
returns were authorized.  Tr. 225. 
c. Mr. Nowak’s Proxy Group Best Captures Central Hudson’s Risk 
Both Mr. Nowak and Mr. Hale developed proxy groups of companies to run their ROE 
models.  Mr. Nowak began his proxy group selection with the companies that Value Line 
classifies as “Electric Utilities,” which includes electric-only and combination utilities.  Because 
the Company’s operations include gas and electric utility operations, he also included companies 
that Value Line classifies as “Natural Gas Distribution Companies.”  Tr. 176.  The proxy group 
is used to inform the ROE for both the DCF and CAPM analyses.  Tr. 2407.   
Mr. Hale’s proxy group was composed only of a group of 26 utility holding companies 
which are classified by Value Line as “electric utilities.” (Tr. 2409), some of which still own 
significant generation assets.  Tr. 229.  The risks faced by electric companies that own significant 


--- Page 184 ---
 
176 
 
generation differ from those that do not.  Id.107  Because Central Hudson is a combined electric 
and natural gas utility, it is entirely appropriate to include both electric-only utilities and natural 
gas-only utilities to reflect the risk of the combined electric and natural gas utility operations of 
Central Hudson in the proxy group.  Id.  Although the results of the DCF and CAPM analyses do 
not differ appreciably between the two competing proxy groups (Tr. 230), Mr. Nowak’s proxy 
group is clearly more reflective of Central Hudson’s business and risks than is Staff’s.  
d. DCF Methodology 
The DCF approach is based on the theory that a stock’s current market price represents 
the present value of all expected future cash flows.  Tr. 182.  The constant growth DCF model 
assumes the expected growth rate will be constant in perpetuity.  The multi-stage forms of the 
DCF model, including both the two-stage model upon which the Commission has relied and the 
multi-stage form of the model used by Mr. Nowak recognize short and long-term growth 
prospects.  Tr. 184.  Mr. Nowak used current market data and earnings growth rates to calculate 
his DCF results.  Tr. 186-190.  As shown in Exhibit 134 (JCN-1), the multi-stage DCF analysis 
based on a three-month average stock price and a range of near-term growth rate assumptions 
produces an updated mean ROE of 10.34%.  Exhibit 230 (JCN-1R), Exhibit 232 (JCN-3R); Tr. 
220.108 
 
107  See Cases 07-E-1507 et al. - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Establish a Long-Range Electric 
Resource Plan and Infrastructure Planning Process, Order Initiating Electricity Reliability and Infrastructure 
Planning at 23 (Dec. 24, 2007) (“While we recognize the benefit to consumers of transferring all risks to 
generators, if the result is a volatile market that is limited in its ability to attract a variety of merchant 
investments, the public may not be well served.”). 
108  In updating his DCF results, Mr. Nowak maintained all of the assumptions applied in his direct testimony, but

The most significant difference between Mr. Nowak’s DCF analysis and Mr. Hale’s 
approach is that Mr. Nowak uses a consensus of analysts’ earnings per share (“EPS”) growth 
rates for the proxy group companies as the near-term growth rate, and an estimate of growth in 
the overall economy for the long-term growth rate.  Tr. 236.  Mr. Nowak’s approach mitigates the 
uncertainty associated with forecasting individual companies’ growth rates over very long-time 
horizons.  In contrast, Mr. Hale uses dividend growth projections from a single source (i.e., Value 
Line) for the near-term growth rate, and a “sustainable growth rate” derived from the same single 
source for the long-term growth rate.  Id.  Mr. Hale prefers using dividends over earnings, even 
while he explicitly concedes that “earnings drive companies’ dividend payout policies.”  Tr. 
2414, lines 21-22.  As Mr. Nowak points out, the cash flows in the DCF “are quarterly dividend 
payments plus any capital appreciation that occurs between when the stock is purchased and 
when it is sold…. [d]ividend payments and capital appreciation are both a function of [EPS], 
which is ultimately what determines the return to the investor.”109  Tr. 237.  Earnings growth 
estimates, therefore, are clearly superior because a company’s earnings is the primary driver of 
its dividend growth.  Tr. 238.  
 
Mr. Nowak also demonstrated that Mr. Hale’s sole reliance on Value Line’s growth 
estimates for his DCF is infirm because Value Line relies on the views of a single analyst.  Tr. 
239.  Mr. Hale tried to rebut this infirmity by citing to a letter from Value Line.  Exhibit 285 
(ASH-26).  As Mr. Nowak established, all that letter does is to confirm that, although subject to 
review, the estimates are still the estimates of a lone analyst.  Id.  In contrast to Mr. Hale’s 
reliance on one analyst’s views, Mr. Nowak’s growth estimates rely on a number of sources, 
 
109  Investment analysts report predominant reliance on EPS growth projections.  In a survey completed by 297 
members of the Association for Investment Management and Research, the majority of respondents ranked 
earnings as the most important variable in valuing a security (more important than cash flow, dividends, or book 
value).  Tr. 238. 


--- Page 186 ---
 
178 
 
including three-to five-year earnings growth consensus estimates (i.e. averages of multiple, 
independent analysts) from First Call and Zacks, in addition to Value Line.  Tr. 186.  Surely, the 
opinions of several analysts, including Value Line, is superior to Mr. Hale’s sole reliance on a 
single analyst.   
 
For the longer term, Mr. Hale calculated an estimate of the sustainable growth rate for his 
proxy group which he then checked against other macroeconomic forecasts.  Tr. 2421-2422.  Mr. 
Nowak gave several reasons why this approach should not be continued in the DCF model.  
First, although Mr. Hale asserts that his calculation of the sustainable growth rate represents a 
measure of long-term growth for the period 2028 and beyond, the inputs to his calculation are, 
for the most part, shorter-term estimates for the period 2028 and earlier.  Tr. 245.  Mr. Hale’s 
longer term growth rate, however, extends 200 years, with the second stage beginning in year six 
of the analysis period.  Tr. 246.  This means that Mr. Hale is attempting to assess the 
reasonableness of a long-term growth rate using a projection that covers approximately 15.00% 
of the period it is being applied to (i.e., Mr. Hale is assessing a 195-year projection using a 30-
year projection).  Id.  In contrast, Mr. Nowak’s estimate of long-term GDP growth reflects 
projections over 30 years or more and considers overall measures of economic growth.  Tr. 243.  
Second, Mr. Hale’s sustainable growth rate relies on Value Line’s estimate of each proxy 
company’s ROE, as the “r” in the “b * r” component of his growth rate in the expected ROE, 
which introduces an element of circularity into Mr. Hale’s calculation that he fails to reconcile.  
Tr. 243.  In fact, based on Exhibit 280 (ASH-21), the mean and median ROEs assumed in Mr. 
Hale’s DCF calculation are 11.00% and 10.34%, respectively, yet Mr. Hale’s analysis suggests 
the proxy group average ROE is 8.61%.  Id.  This is an irreconcilable conundrum.  


--- Page 187 ---
 
179 
 
 
A final problem with Mr. Hale’s approach is his reliance on the “IRR” Microsoft Excel 
function in which dividends are assumed to be received at the end of the year.  Tr. 248.  On 
cross-examination, Mr. Hale conceded that every company in his proxy group issues quarterly 
dividends - not dividends at year’s end.  Tr. 2508.  Consequently, as Mr. Nowak explained, the 
“XIRR” function which Mr. Nowak used – and not the IRR function used by Mr.  Hale - is more 
reflective of this reality.  Tr. 248.  Exhibit 234 (JCN-5R), shows that, when the more realistic 
XIRR function is used for dividends in the DCF, Mr. Hale’s DCF result increased from 8.61% to 
8.78%, which increases his overall ROE estimate from 9.20% to 9.30%.  Id. 
 
Finally, it should not escape notice that Mr. Hale’s DCF result is only 8.61%.  Tr. 2425.  
Mr. Hale agreed that an ROE as low as an 8.61% ROE, to his awareness, has not been adopted 
by the Commission.  Tr. 2508-2509.  This result is, moreover, over 60 BPs below the ROEs 
currently reflected in other Joint Proposals approved recently by the Commission.  Tr. 236.  More 
will be said later in our discussion about the proper weighting to be afforded the DCF and 
CAPM results, but an average of proxy group returns that is 60 BPs or more below the ROEs 
currently being authorized is hardly likely to be a reliable gauge of the Company’s cost of equity.  
Mr. Nowak’s approach is clearly to be favored as more reliable.  
e. CAPM 
The CAPM is a risk premium approach that estimates the cost of equity for a given 
security as a function of a risk-free return plus a risk premium (to compensate investors for the 
non-diversifiable or “systematic” risk of that security).  Tr. 197.  The Commission also has relied 
upon the Zero-Beta CAPM (the form of which is sometimes referred to as the “Empirical 
CAPM”) in estimating the cost of equity.  Tr. 201.  The Zero-Beta form of the CAPM addresses 
the tendency of the “traditional” CAPM to underestimate the cost of equity for companies with 
lower Beta coefficients such as regulated utilities.  Tr. 202.  In that regard, the Zero-Beta CAPM 


--- Page 188 ---
 
180 
 
is not redundant to the use of adjusted Betas; rather, it recognizes the results of academic 
research indicating that the risk-return relationship is different (in essence, flatter) than estimated 
by the CAPM which underestimates the “alpha,” or the constant return term.  Id.  
Mr. Nowak uses average Beta coefficients for the proxy group companies reported by 
Bloomberg and Value Line.  Tr. 201.  For the market return, he relied on the average of:  1) 
forward-looking required market return for the S&P 500 as calculated using a DCF model for all 
companies in the index; and 2) the estimated forward-looking required market return for the S&P 
500, as provided by Bank of America Merrill Lynch.  Tr. 200.  Mr. Nowak’s updated CAPM 
results using both the Traditional CAPM and Zero-Beta CAPM approaches produce results of a 
mean low CAPM of 9.71%, an overall mean CAPM of 11.06%, and a mean high CAPM of 
12.45%.  Tr. 220.     
Mr. Hale also averaged the results of two forms of the CAPM: the Traditional CAPM and 
the Zero-Beta CAPM.  Tr. 2429.  Mr. Hale uses the mean of Value Line Betas in his calculation.  
Tr.  2432.  He also uses market return estimates published monthly by Bank of America 
Quantitative Profiles.  Tr. 2435-2436.  Mr. Hale then averaged the results of the Traditional and 
Zero-Beta CAPM calculations to arrive at a CAPM result of 10.33%.  Tr. 2441.     
 
The Bloomberg Beta coefficients relied on by Mr. Nowak are comparable to those 
reported by Value Line.  Tr. 253.  Furthermore, it is reasonable to consider several measures of 
market conditions in estimating the ROE.  The Bloomberg Beta coefficient is widely used, and 
because it is based on a ten-year period as compared to Value Line’s five-year period, it 
mitigates the effect of exogenous events on utility Beta coefficients.  Id.   
As regards the market return, the S&P 500 is an index that includes the largest 500 
companies by market capitalization.  Tr. 255.  Over time, the specific companies that are 


--- Page 189 ---
 
181 
 
included in the S&P 500 Index will vary, but investor expectations of growth and return overall 
for the index as a whole may not, based on the selection process involved in the index.  Id.  
Furthermore, S&P regularly publishes earnings growth projections for the S&P 500.  Tr. 256.  As 
of October 31, 2023, S&P projected earnings growth of 12.46% for the S&P 500.  Id.  When 
combined with the twelve-month average dividend yield of 1.67% for the S&P 500 reported by 
S&P, that results in a projected return on the S&P 500 of 14.23%.  Id.  Additionally, as of 
November 29, 2023, State Street projected long-term EPS growth of 13.26% for an exchange-
traded fund tracking the performance of the S&P 500 Index.  Id.  When combined with the 
reported dividend yield of 1.54%, that results in a projected market return of 14.90%.  Id.  By 
contrast, the market return that Mr. Hale relied on is 10.83%, which is more than 300 BPs below 
the market return estimates provided by these reputable sources.  Id.  
 
Notwithstanding the superiority of Mr. Nowak’s sources and calculations, it cannot be 
missed that his mean CAPM result of 11.06% and Mr. Hale’s average CAPM result of 10.33% 
are not terribly far apart.  In fact, Mr. Nowak’s mean DCF result of 10.34%, mean CAPM result 
of 11.06% and Mr. Hale’s CAPM result are all within a 75 basis point band.  Yet Mr. Hale’s 
DCF result is more than 170 BPs below these results – again rendering his DCF results 
questionable. 
f. The DCF Should Not be Given Twice the Weight of the CAPM 
Approach 
Mr. Hale determined his proxy group’s cost of equity by weighting his 8.61% DCF result 
two-thirds and his 10.33% average of two CAPM results one-third.  The resulting ROE is 9.18%, 
which he rounded to 9.20%.  Tr. 2441.  Mr. Hale argues that in a 2017 case the Commission 
stated “We have already affirmed our continued 1/3 CAPM and 2/3 DCF weighting to arrive at 
our ROE results, based on the fact that we view the DCF methodology as superior to the CAPM.  


--- Page 190 ---
 
182 
 
We have explained this preference repeatedly in past orders, and we see no need to deviate from 
the viewpoint at this time.”  Tr. 2443, lines 7-14 (emphasis supplied).110  
The basis for Staff’s two-thirds/one-third weighting stems from the Recommended 
Decision in the Generic Finance proceeding, which was issued 30 years ago.111  At the time, 
there was limited experience with the CAPM and, as the Generic Finance RD recognized, the 
CAPM was then used mostly as a check on the DCF.  Now, three decades later, with long 
experience with the CAPM in the industry and its use by the Commission for all those years, 
there is no reason not to weight equally the two methodologies.   
The GFP was initiated because the Commission recognized that the DCF methodology 
was particularly sensitive to interest rate fluctuations and was producing returns far below the 
returns produced by other methodologies.  Just as was the case in the past, the record evidence 
establishes that in current market conditions, the DCF model still suffers the same infirmities –
producing the same, unrealistically low returns.   
The Generic Finance RD, moreover, manifestly did not establish the 2/3 weighting of the 
DCF for all time.  It stated:  
In either an annual proceeding to determine a rate of return or in individual 
proceedings, the 2/3 DCF and 1/3 CAPM convention should be the presumption, 
but as Multiple Intervenors suggests, parties would not be barred from introducing 
new methods or different weightings.  Such parties, however, would have the 
burden of convincing other parties and the Commission of the relevance or 
superiority of their proposals.112  
 
110   Citing National Fuel Rate Order at 56.     
111  The Generic Finance RD was never formally adopted by the Commission.  It is worth noting that the Generic 
Finance RD itself was contrary to the Consensus Document agreed to by Staff and numerous utilities.   
112  Generic Finance RD at 60-61.   


--- Page 191 ---
 
183 
 
In fact, the Generic Finance RD never expected that the CAPM would have a subsidiary and 
inferior role in perpetuity, noting: 
The issue comes down to how far the Commission should go in elevating the 
status of the CAPM, previously used only as a check on the DCF.  There is no 
doubt that the CAPM should figure prominently in the analysis, for it[] includes 
fundamental information on interest rates and the relative returns needed by 
stocks in view of those interest rates, and it provides information of what 
competitive firms are earning.  But the proponents of the proposals have simply 
not shown that the CAPM should be raised all at once to parity with the DCF 
analysis in the setting of returns on equity.113 
Clearly, the Generic Finance RD, in observing that the CAPM should not be raised to parity “all 
at once” recognized that there would come a time when it was appropriate to do so.  That time 
has come.   
 
The cases cited by Mr. Hale, and the market data used to estimate the ROE in those 
proceedings, were from a relatively stable period of low inflation and modest monetary policy 
changes.  Subsequent to those decisions, capital markets have been significantly affected by 
anomalous conditions and sustained periods of Federal Reserve involvement in 
stabilizing markets.  Tr. 232.  The 2017 case cited by Staff Witness Hale was decided at a time 
when financial conditions were substantially different compared to the current capital market 
environment.  In 2017, the Fed funds rate was 0.50% to 1.50%, while the current Fed funds 
target rate is 5.25 to 5.50%.114  Tr. 232-233.   
Mr. Nowak noted, moreover, that other jurisdictions have also recognized the need to 
deemphasize the DCF in recent cases.  Tr. 235.  Therefore, it is reasonable to place more weight 
 
113  Id. at 60 (emphasis supplied).   
114  It is ironic that Mr. Hale criticizes Mr. Nowak’s proposal to give equal weight to the DCF and CAPM, arguing

Tr. 2525.   


--- Page 192 ---
 
184 
 
on the CAPM than the one-third weight it has been given in prior cases.  As shown in Exhibit 
236 (JCN-7R) – even putting aside the infirmities of Staff’s DCF methodology – evenly 
weighting the Staff’s DCF and CAPM results would increase Staff’s ROE from 9.20% to 9.50%.  
Id.  Doing so would recognize the reality that the average ROE authorized by other regulatory 
agencies for electric and natural gas distribution utilities since 2021 averaged 9.59%.  Tr. 218.  In 
fact, despite historically low interest rates over part of that period, only three of the 112 electric 
and gas cases during that time were as low as Mr. Hale’s recommendation of 9.20%.  And, as 
noted earlier, no regulator has authorized an ROE for a major utility as low as Mr. Hale’s DCF of 
8.61%.  A simple modernization of the weighting approach to equality would help alleviate the 
problem of New York’s substandard ROEs and bring them closer to the national average.

h. Walmart Witness Kronauer’s Testimony Shows Staff’s ROE and 
Equity Ratio to be Substandard 
Walmart witness Kronauer does not conduct an ROE analysis and does not provide a 
specific ROE recommendation for Central Hudson in this proceeding.  Rather, Mr. Kronauer 
provided data from Regulatory Research Associates (“RRA”) on authorized returns for electric 
utilities in other jurisdictions from 2020-2023.  Tr 272.  There are several problems with Mr. 
Kronauer’s offering.  First, the returns authorized from early-2020 through mid-2022 are 
inapposite because they were determined at a time when interest rates were at historically low 
 
115  Exhibit 236 (JCN-7R); Tr. 221. 


--- Page 194 ---
 
186 
 
levels.  Tr. 273.  Since August 2022, however, interest rates have increased considerably.  Id.  
Second, he uses several ROE decisions that included specific penalty reductions in ROEs that are 
inapplicable to a proper cost of equity.  Id.  Furthermore, he employs decisions for Illinois 
electric utilities which for a time operated under a formula rate plan that contained a 
comprehensive alternative regulatory framework that provided protection from under-earnings 
that are not available to New York utilities, or most other state-jurisdictional utilities and which 
reflect minimal business risk.  Id. 
When those corrections are made to Mr. Kronauer’s presentation, his comparison chart, 
in fact, supports Central Hudson’s requested ROE of 9.8%.  Tr. 274-275.  For example, just 
averaging the allowed ROEs on Mr. Kronauer’s Exhibit 469 (AJK-3) for 2023 and removing the 
penalty return for UI in Connecticut and the New York companies produces an average ROE of 
9.66%.116  More important, only four of those 29 utilities had authorized equity ratios below 50% 
and the average allowed equity ratio was over 52%.  Id.  Again, this confirms Central Hudson’s 
request for an ROE of 9.8% on an equity ratio of 50%.     
i. PULP Witness Yates’s ROE Testimony is Inapplicable to a Litigated, 
One-Year Case 
PULP witness Yates provided ROE testimony (Tr. 636-653) that addressed primarily a 
complaint regarding the need for an annual ROE reset in the context of a multi-year rate plan.  
Because this case has evolved into a one-year litigated case, Mr. Yates’s testimony is 
inapplicable to it.  To the extent that Mr. Yates’s testimony asserts that Central Hudson “over 
earned” in the past, that contention was based on his recalculation of ROEs and ignored the 
benefits of the multi-year rate plans and associated earnings sharing benefits.  Furthermore, Mr. 
 
116  Interestingly, Staff Witness Hale testified that he did not investigate the most recently allowed ROEs for his 
proxy group companies.  Tr. 2504.    


--- Page 195 ---
 
187 
 
Nowak points out that Mr. Yates’s view ignores the years when Central Hudson failed to earn its 
allowed ROE.  Tr. 266-267.  Finally, as Mr. Nowak explained, ratemaking is prospective, and 
any attempt to address past earnings would be a prohibited retroactive re-litigation of previous 
rate cases.  Tr. 268. 
j. There is no Evidence that Central Hudson Faces Lower Regulatory 
Risk and, in fact, the Risk Imposed by Staff Would Warrant an ROE at 
the High End of the Proxy Group Results 
Finally, Mr. Hale tries to minimize the business and regulatory risk faced by Central 
Hudson, contending that “while many operating companies operate in jurisdictions that allow 
some of these protections, there are very few that enjoy all the regulatory protections that New 
York has.”  Tr. 2391-2392.  In Mr. Hale’s telling, regulatory risk is low in New York and 
regulation is predictable and supportive.  The facts tell a different story. 
First, as previously discussed with respect to Mr. Kronauer’s ROE offering, it is clear that 
New York authorized ROEs are near the very bottom nationally, especially when formula rates 
and punitive ROEs are eliminated.  Second, the mere fact that this is a litigated case for Central 
Hudson and not a multi-year settlement makes New York rate regulation less predictable.  Add 
to that the CLCPA and other incipient regulatory and legislative actions and New York’s risks 
loom even larger. 
More important, however, Mr. Nowak addressed several other elements that Mr. Hale 
ignores.  Mr. Nowak pointed out that Mr. Hale has not performed any comparative analysis to 
demonstrate that progressive regulatory mechanisms are unique to New York, or otherwise 
reduce the Company risk profile as compared to the industry or the proxy companies.  Tr. 258.  
Mr. Nowak further noted that RRA found that New York’s risk profile offers no comparative 
advantage compared to other jurisdictions, as RRA has assigned a ranking of “Average/2” to 
New York, which is consistent with the majority of regulatory jurisdictions across the United 


--- Page 196 ---
 
188 
 
States.  Id.  This puts New York in the middle of the range; supporting the contention that, even 
with the risk reducing measures implemented in New York, those measures result in no 
significant difference in regulatory risk between New York and other jurisdictions.  Id.  With 
average business and regulatory risk, New York should be affording average ROEs.  But that is 
not the case, as Mr. Kronauer’s presentation shows that New York’s ROEs are below the 
national average.   
Next, Mr. Hale has provided no evidence that any other utilities are subject to the 
 penalties comparable to the Company’s exposure.  Tr. 258-259.  In fact, although “broadly 
aware” of them, Mr. Hale confessed on cross-examination that he was unaware of the specifics 
of Staff’s proposed NRAs (Tr. 2499), did not know how the rating agencies would view them 
(Tr. 2500-2501) and was unaware of Staff’s proposed bill credits.  Tr. 2501.  In fact, Mr. Hale 
did not know the total BPs of Staff’s NRA penalties or their dollar value.  Tr. 2505.  That is, 
while other utilities may be held to performance standards, there is no evidence that there is a 
direct, downside-only penalty in other jurisdictions.  Id.  Further, the proposed dramatic 
increases to NRAs recommended by Staff in this case would further distinguish Central Hudson 
from utilities in other jurisdictions, vastly increasing the Company’s business and regulatory risk 
and hence, its required ROE.  Id.   
All of the above demonstrates that Central Hudson’s business and regulatory risk warrant 
an ROE at the high end of the range of results.  Moreover, as the discussion of Mr. Kronauer’s 
report indicates, the 48% equity ratio recommended by Staff is also at the low end of the range of 
authorized equity ratios nationally, increasing Central Hudson’s financial risk above other 


--- Page 197 ---
 
189 
 
companies117 and warranting an ROE at the high end of the range.  Consequently, the 9.8% ROE 
and 50% equity ratio sought by the Company are reasonable, albeit conservative, and should be 
adopted.    
E. 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
The only party addressing an Earnings Sharing Mechanism (“ESM”) in this matter is 
PULP Witness Yates.  Mr. Yates, however, only contended that an annual ROE update should be 
included to any ESM adopted in the context of a multi-year settlement.  Tr. 642.  Irrespective of 
the merits of Mr. Yates’s proposal, neither he, nor any other witness, has proposed an ESM and 
this case clearly is a one-year litigated case and not a multi-year settlement.  Consequently, there 
is no evidence concerning either the need for an ESM or what one would look like if such a need 
had been established on this record.    
VII. 
NEGATIVE REVENUE ADJUSTMENTS 
A. 
Applicability in a One-Year Case 
The Company is currently subject to a set of gas safety and CSPI metrics that were 
negotiated as part of the 2021 Rate Plan.  The Company agreed to be subject to these 
performance standards – and millions of dollars of associated performance penalties – as part of 
a rate settlement that provided for an agreed level of capital investments and O&M spending, in 
addition to resolving other contentious issues among the parties.  The Company was able to 
assess the reasonableness of the performance targets (and associated penalties) and assume a 
level of risk commensurate within the overall structure of the settlement. 
Similarly, in reaching an agreement in these proceedings on electric capital and 
operations investments, including incremental Hazard Tree Program bucket truck crews, the 
 
117  Mr. Hale agreed that more debt produces more financial risk.  Tr.  2485.  He also conceded that the rating 
agencies view higher equity ratios as providing better cash flow support.  Tr. 2494-2495.  


--- Page 198 ---
 
190 
 
Company is able to assess the reasonableness and assume a level of risk associated with the 
SAIFI and CAIDI performance targets and associated penalties.  In contrast, because there is 
significant disagreement between the Company and Staff related to gas capital investments and 
customer service/experience investments, a litigated, one-year case does not enable the Company 
to agree on a reasonable set of performance targets or NRAs for gas safety and CSPI or, 
importantly, ensure an adequate level of capital and O&M funding to achieve those performance 
targets.  In fact, in setting certain gas safety performance targets, Staff conceded that it did not 
assess the cost it would take to achieve the targets.  Tr. 2772. 
While the Company proposed gas safety and CSPI metrics in its direct testimony, it did 
so in contemplation of a multi-year rate plan and within the context of its overall filing, which 
sought approval of various gas safety and customer service initiatives as well as changes to the 
metrics themselves.  The Company’s proposed investments in gas safety and customer 
experience programs, many of which Staff rejects, would drive improvements over time.  While 
it may be appropriate to attach NRAs to gas safety and customer service performance in a settled 
multi-year rate plan, the Company does not agree with the inclusion of gas safety and CSPI 
performance metrics in a one-year litigated case.118  Indeed, in the past the Commission 
specifically declined to impose gas safety or customer service performance metrics in a one-year 
litigated case.119  
Moreover, Staff’s recommended performance standards for safety and customer service 
mechanisms are punitive in nature and lack support under the law.  Both the Staff Pipeline Safety 
 
118  Staff has recognized this distinction in other contexts.  See Case 23-M-0040 – In the Matter of Utility Customer 
Service Performance, 2022 Consumer Service Performance Report (Jun. 22, 2023) (“Each utility submits 
performance reports for the prior year, including any incurred negative revenue adjustments (NRAs) for 
measures as negotiated in individual utility rate case proceedings.”) (emphasis added). 
119  See National Fuel Rate Order at 63-64. 


--- Page 199 ---
 
191 
 
Panel and the Staff Consumer Services Panel seek to impose penalties on the Company if it falls 
below certain metrics for gas safety and customer service, respectively - metrics found nowhere 
under the Public Service Law or the Commission’s regulations.  In the absence of a settlement 
and in the context of a litigated rate proceeding, it is the Company’s position that any imposition 
of a mechanism that seeks to penalize Central Hudson for any violation of performance standards 
would be unlawful because the Commission lacks the authority to impose these penalties.120   
The Public Service Law provides that the Company, like every other electric and gas 
corporation, “shall furnish and provide such service, instrumentalities and facilities as shall be 
safe and adequate.”121  The Public Service Law, therefore, requires that utilities provide 
“adequate service” - a uniform, statewide standard, established by the Legislature.  Unless a 
compelling reason is provided, the adequate service standard must be the same and universally 
applied to all electric and gas utilities.  There is, however, no Commission regulation for gas and 
electric companies establishing “adequate” customer service standards including acceptable 
minimum levels of service for: PSC Complaints (Tr. 4499); Residential Customer Satisfaction 
(Tr. 4510); Call Answer Rate (Tr. 4505); Estimated Bills; and Adjusted Bills.  Likewise, there is 
no Commission regulation setting forth “adequate” gas safety standards or acceptable minimum 
levels for leak management, damage prevention, or emergency response.   
Absent Commission-set uniform service level standards such as those adopted for 
 
120   This issue has been presented to the courts previously when the Commission imposed such customer service 
and gas safety standards on National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation in Case 07-G-0141.  National Fuel 
appealed the imposition of such standards and the Appellate Division held that “Petitioner will suffer no such 
injury, however, unless respondent holds a show cause hearing, finds that petitioner is not in compliance with 
the plan and, as a result, reduces petitioner’s rates.  Inasmuch as the harm anticipated by petitioner may never 
occur, the controversy is not ripe for judicial review.”  Mtr. of National Fuel Gas Distribution Corp. v. Pub. 
Serv. Comm’n, 71 A.D.3d 62, 64 (3d Dep’t 2009).  Accordingly, the issue of the legality of the imposition of 
such penalties remains unresolved.   
121  Public Service Law § 65(1). 


--- Page 200 ---
 
192 
 
telephone companies through rulemaking,122 one must conclude that adequate service is, at most, 
the minimum level of performance that is deemed satisfactory by the Commission through the 
annual customer service and gas safety reports that are prepared by Staff and reviewed by the 
Commission each year, which compare performance across utilities, and that no punishment can 
lawfully or logically be applied for service that exceeds that minimum standard – even if it falls 
below the standard previously supplied by the utility in question.123   
The Company is not contesting the Commission’s authority or duty to regulate gas safety 
and customer service.  However, given that neither the Public Service Law nor the Commission’s 
regulations set forth safe and adequate service levels for gas safety and customer service 
performance, the Company objects to the imposition of a scheme to impose penalties against it 
for any future failure to meet certain arbitrary measures of customer service and gas safety 
performance.  This is particularly the case when the standards are not based on any objective 
measure of gas safety or customer service and would result in another utility escaping a penalty 
even when it provides a lesser level of service than the Company.  In fact, Staff freely admits that 
the Company could have the best performance in the state and still be subject to a penalty.  Tr. 
2722. 
The gas safety and CSPI NRAs proposed by Staff are simply not authorized by law.  The 
Commission “possesses only those powers expressly delegated to it by the Legislature, or 
 
122  See 16 NYCRR Part 603. 
123   Not only do the telephone customer service standards set in the regulations at 16 NYCRR Part 603 demonstrate 
the need for uniform standards, but Case 91-M-0500, in which the Commission proposed a draft Part 15 to the 
New York regulations to establish uniform standards for electric and gas utilities also proves the need to do so.  
See Case 91-M-0500, Order Directing Utilities to Supply Service Data (January 16, 1992).  The Commission 
has the authority to adopt customer service standards, however, exercise of that authority requires that it be 
done in a uniform fashion and through a rulemaking with public comment, pursuant to SAPA and not ad hoc in 
each utility rate case.  Such method is ideal as it puts all utilities on clear notice of what is uniformly acceptable 
performance and what is not.  


--- Page 201 ---
 
193 
 
incidental to its expressed powers, together with those required by necessary implication to 
enable [the Commission] to fulfill its statutory mandate.”124  The statutory structure could not be 
more straightforward:125 
PSL § 65(1) requires that utilities provide “service, as shall be safe and adequate 
and in all respects just and reasonable.” PSL § 66(2) empowers the Commission 
to hold utilities to this obligation, by vesting the Commission with the authority 
to investigate utilities and their performance, as well as to order utilities to make 
reasonable improvements that are in the public interest. 
 
The Commission has significant regulatory authority to ensure that utilities meet 
their obligations to provide safe, adequate, and reliable service.  As the Public 
Service Law makes clear, “[e]very combination gas and electric corporation . . . 
shall adhere to every provision of this chapter and every order or regulation 
adopted under authority of this chapter[.]” 
 
PSL § 25-a(3) and (4) authorize the Commission to commence an administrative 
penalty proceeding against a combination gas and electric corporation.126 
 
As the Commission has made clear, the appropriate remedy to address a utility’s failure 
to provide safe, adequate and reliable service in violation of the Public Service Law or 
Commission regulation is to investigate the utility’s performance and then, if necessary, 
commence an administrative penalty proceeding and assess penalties under Public Service Law § 
25-a(3) and (4).127  In violation of this legislative scheme, Staff has arbitrarily proposed 
 
124  Matter of Niagara Mohawk Power Corp. v. Public Service Comm’n., 69 N.Y.2d 365, 368-369 (1987); see also 
Matter of Crescent Estates Water Co. v. Public Service Comm’n, 77 N.Y.2d 611, 616-617 (1991).   
125  The Commission could also pursue penalties under Public Service Law Section 25.  Under Section 24 of the 
Public Service Law, the Commission must go to court to have any penalty or forfeiture established, both as to 
amount and enforcement.  Such penalties lie only for violations of the Public Service Law or duly promulgated 
orders.  In the case of regulations, penalties are authorized under Public Service Law Section 25 only in two 
specific circumstances, neither of which are applicable here.  PSL § 25(3)(4).  See also People v. Consolidated 
Edison Company of New York, Inc., Albany County Special Term, March 11, 1983, Motion No. 32, Klein, J. 
(slip opinion).

citations omitted). 
127  Under Public Service Law § 25-a(3), the Commission may assess a civil penalty not exceeding the greater of 
$100,000 or “two one hundredths of one percent of the annual intrastate gross operating revenue of the

194 
 
standards that are specific to Central Hudson and to establish a penalty scheme for violations of 
those standards, regardless of whether the Company is providing a higher level of gas safety or 
customer service than any other company.  Tellingly, these penalties far exceed the maximum 
penalties permissible under Public Service Law §§ 25 or 25-a and also far exceed the NRAs that 
other utilities have agreed to as part of multi-year settlements.  Staff’s recommended NRAs thus 
lack a rational basis and result in disparate treatment.   
Under Staff’s proposal, the Company could be penalized for providing service that is 
considered superior to that of another company while that other company escapes penalties.  This 
approach is not only unreasonable, it is unlawful.  “An agency of the State denies equal 
protection when it treats persons similarly situated differently under the law...”128  There is 
absolutely no basis in the record to demonstrate why it is appropriate to apply different standards 
to Central Hudson than to other gas and electric companies.  Utilities are, as a matter of fact, 
“similarly situated” and a company’s past performance or rate level is not rationally related to 
what should be an objective safety or customer service goal.129  Furthermore, equal protection 
considerations aside, treating similarly situated entities differently is inherently arbitrary and 
capricious.130  Using different standards for similarly situated electric and gas companies cannot 
be permitted.131   
 
corporation[.]” Under Public Service Law § 25-a(4), the Commission may assess a civil penalty not exceeding 
the greater of $250,000 or “three one-hundredths of one percent of the annual intrastate gross operating revenue 
of the corporation[] . . . whichever is greater[.]”. 
128  Abrams v. Bronstein, 33 N.Y. 2d 488, 492 (1974) (citations omitted). 
129  Weissman v. Evans, 56 N.Y.2d 458, 466 (1982) (“classification must then be compared to the objective to
