Moreover, the Commission cannot lawfully impose penalties unless they are authorized 
by law, and the imposition of NRA penalties for failing to meet arbitrary service levels not 
contained in the Public Service Law or Commission regulations, is beyond the Commission’s 
statutory powers.  In fact, the very nature of the NRAs to operate retroactively, i.e., to reduce 
earnings based on a subsequent event, demonstrates that the NRAs are unlawful retroactive 
ratemaking mechanisms.   
If Staff’s proposal is adopted, the Commission would have the ability to retroactively 
adjust its previously allowed return to account for some future conduct on the part of the 
Company.  This is clearly beyond the Commission’s power.  It is well-settled that legislative acts 
concerning the approval of public utility tariffs, are prospective only and may not be applied 
retroactively.  If, in the future, safety or customer service might fall to unacceptable levels, the 
Commission can reset the rate of return prospectively to reflect that diminution of service.132  As 
the U.S. Supreme Court explained, however, although an agency may “repeal the order as it 
affected future action, and substitute a new rule or conduct as often as occasion might require, [ ] 
this was obviously the limit of its power, as that of the legislature itself.”133  The NRA 
mechanisms proposed by Staff are clearly unlawful efforts to amend the rate of return set in these 
cases retroactively and are, accordingly, invalid. 
The elimination of the NRAs will not leave Staff or the Commission powerless with 
respect to the Company’s gas safety and customer service performance.  The fact is that all gas 
and electric utilities have been submitting customer service performance data since 1992 
pursuant to the Commission’s January 16, 1992, Order Directing Utilities to Supply Service Data 
 
132  See Mtr of Hurley Water Co., Inc. v. Pub. Serv. Comm’n, supra.   
133  Arizona Grocery v. Atchison Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Ry. Co., 284 U.S. 370, 389 (1932).   


--- Page 204 ---
 
196 
 
in Case 91-M-0500 and subsequent orders.  Similarly, New York gas utilities are subject to 
robust regulatory reporting requirements for various gas safety performance metrics, which 
began as the result of collaborative efforts beginning in the 1990s.  These reporting requirements 
will occur regardless of whether NRAs are attached to them.  The Commission will be able to 
analyze the Company’s performance through these reporting obligations and, ultimately, the 
Commission can take action, in the form of a penalty proceeding, if the Company fails to provide 
“safe and adequate” service. 
Based on the foregoing, it is the Company’s position that NRAs should not be imposed 
on the Company for failing to meet specified gas safety and CSPI performance targets.  If the 
Commission were to determine otherwise, the Company submits that the targets and NRAs 
should be set at the Company’s proposed levels as set forth in Sections IX and X herein. 
B. 
Carrying Charges on NRAs 
The Staff Consumer Services Panel recommends that if the Company incurs NRAs, the 
balance should accrue carrying charges (i.e., interest at the pre-tax rate of return) from the time it 
is recorded on the Company’s books at the close of the year until such time as the Commission 
rules on the disposition of the balance.  Tr. 4377.  Staff asserts that this practice is consistent 
with current practice at other utilities.  Tr. 4377.   
Staff’s recommendation should be rejected because it is inconsistent with the intent 
behind carrying charges.  Accruing interest or carrying charges on a balance is appropriate to 
compensate for financing costs incurred that are associated with the outlay of cash when the 
timing of recovery from or return of cash to customers is delayed.  Tr. 1550.  During cross-
examination, Staff conceded that carrying charges provide for the recovery of financing costs 
associated with outlays of cash by customers or the Company and that when there is no outlay of 


--- Page 205 ---
 
197 
 
cash, carrying charges do not apply.  Tr. 4171.  Importantly, when the Company incurs an NRA, 
there is no cash outlay and therefore carrying charges are not applicable.  Tr. 1550. 
The fact that other utilities agreed to have carrying charges apply to NRA balances is 
inapposite because those utilities did so in the context of multi-year settled rate plans that 
included compromise on a number of rate and other regulatory issues.  Rate case settlements by 
their very nature involve give and take among the parties to reach a comprehensive agreement.  
Staff’s stated basis for applying carrying charges to NRA balances (i.e., consistency with other 
utility practice) inappropriately ignores the settlement process give and take that went into 
reaching the other utility rate plans and thus cannot be used as a basis to impose carrying charges 
on NRA balances in these proceedings. 
VIII. ELECTRIC RELIABILITY 
A. 
CAIDI and SAIFI Targets and Associated NRA 
Electric reliability performance at Central Hudson is primarily measured utilizing the 
System Average Interruption Frequency Index (“SAIFI”) and Customer Average Interruption 
Duration Index (“CAIDI”) indices, long used by the Commission to measure reliability.  Tr. 
2059.  SAIFI is the number of times the average customer’s service is interrupted in a year, and 
CAIDI is the average number of hours required to restore service to a customer whose service 
was interrupted.  Tr. 2611.  The Company’s current electric reliability performance targets are at 
or below 1.3 for SAIFI and at or below 2.5 for CAIDI.  Tr. 2071.  There is an NRA of 30 BPs 
associated with missing each of these targets.  Tr. 2071. 
The Company proposed maintaining the current System SAIFI and CAIDI targets but 
modifying the SAIFI mechanism to a two-tier system.  Under the Company’s proposal it would 
incur a 10 BP NRA for exceeding 1.30 and an additional 20 BP NRA for exceeding 1.34.  Tr. 
2071-2072.  The Company’s proposal would thus maintain the existing maximum total NRA 


--- Page 206 ---
 
198 
 
exposure of 30 BPs.  Tr. 2072.  The minimum threshold of 1.30 aligns with the current 
aggressive SAIFI target whereas the 1.34 target aligns with the five-year average performance.  
Tr. 2072. 
Staff opposed the Company’s recommended two-tier structure for SAIFI and instead 
recommended that the Commission maintain the Company’s current SAIFI and CAIDI targets of 
1.3 and 2.5, respectively.  Tr. 2613-2614.  Staff’s reason for opposing the two-tier structure was 
that the Company only missed the SAIFI target one time in the past three years and thus the two-
tier structure would not encourage the Company to continue to improve its outage performance.  
Tr. 2613-2614  
In rebuttal testimony, the Company continued to recommend the two-tier SAIFI structure 
arguing that it would incentivize rather than disincentivize consistent improvement in this area.  
Tr. 2134. 
The Company and Staff ultimately entered into the Electric Stipulation (see Exhibit 516) 
that, among other things, sets the SAIFI and CAIDI targets that would apply to Central Hudson 
in calendar year 2025.  Specifically, the Company and Staff agreed as follows: 
SAIFI - The average number of times that a customer is interrupted for five 
minutes or more per 1,000 customers served during the year.  The Company will 
be subject to a 30 basis point (electric, pre-tax) potential negative revenue 
adjustment for failure to achieve an annual SAIFI target of 1.30 in 2025.  
  
CAIDI – The average interruption duration time (customers-hours interrupted) for 
those customers that experience an interruption during the year. The Company 
will be subject to a 30 basis point (electric, pre-tax) potential negative revenue 
adjustment for failure to achieve an annual CAIDI target of 2.50 in 2025. 
 
The Company and Staff, typically adverse parties, settled their dispute regarding SAIFI 
and CAIDI targets by agreeing to utilize Staff’s litigation position.  The Electric Stipulation as it 
relates to this issue is supported by the record and falls within the range of testimony filed by the 


--- Page 207 ---
 
199 
 
Company and Staff.  The agreed upon SAIFI and CAIDI targets are consistent with the Public 
Service Law and Commission precedent.  For these issues, the Electric Stipulation also strikes a 
fair balance between the interests of customers and the Company.  Given these factors, it is 
reasonable to conclude that had these issues been fully litigated, Your Honors and the 
Commission would have reached a similar result.  Accordingly, this portion of the Electric 
Stipulation meets the public interest standard set forth in the Commission’s Settlement 
Guidelines and should be adopted by Your Honors and the Commission without modification. 
B. 
CAIDI and SAIFI Exclusions 
Currently, the Company excludes from its SAIFI and CAIDI calculations major storm 
outages, incidents resulting from a catastrophic event beyond the control of the Company and 
any incident where problems beyond the Company's control involving generation or the bulk 
transmission system is the key factor in the outage, from current calculations.  Tr. 2070.  In 
direct testimony, the Company also noted that Factors beyond the control of the Company 
(“Non-Utility Control Outages”) could adversely affect the ability of the Company to meet the 
electric reliability performance measure targets established in this proceeding.  Tr. 2070.  Non-
Utility Control Outages include, but are not limited to, outages due to the following: 1) 
vandalism; 2) unexpected deforestation (e.g., Emerald Ash Borer); 3) foreign utility supply; 4) 
motor vehicle accidents; 5) weather; 6) strategic pole hits; and 7) disruptions in neighboring 
utility systems.  Tr. 2070.  The Company’s Electric Capital and Operations Panel stated that the 
Company does not waive and expressly retains their right to petition the Commission for a 
waiver, release, or other relief related to a Company’s failure to meet the targets set forth in this 
proceeding as a result of these factors and others beyond the Company’s control.  Tr. 2070-2071.   
Staff testified that Central Hudson should not be able to automatically exclude Non-
Utility Control Outages from its SAIFI and CAIDI calculations.  Tr. 2616.  Staff noted that a 


--- Page 208 ---
 
200 
 
waiver process is already available to the Company where a utility may petition the Commission 
directly if the Company believes extraordinary circumstances exist that warrant excluding an 
event from being counted towards the utility’s reliability metrics.  Tr. 2616.  Staff further argues 
that it would not be appropriate to maintain the Company’s current SAIFI and CAIDI targets if 
these exclusions were universally granted without a case-by-case review.  Tr. 2616. 
There appears to be some confusion as to the Company’s request with respect to Non-
Utility Control Outages.  The Company is not looking to have such outages automatically 
excluded from its SAIFI and CAIDI calculations as Staff suggests.  The purpose of the 
Company’s testimony was to indicate that there are outages that are beyond the Company’s 
control and to establish that the Company be allowed to petition the Commission to request that 
Non-Utility Control Outages be exempt from SAIFI and CAIDI calculations within 60 days after 
such an outage occurs, rather than within 45 days as is currently set forth by Commission 
order.134 
IX. 
GAS SAFETY 
A. 
Leak Prone Pipe Removal 
The replacement of leak prone mains and services is important, as these projects 
eliminate materials such as wrought iron, cast iron, and unprotected steel pipelines, that pose the 
greatest risk to the public safety based on leak rate.  The Company develops project designs 
based on current industry standards to increase operational safety and strengthen reliability.  Tr. 
1117.  The current annual NRA target is a penalty of 15 BPs for the failure to achieve 15 miles 
or more of LPP replacement per year.  Tr. 1118.   
 
134  Cases 02-E-1240 et al. - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission to Examine Electric Service Standards and 
Methodologies, Order Adopting Changes to Standards on Reliability of Electric Service (Oct. 12, 2004). 


--- Page 209 ---
 
201 
 
Despite the 15-mile minimum penalty threshold, Staff recognizes that the Company has 
done much better, replacing on average over 19 miles of LPP per year over the last five years.  
Tr. 2666.  Staff and the Company agree that the 15-mile threshold for main replacement is 
appropriate, should be continued in the Rate Year and remain in effect until changed by the 
Commission.  Tr. 1150-1151, 2218.   
As discussed above, however, the Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act is likely to 
increase costs for the Company’s distribution improvement projects, including costs to replace 
LPP.  Tr. 1151.  Significant increases to LPP removal costs per mile due to the Act will likely 
place the Company at risk of failing to meet its LPP removal targets without a corresponding 
increase in expenditure levels.  Tr. 1151.  In other words, the Company runs the risk of not 
completing the proposed mileage targets with the current funding proposed, or the Company runs 
the risk of severely overrunning the capital budget to achieve the 15-mile target while staying 
compliant with the requirements of the new law.  Tr. 1151.  It is for this reason that the Company 
is seeking a deferral for increased costs related to the Act.  Without such a deferral, the Company 
should not be subject to an NRA for this metric as it may not have the ability to recover costs 
related to achieving the 15-mile target.   
B. 
Leak Management 
Any discussion of leak management must precede from the clear reality, revealed in 
Staff’s most recent 2022 Pipeline Safety Performance Measures Report (“Staff Safety Report”)  
that Central Hudson is one of the better performing New York utilities in this area.135  In fact, 
several other gas distribution utilities have leak backlogs many times higher than the Company’s 
 
135

the impacts these factors may have on future leaks.  Tr. 1147.138  That volatility is apparent

As noted in the Company’s discussion of penalties, there should be no NRA in a one-year

target of 86.  Id. 
Staff also proposes to cease any positive revenues adjustments (“PRAs”) associated with 
leak management.  Staff offers two reasons for why the PRA should be discontinued: 1) Staff 
asserts that, given the recent annual level of Central Hudson’s leak backlog, it is unnecessary to 
provide an incentive to reduce it; and 2) Staff argues that the Company should not be rewarded 
for simply complying with the requirements of 16 NYCRR Part 255 related to leak management.

First, an appropriate level of PRAs coupled with reasonable “reach” targets motivate 
improved performance.  Fundamentally, PRAs will drive increased levels of performance

require a repair be made during its lifetime.139  Given that the Company must repair Type 3 leaks 
in order to achieve its leak management target (Tr. 2753), Staff is incorrect that the Company’s 
proposed PRAs for reducing leaks would reward the Company for simply complying with the 
requirements of 16 NYCRR Part 255.   
Third, the SPSP’s position is inconsistent with positions that Staff has taken in other gas

cross-examination, Staff was unable to identify anything unique about Central Hudson that led to 
the SPSP’s recommendation to discontinue the PRA other than the Company’s exemplary 
performance in this area.  Tr. 2753-2754.  In other words, Staff’s is proposing to punish good 
behavior.

C. 
Damage Prevention 
In the area of Damage Prevention, Staff’s Safety Report (Exhibit 222 (GSP-2R)) is, 
again, revealing.  Here Central Hudson is seen as one of the best performers, with total damages 
below the statewide average.  Exhibit 222 (GSP-2R), page 15.  Despite explicitly recognizing 
that Central Hudson performs much better in this regard than the statewide average (Tr. 2680), 
Staff, nevertheless, proposes to increase the Company’s targets and NRAs dramatically.  Staff’s 
proposed damage prevention targets, specifically the threshold ranges for determining basis point 
impacts, are neither aligned with past Company rate plan targets nor consistent with targets 
established for other utilities.  Tr. 1152. 
 
Central Hudson’s current targets and associated NRA basis point penalties are divided 
into three tiers as follows: failure to meet the total damage rate of 1.65 per 1,000 one-call tickets 
for tier one would result in an NRA of five pre-tax BPs; failure to meet the total damage rate 
between 1.85 and 2.00 per 1,000 one-call tickets for tier two would result in an NRA of 10 pre-
tax BPs: and failure to meet the total damage rate of 2.00 per 1,000 one-call tickets for tier three 
would result in an NRA of 20 pre-tax BPs.  Tr. 2681-2682.  The Company is also eligible for 
PRAs as follows: for a total damage rate below 1.35 per 1,000 one call-tickets, Central Hudson 
can earn up to four BPs; for a damage rate below 1.25 per 1,000 one-call tickets, Central Hudson 


--- Page 215 ---
 
207 
 
can earn up to six BPs; and for a damage rate below 1.19 per 1,000 one-call tickets, Central 
Hudson can earn up to 10 BPs.  Id. 
 
Despite conceding both that Central Hudson is a superior performer (Tr. 2758) and that 
damages are not even “entirely within the Company’s control” (Tr. 2682-2683, 2759), Staff, 
nevertheless, baselessly seeks dramatically increased targets and penalties.  Staff recommends 
the following total damage targets and associated revenue adjustments: for a total damage 
performance rate of 1.70 or greater per 1,000 one-call tickets, the Company would incur an NRA 
of 20 BPs; for a rate between 1.55 and 1.69, the Company would incur an NRA of 10 BPs; for a 
rate between 1.40 and 1.54, the Company would incur an NRA of 5 BPs; for a total damage rate 
between 1.10 and 1.24, the Company would earn a PRA of 4 BPs; for a rate between 1.00 and 
1.09 the Company would earn a PRA of 6 BPs; and for a rate below 1.00, the Company would 
earn a PRA of 10 BPs.  Tr. 2685.  Staff avers that its proposed penalties are “reasonable.”  Tr. 
2685-2686.  With a 2022 state average rate of 1.70 damages per 1000 tickets (Tr. 2758; Exhibit. 
222 (GSP-2R), page 14), Staff’s proposed penalties – which would penalize Central Hudson 20 
BPs (the maximum penalty) for performing at the statewide average level of performance – are 
not “reasonable” - they are outrageous.    
Staff’s sole rationale for these targets and NRA levels is “[w]e believe the Company’s 
performance in the last five years…has proven that the recommended targets are achievable.” Tr. 
2685-2686.  While the Company is proud of its results and has worked very hard to achieve 
them, the premise that the Company’s high performance in this area necessitates that it should be 
held to a much more stringent target than past Company rate plans and other gas utility rate 
plans, is wildly unwarranted.    


--- Page 216 ---
 
208 
 
Staff conceded on cross-examination that in recommending its target, Staff measured 
Central Hudson only against its own high past performance.  Tr. 2720-2721.  This is also unfair 
and unreasonable when compared to other New York gas utilities’ approved targets.  As shown 
in Exhibit 223 (GSP-3R) for 2024, the 1.70 value proposed by Staff is 32% lower than the state 
average maximum NRA target of 2.50.  Tr. 1154.  Also, the 1.00 value proposed by Staff is 21% 
lower than the state average maximum PRA target of 1.26.140  Id.  Finally, the 0.15 value 
proposed by Staff is 71% lower than the state average dead band width for zero basis point 
impact of 0.52.  Id.  Exhibit 223 (GSP-3R) demonstrates that if Staff’s recommended targets are 
adopted, Central Hudson would be an outlier in this area.  The variation in target values among 
the New York gas utilities, combined with Staff’s conclusory assertion that its proposed targets 
are “achievable” simply based on past performance, calls into question the reasonableness of 
Staff’s recommended targets and turns performance-based regulation on its head.  Instead of 
being penalized for achieving exceptional performance as Staff recommends, the Company 
should be rewarded for such performance. 
Staff’s claim that damages should decline as LPP is replaced (Tr. 2687) is also misplaced.  
The Company’s Gas Safety Panel explained that, until program completion, the Company has a 
much greater exposure to damages due to associated unknown, unrecorded gas facilities for 
which the Company has no records.  Tr. 1156.  Replacement of these LPP facilities requires 
excavation in very close proximity to other underground facilities within urban streets.  Much of 
the pipe being replaced was installed in the early 1900s when asset recording practices were 
lacking and many of these records have been misplaced or lost.  Id. 
 
140  Tellingly, not a single utility was able to achieve a 1.00 target in 2022.  Tr. 2759-2760. 


--- Page 217 ---
 
209 
 
Based on the foregoing, it should be clear that, if any level of NRAs/PRAs is to be 
employed in this one-year litigated case, which they should not be for the reasons discussed 
above, the level adopted in the 2021 Rate Plan, as explained by the Company’s Gas Safety Panel 
in their direct testimony (Tr. 1128-1129) should be the one adopted.

Once again, despite the fact that the Staff Safety Report shows that Central Hudson is one

recommends that all high-risk field audit violations should result in an NRA of one BP, as 
opposed to the current NRA of 0.5 BP for the first 20 violations and one BP for violations 
greater than 20.  In addition, the SPSP recommends lowering the number of high risk record 
violations from 6-20 to 6-10 for the second bracket, from 21 plus to 11 plus for the third bracket, 
and lowering the number of other risk record violations from 1-15 to 1-10 for the first bracket 
and from 16 plus to 11 plus for the second bracket, as shown in Exhibit 335 (SPSP-2).  Tr. 2696.

“other risk.”  Tr. 2761.

currently in place for other New York gas utilities.  Exhibit 224 (GSP-4R) contains the Record 


--- Page 219 ---
 
211 
 
Audit and Field Audit targets for other New York gas utilities.  This exhibit demonstrates that, if 
Staff’s proposals were implemented, Central Hudson would have some of the most restrictive 
targets and penalties compared to the other New York gas utilities.  Indeed, most New York gas 
utilities currently have an NRA of 0.5 BPs for 6-20 occurrences for the “High Risk” record audit 
category.  Staff’s proposal here – that the Company be subject to a one BP NRA for 11 or more 
High Risk record audit occurrences – is far more punitive and discriminatory than any other such 
NRA scheme in the state.  Tr. 1158.  The same is true for Staff’s proposed changes to the Other 
Risk record audit category in which the Company would incur a .25 BP NRA for 11 or more 
occurrences instead of the previous target of 16 or 20 more occurrences.  For the High Risk Field 
Audit, no other utility has a target or penalties as extreme as Staff’s proposal (i.e., that the 
Company would incur a one BP NRA for every field audit occurrence).  Other utilities’ gas 
safety regulation metrics contain an NRA of .5 BPs up to 20 field violations, and one BP for 
every occurrence beyond 20.  Id. 
 
Again, as noted in the Damage Prevention and Leak Management sections above, and as 
shown here, the variation in Staff’s proposed target values among the New York gas utilities and 
Central Hudson, combined with Staff’s conclusory assertion that its proposed targets are 
achievable simply based on Central Hudson’s own excellent past performance, demonstrates the 
patent unreasonableness of Staff’s recommended targets.   
As noted in the Company’s discussion of penalties, there should be no NRAs for this 
metric in a one-year litigated case.  If, however, targets and NRAs were to be set, the Company’s 
existing targets and NRAs should remain in place for the Rate Year.

achievable from a cost-benefit analysis perspective.  It would make little sense for a PRA 
mechanism to be structured in a manner that would provide a reward valued less than the amount 
it takes to achieve the reward.  A cost benefit analysis would not warrant Staff’s doubling of the

PRA benefit levels.  Indeed, Staff freely admits that it does not take costs into consideration 
when setting these gas safety targets.  Tr. 2772.

G. 
Residential Methane Detection Program 
As directed in the June 14, 2018 Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and 
Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan in Case 17-G-0460, Central Hudson developed a 
Residential Methane Detection (“RMD”) Program using NRA funds.  Tr. 1134.  The RMD 
Program continued in the Company’s 2021 Rate Plan.  To date, approximately 7,900 methane 
detectors have been provided to customers.  Central Hudson continues to respond to gas odor 
complaints and if the methane detector alarms or is defective, Central Hudson will send them a 
replacement unit.  Tr. 1135.  Central Hudson is proposing to continue the existing program 
during the Rate Year, while coincidentally re-evaluating and researching enhancements to the 
program in accordance with newly developed National Fire Prevention Association guidelines.  
It is anticipated that an enhanced program would be established and rolled out during a future 
Rate Year.  Id.   
 
Staff agrees that the RMD be continued and recommends that part of the $100,000 
currently funded through Company’s O&M approved in the 2021 Rate Plan be applied towards 
researching and developing an enhanced RMD Program, with connected methane detector 
devices that make use of Long Range Wide Area Network, or LoRaWAN, technology with long 
device battery life.  Tr. 2706.  In Staff’s view, this technology provides greater communication 
range with low bandwidths compared to other wireless data transmission technologies.  Id.   
The Company notes that the identified $100,000 currently funded in rates pursuant to the 
2021 Rate Plan is not subject to deferral, and the revenue requirement in these cases does not 


--- Page 222 ---
 
214 
 
include the same $100,000 for the RMD Program.  Staff’s recommended research and 
development, therefore, would need to occur under the existing rate plan (i.e., by June 30, 2024).  
Due to timing constraints, that is not possible.  
H. 
Pipeline Safety Management System 
The Pipeline Safety Management System (“PSMS”) is the American Petroleum 
Institute’s recommended practice (API RP 1173) that establishes an end-to-end safety framework 
for pipeline operators under jurisdiction of the United States Department of Transportation.  Tr. 
1135-1136.  The goal of PSMS is to result in zero safety accidents or incidents.  Staff recognizes 
that the PSMS is not a mandated program, but also encourages and supports the continuation of 
the program with funding at a reasonable level.  Tr. 2706-2708.  Staff, however, recommends a 
reduction of $150,000 from the $250,000 requested by the Company based on historical averages 
and a lack of support.  Tr. 2707-2708. 
The Company’s Gas Safety Panel noted that Staff’s proposed $100,000 per year for this 
program is primarily based on historical averages that included the creation of a Road Map and 
the initial launch of the program.  Tr. 1159.  The Panel explained that the $100,000 is insufficient 
to implement programs for each phase of the Road Map that is required to reach a mature PSMS 
program.  The Road Map activities to be implemented include a PSMS policy, visioning 
sessions, awareness plans, organizational design and resource plan, developing goals and KPIs, 
and element specific process and procedures.  It also includes process tools and templates, 
training and awareness modules, quality assurance and quality control procedures, scorecard 
developments, risk identification and mitigation.  Tr. 1159-1160.  Confidential Exhibit 226 
(GSP-5R) contains a detailed breakdown of costs and supports the $250,000 funding request 
contained in the Gas Safety Panel’s direct testimony.  The Company has fully supported its 


--- Page 223 ---
 
215 
 
requested $250,000 funding for this program, which should be included in the Company’s gas 
revenue requirement.   
I. 
Community Gas Emergency Response Drill Program 
In the 2021 Rate Plan, the Commission allowed the Company to earn a PRA of four BPs 
for each drill conducted with municipalities and first responders, with a limit of two drills per 
year.  The Company proposed no changes to this program.  Staff agrees that the Community Gas 
Emergency Response Drill Program has the objective to conduct a municipality-wide gas 
emergency drill that evaluates the coordinated response of Company and municipal agencies to a 
gas event and agrees that the Company should continue its current program while earning a PRA 
of four BPs per drill with a cap of two drills per year.  Tr. 2710-2711.  The Company and Staff 
are in agreement that the PRA incentive mechanism for the Community Gas Emergency 
Response Drill Program should continue.

1. 
PSC Complaint Rate 
For the PSC Complaint Rate metric, the Company and Staff agree with maintaining the 
Company’s current minimum target of ≤ 1.0 and the subsequent tier ranges of > 1.0, ≥ 1.1, and 
≥ 1.2 but disagree on the NRA value that should be assigned to each tier146 and whether 
commodity price-related complaints should be excluded from the PSC Complaint Rate metric for 
NRA calculation purposes.   
The Company proposed to exclude commodity price-related complaints from the NRA 
calculation for the PSC Complaint Rate metric because such complaints are outside of the 
Company’s control.  Tr. 3012.  Therefore, complaints resulting from the price of electric and/or 
gas energy and/or capacity do not present a just cause for charging a complaint against the 
Company and do not show or point to any deficiency in the Company’s service to customers.  Tr. 
3012. 
 
144  The Company has previously explained that the imposition of NRAs in a one-year case is unlawful which 
would be compounded if the Company were subjected retroactively to metrics and NRAs that have an effective 
date prior to being reviewed and approved by the Commission. 
145  If Staff’s proposed measures begin in 2024, under Staff’s proposed tripling and quadrupling provisions, the 
Company would have hundreds of millions of dollars at risk in 2025. 
146  The maximum NRAs that should be assigned to each CSPI metric are addressed in Section X.A.   


--- Page 226 ---
 
218 
 
Staff opposes the exclusion and recommends that commodity price-related complaints 
continue to be included in the NRA calculation.  Tr. 4354-4356.  The basis for Staff’s opposition 
is two-fold.  First, Staff argues that the Company’s root cause analysis categories provided in 
Exhibit 352 (SCSP-1) at pages 130-246 do not include complaints specifically attributed to 
commodity prices and therefore, according to Staff, the Company has not demonstrated that it 
can accurately determine whether commodity prices are the root cause of a complaint.  Tr. 4355.  
Second, Staff contends that eliminating commodity price-related complaints from the metric 
calculation would not encourage proactive communication by the Company to its customers 
regarding commodity prices.  Tr. 4355-4356.  Staff is wrong on both fronts. 
While the Company’s root cause analysis categories provided in Exhibit 352 (SCSP-1) do 
not include commodity price-related complaints, Staff ignores that Staff’s discovery request 
provided in that exhibit requested information for 2018-2022 only.  Notably, in 2023, the 
Company started categorizing complaints as “Pricing-bill is correct” when the Company has 
determined that commodity prices are the root cause of a complaint. 147  See Exhibit 182 (CEP-
4R).  Specifically, through the complaint analysis process, the “Pricing-bill is correct” category 
is used when the Company has verified that actual meter reads have been billed bi-monthly and 
there are no other complaints regarding the handling of other account activity.  Tr. 3060.  
Notably, Staff concedes that commodity prices and commodity markets are outside of the 
Company’s control.  Tr. 4484, 4501.  Given that the Company has the ability to identify 
commodity price-related complaints, excluding such complaints, which are outside of the 
Company’s control, is completely appropriate.  As UIU Witness Collar notes, excluding 
 
147  The Company began including this category of complaints in reports to the Commission beginning in April 
2023.  See Case 21-M-0045 - In the Matter of the Petition of Central Hudson Gas and Electric Corporation to

Report (Filed Apr. 17, 2023). 


--- Page 227 ---
 
219 
 
commodity price-related complaints from the NRA calculation is also in alignment with other 
utilities.148  Tr. 493.   
Staff is also wrong that excluding commodity price-related complaints would discourage 
proactive communication by the Company to its customers regarding commodity prices.  First, 
Central Hudson’s efforts to communicate the impact of rising or variable supply prices on a 
customer’s overall bill has been ongoing for many years and is unrelated to the PSC Complaint 
Rate.  Tr. 3042.  As shown in Exhibit 183 (CEP-5R), the Company makes extensive efforts to 
communicate with customers on the components of electric and gas bills, including about supply 
prices, so that customers can actively manage their accounts and be informed consumers.  
Second, Staff’s argument ignores that a lack of communication regarding commodity prices 
would likely increase call volume, which may impact the Company’s ability to achieve its Call 
Answer Rate and would likely negatively impact the results of the Residential Customer 
Satisfaction Survey.  Tr. 3042-4043.  In fact, there is a 54% and 63% correlation between PSC 
Complaints received and electric and gas supply prices, respectively.  Tr. 3043.  Central 
Hudson’s communication to its customers regarding commodity prices will, therefore, still be 
linked to potential NRAs and the Company will still be incentivized to proactively communicate 
with customers on commodity prices. 
Given the Company’s demonstrated ability to identify commodity price-related 
complaints and the Company will continue to be incentivized to educate customers regarding 
commodity prices, commodity price-related complaints should be excluded from the Company’s 
PSC Complaint Rate metric.

2. 
Residential Customer Satisfaction 
The Company proposes to replace its existing Customer Satisfaction Survey with the 
Statewide Pilot Survey established in Case 15-M-0566.  Tr. 3012-3016.  This new survey would 
be administered via multiple channels including email, phone, text, web and mobile application, 
and field visits.  Tr. 3013.  It would be scored on a five-point scale using one question only.  Id.  
Additionally, the survey recipients would be split into two groups: General Account & Billing 
and High Priority Interactions.  Tr. 3014-3015.  The Company proposes a target of 3.08 and an 
NRA of 7.5 BPs for each of the target groups.  Tr. 3016. 
While Staff agrees with the Company’s proposal to move to the Statewide Pilot Survey 
and have two survey participant groups,149 Staff recommends that the Commission maintain the 
Company’s current survey target of 89% for each participant group (i.e., 89% of the responses be 
rated “4” for “Satisfied” or a “5” for “Very Satisfied”).  Tr. 4364.  Staff’s proposal to apply the 
existing 89% target applicable to the existing survey methodology is patently unreasonable and, 
again, treats Central Hudson in a different and discriminatory manner from other utilities.  
Beginning in the 1990s, every major New York gas and/or electric utility began 
implementing and reporting on a Residential Customer Satisfaction Survey.  While the specifics 
of each utility’s survey has been negotiated in rates cases over the years, Staff agrees that these 
historic surveys are generally the same.  Tr. 4511.  Then, in October 2018, the Commission 
ordered every major New York gas and/or electric corporation to implement the previously 
described statewide pilot customer satisfaction survey, commencing on January 1, 2019.150  
 
149  Staff and the Company also agree that that the surveys should be transaction-based and every transactional 
contact be included in one or the other participant groups.

3. 
Call Answer Rate 
The Company and Staff disagree on how the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 
Seconds metric should be calculated.  The basis of the dispute is a calculation error that the 
SCSP identified within the Company’s past calculations of this metric.  Tr. 4356-4363.  
Specifically, the SCSP states that the 2021 Rate Plan defines the calculation of this metric as the 
percentage of calls answered by a Company representative within 30 seconds of the customer’s 
request to speak to a representative between the hours of 8:00 PM and 4:30 PM Monday through 
Friday (excluding holidays), but that the Company has historically calculated this metric as the 
sum of the system-wide number of calls answered by a representative within 30 seconds 
(including callbacks) divided by total number of calls answered (including callbacks).  Tr. 4358.  
In rebuttal, the Company’s Customer Experience Panel agreed with Staff’s calculation 
corrections, subject to one exception.  Tr. 3046-3047.  The Company maintains that customer 
callbacks should be included within the numerator and denominator of the calculation.  Tr. 3047. 
The Company’s proposal is reasonable because it aligns with the intent and purpose of 
the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds metric.  A callback is an option available to 
customers who wish to have a Company representative call them back rather than wait in a hold 
status for a customer service representative.  Tr. 3047.  Despite the SCSP being incredulous that 
any customer would ever choose to receive a callback rather than have a call answered by a live 
representative (Tr. 4508-4509), the Company’s experience has proven that there are customers 
who use this option rather than have the potential to be on hold for an extended period of time.  
Tr. 3048.  The callback option is also available when customers are trying to reach the Company 
after hours.  See Exhibit 352 (SCSP-1) at page 1835.  Callbacks also have the benefit of reducing 
abandoned calls and allowing representatives to be better prepared for the conversation by 
providing them time to review the customer's information before making the return call.  Tr. 


--- Page 232 ---
 
224 
 
3048.  When the customer chooses the callback option and the Company does in fact call the 
customer back within two hours, the Company undoubtedly meets the customer’s expectation.  
During cross-examination, Staff agreed that meeting a customer’s expectation is a good thing.  
Tr. 4505. 
Including callbacks within the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds metric 
ensures that the customers that choose this option will be included in the overall measurement of 
this metric.  It also encourages rather than discourages the Company’s use of automated 
technology, a technology that is pervasive among all companies with a customer service 
component (Tr. 4510) and for which the Company’s customers have come to expect. 
Due to the calculation error identified by Staff, it is the Company’s position that the 
targets for the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds metric should be updated to align 
with the Company’s actual performance based on a modified Staff calculation methodology that 
includes customer callbacks for the period 2015 through 2019 in lieu of the Company’s 
performance utilizing the incorrect calculation for the period 2015 through 2019.  Tr. 3047-3048.  
This results in the following tiers for the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds metric:  < 
62.6%; ≤ 57.3%; and ≤ 52.0% for tiers one, two, and three, respectively (See Exhibit 186 (CEP-
8R)), instead of maintaining the Company’s existing tiers of: < 67.0%; ≤ 61.4%; and ≤ 55.8% as 
proposed by Staff.   
The Company also notes that there is a disagreement between the Company and Staff 
regarding the NRAs associated with the Company’s recalculated Percent of Calls Answered 
within 30 Seconds metric for years 2020-2022.  Staff alleges that the Company should have 
incurred an NRA for this metric in 2020, 2021 and 2022 in the amounts of $150,000, $600,000, 
and 10 BPs, respectively.  Tr. 4362.  In contrast, the Company believes that it should have 


--- Page 233 ---
 
225 
 
incurred NRAs for this metric in 2021 and 2022 only in the amounts of $600,000 and 10 BPs, 
respectively.  Tr. 3048.  The Company disagrees that it should be subject to a $150,000 NRA for 
its 2020 performance.  Tr. 3048.  As previously noted, the Company proposes that these NRAs 
should be utilized as a rate moderator to offset costs associated with hiring the incremental 
resources necessary for achieving targeted customer service performance levels during the Rate 
Year.   
Regardless of the total amount of the NRAs at play, the Company also disagrees with 
Staff’s proposed allocation of such NRAs to the Company’s electric and gas businesses.  Staff 
noted that the NRAs for all three years would be allocated $1.412 million to electric and $0.605 
million to gas.  Tr. 3944.  Staff appears to have erroneously used a 70%/30% (electric/gas) 
allocation instead of the actual allocation in place within each calendar year.  If it is determined 
that the Company should have incurred NRAs in each of the three years, the allocation should be 
$1.486 million to electric and $0.531 million to gas.  If it is determined that the Company should 
have incurred NRAs in just 2021 and 2022 as the Company contends, the allocation should be 
$1.366 million to electric and $0.501 million to gas. 
4. 
Estimated Bills 
The SCSP recommends that the Commission impose a new Estimated Bill metric with an 
associated NRA.  Tr. 4368.  For purposes of this metric, an estimated bill occurs when the utility 
renders a bill based on an estimated reading because it did not obtain an actual reading at the 
time of a regularly scheduled meter reading.  Tr. 4366.  The estimated bill metric would be 
calculated as a percentage calculated by dividing the number of estimated bills by the number of 
scheduled actual readings.  Tr. 4367.  Under Staff’s rigid proposal, the only estimated bills that 
would be excluded from the calculation of the metric would be regularly scheduled off-month 
estimated readings and customer readings.  Tr. 4540.  Estimated bills due to circumstances 


--- Page 234 ---
 
226 
 
beyond the Company’s control, including estimated bills due to no access, would be counted 
toward the metric.  Tr. 4540.  Staff recommends the metric follow a four-tier structure with an 
associated basis point NRA value for each tier as follows: 
Target 
NRA 
< 1% 
0 
1% - 4.5% 
8 
> 4.5% - 10.0% 
14 
> 10.0% 
18 
 
Assuming NRAs are appropriate in a one-year litigated case (they are not), the Company 
agrees in concept with a new Estimated Bill metric, but it disagrees with Staff’s proposed targets 
and associated NRA values.  Tr. 3051, 3057.  The Company also recommends that further 
exclusions should apply to the calculation of the metric.  Tr. 3053-3054.   
Staff’s proposed Estimated Bill metric tiers are inappropriate because the SCSP grossly 
deviated from Staff’s traditional methodology for determining CSPI metric targets.  Exhibit 642.  
“To set the metric targets, Staff typically calculates a utility’s historical average performance 
using between 36 and 60 months of data.  Staff then sets the minimum target two standard 
deviations below that historical average.”  Tr. 4351, lines 8-10.  Staff confirmed that for a metric 
such as Estimated Bills where better performance equates to a lower number, Staff’s traditional 
methodology would result in a minimum target – at which no NRAs would apply – set at two 
standard deviations above the historical average.  Tr. 4481-4482.   
In this instance, however, Staff: 
proposed the tiers based off of the three-year average from 2020-2022, which 
includes the Company’s billing system transition, plus two standard deviations, 
which equals 10.17 percent, and rounded to 10 percent.  Because the Company has 
committed to implementing monthly meter reading within the Rate Year, rather 
than a minimum target of 0 percent, the Panel recommended a minimum target of 
1 percent for the first tier based upon the three-year standard deviation of .97 


--- Page 235 ---
 
227 
 
percent rounded to 1 percent.  The second and third tiers then split the difference 
between the minimum of 1 percent and maximum of 10 percent. 
Exhibit 642. 
As noted above, even assuming the appropriate three-year period to set the Estimated Bill 
is 2020-2022, which it is not, under Staff’s traditional methodology, the minimum target would 
be set at 10% (two standard deviations above the three-year average) not the unreasonably low 
1% recommended by Staff.   
As noted in the quoted text above, Staff’s proposed target of 1% represents the value of 
the three-year standard deviation – not the result of applying the standard deviation to the 
Company’s performance – but the value of the standard deviation itself.  There is simply no basis 
to set the minimum target at that value.  A standard deviation is a statistical measurement that 
shows how much variation there is from the average.  In other words, it measures how far apart 
numbers are in a data set – it has no relationship to actual performance.  Staff’s recommendation 
to set a minimum target based on the value of a standard deviation is absurdly illogical. 
Since the Estimated Bill metric with associated NRAs would be new to the Company, the 
targets should be based on Staff’s traditional methodology for determining CSPI targets that uses 
a five-year historical performance average with tiers set at intervals of two standard deviations.  
Tr. 3050.  In this instance, given the impacts that the COVID-19 Pandemic and the SAP-CIS 
transition have had on estimated bills, the appropriate five-year period to base the target upon is 
2015-2019.  Tr. 3050.  The average over that five-year period is 2.1% with a standard deviation 
of .70%.  Exhibit 197 (CEP-9R).  Thus, the minimum target should be set at 3.5%.  This 
approach would result in a four-tier structure with an associated basis point NRA value for each 
tier as follows:

B. 
Negative Revenue Adjustments 
1. 
Maximum BP Per Metric 
Staff recommends that the Commission establish NRAs set at a maximum of 18 BPs for 
each of the following metrics: PSC Complaint Rate, Customer Satisfaction Survey, Percentage of 
Calls Answered within 30 Seconds, and the newly proposed Estimated Bills.  Tr. 4376.  The total 
maximum basis point exposure (excluding any tripling or quadrupling provision) would thus be 
72 BPs under Staff’s proposal.  Using Staff’s one basis point value of $0.161 million (Tr. 4376), 
the total amount at risk under Staff’s proposal (again excluding any tripling or quadrupling 
provision) is approximately $11.6 million.   
Staff has not justified a need to increase the maximum CSPI NRA basis point exposure 
from its current level of 42 BPs.  A maximum NRA exposure level of 42 BPs equates to 
approximately 5% of Staff’s proposed ROE of 9.2% or 4% of the Company’s proposed 9.8% 
ROE and would be considered a material financial impact and provides significant motivation 
for avoidance.  Tr. 3057.  Increasing the Company’s NRA exposure is also improper because 
Staff is unwilling to adjust the Company’s CSPI metric targets to reflect current circumstances.  
Tr. 4352.  In fact, when pushed, the SCSP took the overly harsh and inflexible position that it 
would never be appropriate to make CSPI targets less stringent, even due to circumstances that 
are beyond the Company’s control.  Tr. 4483.  Staff’s unwillingness to relax the CSPI standards 
for any reason makes their recommendation to increase the Company’s CSPI NRA exposure all 
the more unreasonable.  If the Commission imposes CSPI NRAs in this case, the maximum 


--- Page 240 ---
 
232 
 
exposure of 42 BPs should be maintained and spread out evenly among the four CSPI metrics 
(i.e., a maximum NRA of 10.5 BPs for each metric).  Tr. 3057. 
2. 
Tripling / Quadrupling Provisions 
Staff’s CSPI recommendation goes far beyond increasing the total amount at risk from 42 
to 72 BPs.  Staff also recommends that the “Commission reinstate the tripling and quadrupling 
provisions for customer service NRAs when the Company’s performance results in it incurring 
an NRA in multiple years,” which were established in the Fortis, Inc. acquisition proceeding 
(Case 12-M-0192).  Tr. 4378.  In reality, Staff is not simply looking to reinstate those provisions 
but instead is seeking significant modifications to those provisions that would make them far 
more punitive than the prior doubling and tripling provisions.160  Under Staff’s recommendation, 
the NRAs would triple if the targets for two metrics are missed in one year, and then quadruple if 
one or more metric targets are missed the following year.  Tr. 4379.  For example: 
If the Company misses two targets, e.g., for the Call Answer Rate and PSC Complaint Rate 
metrics, in 2024, the value of both missed NRAs would triple for 2024. If the Company 
then meets its targets for those two metrics in calendar year 2025, but misses its Call 
Answer Rate performance target in that same year, the NRA for this missed metric would 
quadruple because it would be the second year in a row in which the Company missed a 
CSPI target. 
Tr. 4379, lines 14-24.  As a result of Staff’s tripling and quadrupling provisions, Central 
Hudson’s total CSPI NRA exposure would rise from 72 BPs to an astonishing 288 BPs (Tr. 
4490-91), representing a 586% increase from current levels.  Tr. 3054.  Staff acknowledged that 
other utilities are subject to a maximum CSPI NRA exposure of 60 BPs.  Tr. 4496.  Increasing 
 
160  Under the order in Case 12-M-0192, the existing CSPI NRAs were doubled, and then tripled during a period of 
dividend restriction under and quadrupled if performance targets were missed three times in any five-year 
period.  Case 12-M-0192 - Joint Petition of Fortis Inc. et al. and CH Energy Group, Inc. et al. for Approval of 
the Acquisition of CH Energy Group, Inc. by Fortis Inc. and Related Transactions, Order Authorizing 
Acquisition Subject to Conditions at 20-21 (Jun. 26, 2013).  Under that quadrupling provision, the total CSPI 
NRA exposure was $7.6 million, which is a fraction of the $46.4 million Staff recommends in this case.

Central Hudson’s CSPI NRA exposure by more than 400% as compared to other utilities is 
discriminatory and unwarranted.  
Staff’s proposed potential maximum CSPI NRA level is excessive and overly punitive.  
The draconian nature of Staff’s recommended tripling and quadrupling provisions are best 
highlighted by Staff’s discovery response to CH-055 (Exhibit 640), which addressed the

of the SAP-CIS transition, which form the basis for Staff’s recommendation (Tr. 4378-4379), do

Customer Satisfaction will be accompanied by a higher number of PSC Complaints being

another and that the tripling of NRA values for missing more than one NRA in a given year is 
neither just nor appropriate. 
Similarly, the CSPI metrics are strongly correlated with commodity price fluctuations and 
an NRA should not be tripled due to market conditions beyond the Company’s control.  For 
example, electric supply price has a correlation of 58% with Customer Satisfaction, 54% with 
PSC Complaints, and 16% with the number of calls the Company receives that request to speak 
with a Customer Service Representative.  Tr. 3056.  Additionally, gas supply price has a 
correlation of 71% with Customer Satisfaction, 63% with PSC Complaints, and 32% with the 
number of calls the Company receives that request to speak with a Customer Service 
Representative.  Tr. 3056.  Again, this demonstrates that in times of commodity price increases it 
is more likely that the Company will fail one or more of SCSP’s proposed CSPI metrics than
