C. 
Customer Bill Credits 
As an initial matter, there is no legal authority for Staff’s recommendation that the 
Company be required to provide a $20 estimated bill credit, a $20 adjusted bill credit or a $20 
adjusted community distributed generation (“CDG”) bill credit.  Because such credits would be 
at shareholder expense, they would result in a forfeiture and absent Company agreement there 
needs to be affirmative legislative authority for such a charge, such as the $25 and $50 credits 
found in Public Service Law § 35(2) for not timely reestablishing service.  Without such 
authority, the credits proposed by Staff lack a lawful basis.162   
 
162  Since the Company has voluntarily agreed to a $20 missed appointment bill credit, the legal authority for such a 
credit is not at issue. 


--- Page 244 ---
 
236 
 
1. 
Estimated Bill Credit  
Staff recommends that the Commission require the Company to implement a $20 
estimated bill credit.  Tr. 4370.  Staff would have this credit apply despite that estimated bills are 
authorized under 16 NYCRR § 11.13 and the Company’s tariffs, and despite that estimated bills 
are normal, required and expected within every billing system.  Tr. 3058.  Not only does 16 
NYCRR § 11.13 authorize the Company to issue estimated bills, it also provides customers 
redress for when an estimated bill significantly understates that actual amount owed by the 
customer: 
Where a utility has submitted an estimated bill or bills to a residential customer that 
understate the actual amount of money owed by such customer for the period when 
estimated bills were rendered by more than 50 percent or $100, whichever is 
greater, the utility shall notify the customer in writing that he or she has the right to 
pay the difference between the estimated charges and the actual charges in regular 
monthly installments tailored to the customer’s financial circumstances over a 
reasonable period.163 
 
To the extent the customer is harmed by the estimated bill, the customer already has a remedy 
under the Commission’s regulations.  It may be for that reason that no other utility currently 
provides an estimated bill credit.  See Exhibit 179 (CEP-1R) at 3. 
The stated basis for the estimated bill credit is the unsupported claim that “[c]ustomers 
have been consistently harmed by the Company’s inability to provide accurate bills since the 
billing system transition.”  Tr. 4371, lines 6-7.  There is no record support that demonstrates 
precisely how customers have been harmed by receiving estimated bills or by the increased 
frequency in issuing estimated bills.  In fact, Staff’s estimated bill credit would apply without 
regard to the variation between the estimated bill and what the actual bill would have been.  In 
other words, it would apply regardless of any potential harm the customer would incur.  For 
 
163  16 NYCRR § 11.13(f). 


--- Page 245 ---
 
237 
 
example, if the estimated bill varied from what the actual bill would have been by $1, the $20 
charge would still apply.  Tr. 4541.  Clearly the estimated bill credit is thus intended to punish 
the Company rather than to redress customer harm.  
Given the lack of harm identified in the record, Staff bases its recommended $20 
estimated bill credit on the existing $20 bill credit for Missed Appointments.  Staff’s reliance on 
the $20 bill credit for Missed Appointment is misplaced.  A missed appointment has a unique 
customer impact and is more controllable by the Company.  Tr. 3061.  In contrast, estimated bills 
are driven by a large number of factors many of which are not within the control of the 
Company.  Tr. 3061.  Furthermore, SCSP provides no rationale for equating the customer 
inconvenience, customer cost, or Company control associated with the Missed Appointment bill 
credit to the estimated bill credit.   
The establishment of an estimated bill credit is also redundant to the Estimated Bill 
metric discussed earlier and results in an overly punitive mechanism especially during periods 
when the Company is meeting the Estimated Bill metric performance target.  Staff admits that, 
when making its recommendation, it did not take into consideration or forecast how many 
estimated bill credits the Company is expected to issue in the Rate Year.  Tr. 4542.  As shown in 
Exhibit 188 (CEP-10R), at the average level of occurrences experienced during 2018 and 2019 
prior to the go-live of the SAP-CIS, this $20 bill credit would have resulted in an annual 
financial impact of $1.4 million.164  At times of normal operations, such a penalty is 
inappropriate and overly punitive. 
 
164  In contrast, the Company only had 37 missed appointments during those two years as reported in the 
Performance Indicator Reports filed in Case 15-M-0566 resulting in a total financial impact of $740. 


--- Page 246 ---
 
238 
 
If the Commission determines an estimated bill credit is appropriate, for the reasons 
discussed in Section X.A.4, the Commission should more clearly define what would be 
considered a no access situation that would result in an exclusion to the estimated bill credit and 
the Commission should find that the credit should not apply when estimated bills are issued 
during abnormal operating conditions that are outside of the Company’s control.  
2. 
Adjusted Bill Credit 
Staff’s recommended adjusted bill credit is equally infirm.  Similar to the issuance of 
estimated bills, the Company is authorized to issue adjusted bills under the Commission’s 
regulations.  Specifically, 16 NYCRR § 11.14 allows the Company to render backbills under 
certain circumstances.  16 NYCRR § 11.14 also provides customers redress when the adjusted 
bill increases the amount due by $100 dollars or more: 
A utility issuing a billing adjustment increase of $100 or more under paragraphs 
(2), (3) and (4) of subdivision (b) of this section shall notify the customer in writing 
that he or she has the right to pay the adjusted bill in regular monthly installments 
tailored to the customer's financial circumstances over a reasonable period. An 
adjustment to increase previously rendered bills more than 12 months after the time 
service was provided, pursuant to paragraphs (b)(2), (3) and (4) of this section, shall 
be made within four months of the final resolution of the billing dispute.165 
 
To the extent the customer is harmed by the adjusted bill, the customer already has a remedy 
under the Commission’s regulations.  Notably, no other utility currently issues an adjusted bill 
credit to customers.  Exhibit 179 (CEP-1R) at 2. 
Staff argues that the implementation of a $20 bill credit for each adjusted bill would 
provide an immediate and consistent incentive for the Company to ensure that its bills are 
rendered correctly the first time.  Tr. 4381.  Staff ignores that the Company already has an 
incentive to do so because adjusted bills are likely to lead to increased call volume and 
 
165  16 NYCRR §11.14(c). 


--- Page 247 ---
 
239 
 
complaints thereby impacting the Calls Answered within 30 Seconds and PSC Complaint Rate 
metrics, respectively.  Staff’s proposed adjusted bill credit also ignores that the percent of 
Company bills requiring adjustment is currently down from its peak of approximately 10% (Tr. 
2995) and is now within normal operating levels.  Tr. 3038.   
Like the estimated bill credit, there is no record support that demonstrates precisely how 
customers have been harmed by receiving adjusted bills or by the increased frequency in issuing 
adjusted bills.  Also like the estimated bill credit, Staff’s adjusted bill credit would apply without 
regard to the amount of the adjustment or the amount of harm incurred.  For example, if the bill 
adjustment was only $1, the $20 charge would still apply.  Tr. 4542-4543.  Again, as with the 
other proposed bill credits, Staff’s adjusted bill credit is purely punitive and is not intended to 
redress customer harm.  In addition, for the same reasons it is inappropriate to base the estimated 
bill credit on the $20 credit for Missed Appointments, it is also inappropriate to base the adjusted 
bill credit on that same amount. 
The establishment of an adjusted bill credit is also an overly punitive mechanism given 
that adjusted bills can be a normal and expected part of operations.  Tr. 3058.  As shown in 
Exhibit 188 (CEP-10R), at the average level of occurrences experienced during 2018 and 2019 
prior to the go-live of the SAP-CIS, the $20 adjusted bill credit would have resulted in an annual 
financial impact of $1.0 million.  This excessive penalty should not be imposed for normal 
operating actions that are authorized under the Commission’s regulations.  
The fact that Staff’s adjusted bill credit proposal does not include any exclusions further 
demonstrates how unreasonable it is.  It is patently unreasonable to provide an adjusted bill credit 
to a customer whose culpable conduct (e.g., theft of service) resulted in the adjusted bill as 
Staff’s proposal would have the Company do.  Tr. 4543.  Moreover, Staff’s proposal contravenes 


--- Page 248 ---
 
240 
 
the Commission’s acknowledgement that when reporting on adjusted bills “treatment of bill 
adjustments related to theft of service, shared meters or ESCO bill adjustments should not be 
included in the metric, as they are not under a utility’s control.”166  If the Commission determines 
an adjusted bill credit is appropriate, suitable exclusions must be recognized. 
3. 
Adjusted CDG Bill Credit 
Staff recommends the Commission direct the Company to provide a CDG adjusted bill 
credit on top of the aforementioned adjusted bill credit for any adjusted CDG bill, which would 
result in a combined $40 bill credit for every CDG customer receiving an adjusted bill.  Tr. 4427.  
Establishment of an adjusted bill credit and a CDG adjusted bill credit is by definition redundant 
and overly punitive.  Moreover, the arguments set forth above for why an adjusted bill credit 
should not be adopted apply equally for rejecting Staff’s proposed CDG adjusted bill credit. 
The putative basis for Staff’s recommended CDG adjusted bill credit is to “compensate 
that customer for the inconvenience of receiving multiple bills” and “provide sufficient incentive 
for the Company to reduce the levels of adjusted bills rather than issuing high levels of credits.”  
Tr. 4427, lines 12-18.  Staff’s rationale for the CDG adjusted bill credit is unavailing.  Staff 
concedes that as of August 2023 the number of adjusted CDG bills are about 1/10 of what they 
were in March 2022.  Tr. 4417, 4558-4559.  Staff also recognizes that the Company “continues 
to work to reduce the number of customers receiving delayed bills and is actively implementing 
the automation of CDG customer bills.”  Tr. 4424, lines 16-19.  Given current levels of adjusted 
CDG bills as well as the efforts that have been implemented to reduce the number of 
adjustments, the Company needs no further “incentive” through imposition of a bill credit to 
reduce the number of adjusted CDG bills.  Staff also suggests that a CDG adjusted billing credit 
 
166  Case 15-M-0566, Order Adopting Revisions to Customer Service Reporting Metrics at 12 (Aug. 4, 2017). 


--- Page 249 ---
 
241 
 
is necessary to ensure that the minimum acceptable standards for accurate billing are being met 
(Tr. 4428), but Staff was unable to identify what those minimum acceptable standards are.  Tr. 
4561.   
Like the adjusted bill credit, Staff’s CDG adjusted bill credit does not include any 
exclusions, such as exclusions for adjusted CDG bills that are due to fault of the CDG provider.  
Tr. 4560.  Importantly, there are reasons that adjustments to CDG bills may be required that are 
outside of the control of the Company and should be excluded from any potential CDG adjusted 
bill credit.  “The CDG program requires collaboration between the utility and the host.  It also 
requires understanding of the program by the subscriber.  Subscriber additions, issues related to 
host enrollment and disenrollment can and do occur requiring rebilling of subscribers.”  Tr. 
3059, lines 13-16.  Adjusted CDG bills for these reasons should be excluded from any CDG bill 
credit the Commission may adopt. 
Staff’s recommendation is also misguided because it overlooks the complexity of CDG 
billing and that CDG hosts continue to ask the Commission for changes to allowable billing 
designs that if approved will need to be accommodated through manual billing process that are 
subject to human error and may result in adjusted bills.  Tr. 3059-3060.  These future mandated 
billing system changes will increase the number of adjusted and CDG adjusted bills and result in 
unfair financial impacts to the Company under Staff’s recommendation.  Staff’s testimony also 
suggests that CDG billing issues are unique to Central Hudson, which is not the case.  The 
Commission is looking into CDG billing issues for all utilities on a generic basis in Case 14-M-
0224.  In that proceeding, stakeholders will propose an NRA mechanism to be tied directly to the 
utilities’ CDG crediting and billing performances.  Tr. 4414.  CDG billing credits are also being 
considered as part of that proceeding.  Given that Staff is proposing that any Commission order 


--- Page 250 ---
 
242 
 
or directives issued in Case 14-M-0224, including those related to CDG billing and CDG 
crediting, would supersede any outcome in these rate cases (Tr. 4432), the Commission should 
decline to get ahead of the outcome of its generic proceeding by adopting Staff’s recommended 
$20 CDG adjusted bill credit, particularly since no other utility is currently required to provide 
such a $20 bill credit.  Tr. 4563. 
4. 
Discretionary Customer Credit 
Central Hudson has historically had a discretionary customer crediting program, which 
permits a Contact Center Representative or a Customer Outreach employee to use discretion to 
suggest a customer be issued a credit to resolve an issue raised by the customer and this 
suggestion is then reviewed and approved by Supervision.  Exhibit 495.  Central Hudson does 
not track the specific reason that a credit is provided to a customer, but Central Hudson does 
maintain two general categories of credits provided to customers:  Miscellaneous Adjustment 
Credit (discretionary credit for customer raised issue not specific to a fee on a bill); and Returned 
Payment or Late Fee Credit (discretionary credit for a fee on a customer bill).  Exhibit 495. 
The Company does not have current written procedures for discretionary credits, but 
rather information regarding this process is provided through on-the job training.  In general, 
courtesy discounts may be applied based on individual circumstances to provide resolution to 
situations that are outside of the customer’s control.  A 10% discount is the standard courtesy 
given, although it may be adjusted higher or lower based on the individual circumstances.  
Exhibit 495. 
Central Hudson has provided discretionary credits for many years and these credits are 
operating expenses or reductions to other operating revenue that have historically been included 
in revenue requirements and are treated as such in the determination of regulatory ROE and the 
measurement of shared earnings.  Exhibit 495.  There is $50,000 for this program included in 


--- Page 251 ---
 
243 
 
revenue requirements.  Tr. 822.  These credits are not recorded to delivery revenue accounts, and 
therefore are not included in the calculation of the RDM.  Exhibit 495. 
D. 
Monthly Meter Reading  
As part of direct testimony, the Company proposed a transition to monthly meter reading 
from the present bi-monthly structure, including an incremental annual cost of approximately 
$4.4 million composed of recuring and one-time O&M costs as well as the return on and of 
approximately $1.8 million of a capital expenditures.  Tr. 2999-3000.  That plan was initially 
filed on January 17, 2023 in Case 22-M-0645 and provides for a phased roll-out of monthly 
meter reading.  Following an Interim Agreement between Staff and the Company in Case 22-M-
0645, the Company accelerated the timeline for the transition to monthly meter reading and 
provided revised cost estimates as part of its update filing.  While the revised plan reduced the 
overall estimated annual cost to $4.1 million, the acceleration resulted in an increase to the Rate 
Year costs primarily attributable to the advanced timing of hiring incremental contracted meter 
readers. 
While Staff “generally supports a transition to monthly meter reading for Central 
Hudson,” it curiously recommends that the Commission exclude the Company’s proposed costs 
for monthly meter reading in customer rates “at this time.”  Tr. 4393.  Staff offers two rationales 
for its recommended disallowance of monthly meter reading costs at this time, both of which are 
unavailing.   
First, Staff improperly speculates that the yet-to-be released independent monitor’s 
written report regarding the Company’s monthly meter reading proposal may materially affect 
the Company’s monthly meter reading proposal.  Tr. 4394.  At this time, however, there is no 
indication that the independent monitor’s report will lead to material changes or any changes to 
the Company’s plan.  Tr. 3065.  The Commission could also develop mechanisms to protect 


--- Page 252 ---
 
244 
 
customers from any changes to the final cost of the monthly meter reading plan associated with 
the final independent monitor’s report.  Tr. 3065-3066.  Staff’s reliance on the independent 
monitor’s report as a basis for disallowing monthly meter reading costs is lacking.   
Second, Staff is wrong that the Company has not fully planned the roll-out of this 
initiative, including the customer education component.  The SCSP asserts that the Company’s 
response to DPS-546, included within Staff’s Exhibit 352 (SCSP-1), supports its position (Tr. 
4394), but that response relates to customer communication rather than customer education.  Tr. 
3066.  In reality, the Company has developed a customer education and information campaign to 
communicate the implementation of monthly meter reading, as shown in Exhibit 189 (CEP-11R).  
This includes direct customer communications, including emails and print newsletters and direct 
mail, along with social media, digital communications, and paid advertising across multiple 
formats.  Tr. 3066-3067.  The customer education and information campaign also includes a 
timeline for when each communication channel will be launched and a target audience.  Exhibit 
189 (CEP-11R).  The Company unambiguously has a customer education plan in place. 
Third, Staff’s concern over the cost estimates and supporting analysis related to other 
components of the monthly meter reading proposal, including vehicle purchases, FTEs and 
contractor resources is misplaced.  Tr. 4395.  Although the SCSP states that operational 
efficiencies may exist (Tr. 2906), they provide no evidence or support for a specific level of 
operational efficiencies.  Tr. 3067.  In fact, the Company’s meter reading routes are currently 
designed and optimized based on geographical area and delaying the rollout of monthly meter 
reading to undertake a multi-year project to examine additional efficiencies is counterproductive.  
Tr. 3067-3068.   


--- Page 253 ---
 
245 
 
Staff’s concerns with the Company’s use of Precision Pipeline Solutions (“PPS”) for a 
portion of the monthly meter reading is also overblown.  Tr. 4395-4396.  The Company has 
contracted with PPS without issuing an RFP because presently there are no other meter reading 
companies located within its service territory.  Tr. 3068.  There is no evidence to suggest that 
PPS’s costs are unreasonable or do not align with other utility monthly meter reading vendors.  
Tr. 4548.  PPS meter reading employees are also the Company’s customers who work and live 
within the Company’s service area and therefore have a greater connection and concern for the 
area and our customer base.  Tr. 3067.  A new vendor would need to establish a business facility 
within the service territory as well as hire an entire workforce of management, trainers, and 
reading staff.  Tr. 3067.   
In addition, Staff’s apprehension regarding the Company’s plan to utilize EVs is puzzling 
given New York State’s well-known EV goals.  In any event, the planned purchase of EVs is 
only one component of the plan and does not provide justification for removal of all costs 
associated with the monthly meter reading rollout.  Tr. 3069. 
As of December 19, 2023, the monthly meter reading project is well underway.  Tr. 3064.  
The Company has completed all functional unit testing and performed an initial proof of concept, 
which includes reading meters for a small customer population for two consecutive months and 
validation of subsequent billing.  The monthly meter reading plan filed in Case 22-M-0645 fully 
lays out the timeline and the incremental costs and resources required for the transition to 
monthly billing.  Tr. 3067.  In fact, the Commission has already determined that customers will 
receive “the substantial benefit of a plan for the Company to begin reading meters on a monthly 
basis in relatively short order.”167  The Commission’s finding directly contradicts Staff’s position 
 
167 Case 22-M-0645, Order Adopting Terms of Interim Agreement at 7 (Aug. 18, 2023). 


--- Page 254 ---
 
246 
 
that the Company does not have a sufficient plan for this initiative.  Many stakeholders have 
asked the Company to expedite the rollout of monthly meter reading to reduce the number of 
estimated bills and the Company has already begun the phased rollout of this plan and has 
incurred expenses that it has not asked for recovery of within this proceeding.  Tr. 3067.   
Given Staff’s concession that monthly meter reading may help improve the Company’s 
CSPIs (Tr. 4545), which is a chief concern in this case, it is mystifying that Staff recommends 
the Commission disallow cost recovery for this item.  The Commission should reject Staff’s 
proposed disallowance of these costs and all associated incremental FTEs and associated 
contracted resources.  
E. 
Collections Activities  
Staff argues that the Company has not provided “a clear timeline as to when it will 
resume residential collections and service terminations” and recommends that the Commission 
require the Company to conduct unspecified additional testing of the Company’s billing 
processes before residential service terminations occur.  Tr. 4404, 4406.  Staff is wrong that the 
Company has not developed a clear timeline for when it will resume residential collections and 
service terminations.  They are scheduled to start in April 2024.  Tr. 3078.  Given that residential 
collections and service terminations will recommence prior to the issuance of a Commission 
order in this proceeding, there is no need for unspecified additional testing.  
It is essential for the Company to resume residential collection and service termination 
activities as soon as possible.  Customer arrears balances have grown significantly since the 
Company suspended collections activity for all customers in March of 2020, with residential 
arrears greater than 60 days as of November 30, 2023, totaling $116.1 million.  Tr. 3070.  
Currently, Central Hudson is pursuing soft collections of residential arrears via phone, letter and 
e-mail campaigns, is working with county assistance agencies to promote the Home Energy 


--- Page 255 ---
 
247 
 
Assistance Program (“HEAP”) and Energy Affordability Program (“EAP”), and providing the 
notifications required for residential customers to secure emergency HEAP benefits.  Tr. 3071.  
In April 2024, the Company will commence a phased rollout of collections activities and service 
terminations in conjunction with its monthly meter reading initiative.  Tr. 3071.  Central Hudson 
will resume residential collections with a manual approach and will only send the Final 
Termination Notice on months where the customer has an actual read.  Tr. 3072.  The Company 
will start with a small group of customers and then scale to all customers (Tr. 3129) in 
accordance with the timeline laid out within Exhibit 190 (CEP-12R).  The Company has 
implemented a robust communication plan to alert all stakeholders about the resumption of the 
Company’s formal collections processes.  Tr. 3077-3078.  Communication includes targeted 
reminders (letters, emails, text messages, outbound phone calls, in-person field visits) to 
customers with an AR balance since August 2022 that alert the non-payers that terminations are 
coming.  Tr. 3078.  In resuming residential collections and service terminations, the Company 
will also apply lessons learned from the manual collections and service termination approach, 
which has proven to be very effective.  Tr. 3075-3077.   
There is no basis for the Commission to delay the resumption of residential collections 
and service terminations.  In fact, by the time the Commission issues an order in these 
proceedings, Staff’s recommendation will be moot because such activities will already have 
begun.  It is also unwise to delay residential collections and service terminations any longer 
because, as Staff acknowledges, collection and termination activities area a primary tool to 
control arrears and any further delay would exacerbate the Company’s already large arrears 
balances.  Tr. 4549.   


--- Page 256 ---
 
248 
 
F. 
Extreme Heat Protections 
As part of the 2021 Rate Plan, the Company agreed to suspend electric residential service 
terminations for non-payment of service if the National Weather Service heat index was 
forecasted to reach 93 degrees or higher, including on the calendar day before, in the Company’s 
service territory; or the actual heat index reached 93 degrees or higher on any given day.  Tr. 
656.  PULP Witness Yates’s recommendation to modify those extreme heat protections such that 
electric service terminations would be suspended during periods where the heat index is 
forecasted to hit 90 degrees (including not terminating one calendar day before and two calendar 
days after 90-degree days) are misguided.  Tr. 656.  So too is PULP’s recommendation for 
additional protections for customers residing within a “Heat Island” at temperatures of 85 
degrees. 
First, the Company is not able to ascertain any scientific basis for lowering the 
Company’s threshold for protection from service termination from 93 to 90 degrees or 85 
degrees within more densely populated areas.  Tr. 3082.  Second, the Company’s existing 
temperature threshold also factors in the heat index, so it is highly likely that terminations will be 
suspended during heat events where the surface temperature is below 93 degrees.  Tr. 3082.  
Third, during cross-examination, Mr. Yates conceded that lowering the temperature to 90 
degrees on a single day was not consistent with the threshold used to identify extreme heat 
conditions by federal government agencies such as the Federal Emergency Management Agency 
(“FEMA”) and the Department of Homeland Security as he originally testified.  Tr. 669-671; 
Exhibit 493.  Fourth, Mr. Yates gave no consideration to the administrative difficulties or 
additional cost that may be involved with tracking which days or which areas would be covered 
by his recommendation.  Tr. 671.  Fifth, with respect to the recommendation for heat islands, Mr. 
Yates admitted that neither PULP nor any other entity has determined if there are, in fact, heat 


--- Page 257 ---
 
249 
 
islands within the Company’s service territory.  Tr. 671-672.  The Company would thus have to 
undertake a study to determine the extent to which there are heat islands in its service territory 
but no funding for such a study has been recommended in this case.  Tr. 672. 
The Company’s existing protections represent a balanced approach to collection which 
protects vulnerable customers but mitigates uncollectible expense burdens for all customers.  Mr. 
Yates’s recommendations lack evidentiary support, would be infeasible to implement in the Rate 
Year, and thus should be rejected.

to customer outreach in Appendix 2.  
H. 
Economic Development 
Consistent with Cases 20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429, the Company has included $800,000

Manufacturers Program, the Manufacturing Productivity Program, and the Infrastructure for 
Manufacturers Program) or economic development rate discount programs (Excelsior Jobs 
Program).  Tr. 4460.   
Staff recommends eliminating the economic development for gas during the Rate Year, 
resulting in a rate year allowance of $800,000 for electric and zero for gas.168  Tr. 4462.  Staff 
contends that by ending gas incentives provided through this program, the remaining electric 
development grant program will better align with the CLCPA.  Tr. 4463.  To achieve this result, 
 
168  The Company and Staff are in agreement that the existing economic regulatory liability balances should be used

Staff recommends that the Company modify each of the existing programs to eliminate any

position to monitor performance and adherence by applicants to any state or county required

Credit Card Fees 
With respect to credit/debit card fees, the Company proposes to maintain the rate 
allowance established by the Commission in the 2021 Rate Plan in the Rate Year, and to assume 
that any variation will be captured in the continued symmetric deferral.  Tr. 4452.  This would 
allow customers to continue to pay their bills with a credit or debit card without incurring a fee 
from either the Company or a third-party processing agent.  This model is now common among 
New York State utilities.  Tr. 4452.   
Staff agrees that the Company should continue to maintain a rate allowance for 
credit/debit card fees but recommends that the Company re-evaluate the current vendor and 
associated processing costs and pursue a competitive bidding process for credit card merchant 
services prior to the expiration of the current vendor to include a proposal in the Company’s next 
rate filing.  Tr. 4453.  While the Company is not pursuing a new vendor at this time, it is open to 
considering this recommendation in the future.  Due to timing considerations, however, it may 
not be possible to include such a proposal in the Company’s next rate case filing.  The Company

also does not oppose Staff’s recommendation that the Company provide more information on its 
credit/debit card fee quarterly reports.  See Tr. 4454. 
J. 
Reporting Requirements 
The Company, Staff and UIU entered into a Billing Reporting Stipulation.  See Exhibit 
514.  The stipulation resolves all outstanding billing reporting requirement issues raised in 
testimony in these cases.  To assist Your Honors in assessing the Billing Reporting Stipulation, 
this section summarizes the parties’ litigation positions, presents a rational basis for the 
stipulation, and demonstrates that the stipulation is in the public interest.   
The SCSP testimony proposed several changes related to the CSPI metric report, 
including: 1) modifying the title of the annual report to Customer Service Performance Indicator 
Report to align the monthly CSPI reports with the annual CSPI reports established in rate cases; 
2) developing and reporting a granular Call Hold Time metric; 3) developing and reporting a 
granular Call Abandonment Rate; 4) developing and reporting a Call Handling Time metric; 5) 
enhanced reporting associated with estimated meter reading and estimated bills; and 6) 
continuation of the reporting set forth in the Commission’s Order Approving Revised Billing 
Estimation Methods.  Tr. 3062.  The SCSP also recommended that the Company file CDG 
customer billing reports on a quarterly basis with the Secretary to the Commission that include 
the total and percentage of CDG customer bills that have been issued within the following time 
frames: 1) 30 days or less; 2) greater than 30 days, but less than or equal to 60 days old; 3) 
greater than 60 days, but less than or equal to 90 days old; 4) greater than 90 days, but less than 
or equal to 120 days old; and 5) greater than 120 days, but less than or equal to 150 days.  
Tr. 4431.  Additionally, SCSP recommended the Company track and provide monthly reports on 


--- Page 261 ---
 
253 
 
the number of times a CDG customer bill is adjusted within one billing period, or 30 days.  
Tr. 4431. 
In rebuttal testimony the Company’s Customer Experience Panel agreed with the 
proposal to align the titles for the annual CSPI and the monthly CSPI but had concerns with the 
SCSP’s recommendations that the existing monthly CSPI reports be altered to include more 
granular data related to call abandonment rates, call hold times, call handling times, call answer 
rates, consecutive months of estimated billing and CDG customer bills that have been issued 
within specific time frames.  Tr. 3062-3063.  In particular, the Company noted that the additional 
reporting would require additional resources and an upgrade to the Company’s IVR system.  Tr. 
3063.  The Company’s Customer Experience Panel also agreed in concept with the CDG 
reporting recommendations but noted that additional resources would be needed to comply with 
them since they are incremental and involve manual processes.  Tr. 3064. 
Ultimately, the parties to the Billing Reporting Stipulation reached agreement on what 
billing reporting requirements would be applicable to the Company in the Rate Year.  See 
Exhibit 514.  In particular, the Billing Reporting Stipulation provides that, subject to 
implementation of the IVR modernization project, Central Hudson will track and report more 
granular data for call hold times, call abandonment, and the length of customer calls.  Id.  The 
Company will also provide more granular data regarding estimated meter reading and estimated 
bills.  Id.  The Company will continue the reporting requirements set forth in the Commission’s 
Order Approving Revised Billing Estimation Methods in Case 21-M-0045, which requires the 
Company to provide quarterly reports on which scheduled meter reads were not performed each 
month, with an explanation as to why it was outside of the Company’s control to perform the 
meter read.  At a minimum, the Company will report on the complaints received by the Company 


--- Page 262 ---
 
254 
 
associated with adjusted bills, estimated bills, high bills, inaccurate bills, and any complaints 
related to actual meter readings or bill estimates.  Id.  The stipulation also provides that the 
Company will file CDG customer billing reports on a quarterly basis with the Secretary to the 
Commission that provide detailed information on CDG customer billing delays.  The Company 
will also report on the incremental costs to bill CDG customers on an annual basis.  Id.  The 
Company also commits to providing status updates to Staff and any other interested party, of its 
collection efforts, including collection-related customer outreach and customer inquiries and/or 
complaints directly to Central Hudson regarding arrears balances.  Id.  The Company will track 
and report any customer inquiries made directly to the Company regarding collections, including 
late payment fees, final termination notices, or employee treatment of a customer.  Id.  Finally, a 
Company executive will provide a commitment and attestation, filed in these rate proceedings, 
that a process is in place and has been validated to prevent service terminations from occurring if 
a residential customer's account has not received an actual meter read prior to the issuance of a 
final termination notice.  Id.   
The Company, Staff and UIU, typically adverse parties, reached agreement on the billing 
reporting requirements that would be applicable in the Rate Year.  As demonstrated above, the 
Billing Reporting Stipulation is supported by the records and falls within the range of testimony 
filed by the parties.  Certain of the provisions reflect a position advocated by the Company or 
Staff in pre-filed testimony, while others represent a compromise among the parties.  The 
provisions are consistent with the PSL and, where applicable, Commission precedent.  The 
Billing Reporting Stipulation also strikes a fair balance between the interest of customers and the 
Company.  Given these factors, it is reasonable to conclude that had these issues been fully 
litigated, Your Honors and the Commission would have reached a similar result.  Accordingly, 


--- Page 263 ---
 
255 
 
the Billing Reporting Stipulation meets the public interest standard set forth in the Commission’s 
Settlement Guidelines and should be adopted by Your Honors and the Commission without 
modification.   
XI. 
MANAGEMENT AUDIT 
Management audits are a cooperative process among the auditor, Staff and the Company 
to investigate areas of potential savings and efficiencies.  Tr. 904.  The Public Service Law 
requires that, upon the application of a gas or electric corporation for a major change in rates, the 
Commission shall review the corporation’s compliance with the directions and recommendations 
made previously by the Commission as a result of the most recently completed management and 
operations audit.  Tr. 860.  In these proceedings, the Company and Staff differ on aspects of 
implementation and cost recovery for the Company’s most recent staffing, management and 
operations audits.   
A. 
2013 Staffing Audit 
Staff takes issue with the Company regarding Recommendation VII and VIII based on 
the Company’s response to DPS-256 (included in Exhibit 331 (SMAP-1).  Tr. 872, 831.  
Unfortunately, as the Company clearly identified in its rebuttal testimony, the response to DPS-
256 was incorrectly answered based on a misinterpretation of the question.  The Company 
Management Audit Panel fully acknowledged the confusion caused by its initial interpretation of 
DPS-256 and testified that “the costs identified in the Company’s response to DPS-256 are not 
specifically attributable to the implementation of Recommendations VII and VIII of the 2013 
audit but rather are follow on costs….”  Tr. 832, lines 6-9.   
Recommendation VII of the 2013 Staffing audit identifies the Company’s need to 
actively seek and establish outside training and recruitment alliances and measure effectiveness 
against training and recruitment goals.  Tr. 869.  The response to DPS-256 was not clear whether 


--- Page 264 ---
 
256 
 
the Company would incur an estimated $1.4 million in the Rate Year in connection with 
implementation of Recommendation VII of the Staffing Audit.  Tr. 869.   
However, more specific and further details of the $1.4 million were provided by the 
Company to Staff in response to DPS-754 (included in Exhibit 228 (MAP-2R)) and are 
composed of costs associated with DEI initiatives, enhanced training program offerings and costs 
for additional recruitment.  While the initial response resulted in some confusion, the Staff 
Management Audit Panel acknowledged that they were familiar with the Company’s response to 
DPS-754.  Tr. 896; Hearing Exhibit 228 (MAP-2R).  As demonstrated in that response, the costs 
actually being incurred by the Company are not related to implementing Staffing Audit 
Recommendation VII, but rather are normal operating costs tied to evolving the Company’s 
forward-looking training, recruitment, and workforce development needs.  Tr. 832-833.  As Staff 
conceded on the stand, normal operating costs associated with training, recruitment and 
workforce development are appropriate for inclusion in revenue requirement.  Tr. 896-897.  
Since these cost types are exactly the ones identified and detailed in the Company’s response to 
DPS-754 (Hearing Exhibit 228 (MAP-2R)), the $1.4 million was properly included in the 
revenue requirement.   
Staff’s testimony and position regarding Recommendation VIII of the Staffing Audit was 
also inadvertently impacted by the Company’s response to DPS-256.  Tr. 833.  Recommendation 
VIII related to enhancing the Company’s Human Resource Information System (“HRIS”).  Tr. 
833.  Unfortunately, the HRIS costs identified by the Company in DPS-256 were similarly in 
error to the extent that they implied a reference to the 2013 Staffing audit implementation costs.  
The Company Management Audit Panel corrected this error on the record.  Tr. 833.  The costs 
associated with Staffing Audit Recommendation VIII do not reflect implementation costs but 


--- Page 265 ---
 
257 
 
rather capture costs associated with further enhancements to leverage the system and to pay 
recurring license fees.  Id.  As a result, the $945,000 cost (Tr. 869) was also properly included as 
operational costs in the revenue requirement.  
B. 
2016 Management Audit 
The Company also recently participated in a comprehensive management and operations 
audit in Case 16-M-0001.  The Company is not aware of any open issues with Staff regarding 
this audit.  Tr. 866.  The Staff Management Audit Panel appears to agree as it noted in its 
testimony that on September 20, 2021, the Director of the Department’s Office of Accounting, 
Audits and Finance issued a letter to Central Hudson confirming that the Company had 
satisfactorily implemented all 47 recommendations included in the 2016 Management Audit.  Tr.

particular recommendation.  Tr. 906.

a. Recommendation 2.1 
Staff’s Management Audit Panel accepted at face value Overland’s assessment that the

fact that Central Hudson has prepared such an analysis using internal resources in the past.  Staff 
proposed to disallow the Company’s request for $200,000 (reduced from the Company’s initial 
estimated cost of $250,000) (Tr. 909) to retain an AMI BCA expert.  Tr. 889. 
Staff’s assertions regarding the Company’s alleged ability to conduct an AMI Benefit 
Cost Analysis in-house is not supported by the record.  As the Company Management Audit 
Rebuttal Panel testified, for the last AMI BCA in 2016, the Company engaged a third-party 
consultant, and these costs were included in the Company’s workpapers and served as the basis 
for the Company’s recovery.  Tr. 834.  When pushed, the Staff Management Audit Panel 
admitted that they were not AMI BCA experts and had no idea what type of expertise was 
required to conduct an AMI BCA.  Tr. 911.  The Staff Panel noted it had no independent 
knowledge and solely relied on Overland’s access to discovery and interviews – none of which 
 
171  Further justification for the FTEs discussed in this section can be found in Appendix 2.

are on the record.  Tr. 911.  Thus, the Staff Panel’s denial of the Company’s request for an 
outside expert on the grounds that the work could be done in-house lacked a factual basis.  
The Staff Accounting Panel next sought to reject the Company’s request based on

In these recommendations, Overland identified labor cost differences reflected as FTE

--- Page 268 ---
 
260 
 
Interestingly, Overland and Staff imply that the work to be performed by these 11.5 FTEs

incremental time and assume that there’s a direct relationship to expected incremental costs.”  Tr.

is certainly true in many cases, including this one.  As Staff conceded, the work capability for 
each existing employee is finite and there are “going to be times when additional tasks and work 
cannot be absorbed by the existing Company workforce.”  Tr. 916, Lines 6-8.  While Staff 
concedes it is simply deferring (with no independent knowledge) to Overland’s view that the 
implementation work can be absorbed by the existing workforce, Staff also admits that “it is 
possible” that additional tasks and work cannot be absorbed by the existing workforce.  Tr. 916.  
Such is the case here, where the Company is tasked with implementing 37 Management Audit 
recommendations developed in a multi-year audit process.   
Turning first to Recommendations 2.4 (Enterprise Risk Management (“ERM”)) and 2.5

actively back filling the position.  Overland’s recommendation is also inconsistent with the 


--- Page 269 ---
 
261 
 
various benefits listed by Overland, including increased documentation, more timely risk

recommendations, one full time FTE is needed and the full cost of $106,000 should be reflected 
in revenue requirement.  Tr. 835-836.

that are not known today.  Tr. 906.  Costs required to be incurred because of the Final 
Implementation Plan are valid costs of doing business and necessary expenditures for the 
Company to comply with the Management Audit’s findings.  Absent the grant of deferral 
treatment, some of these costs may be improperly stranded as they would be incurred after the 
revenue requirement is established in these proceedings.  As a result, deferral treatment of these 
costs (to the extent they are not yet knowable whether because of the timing of the 
Implementation Plan schedule or because the Commission has not issued its final approval of the 
2021 Management Audit), should be granted.  Tr. 843.

Management FTE and the Testing QA Analyst.  Given the lack of record evidence or a rationale 
for removal of these two FTEs, the Staff adjustment should be rejected.  
XII. 
EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT MECHANISMS 
A. 
DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits  
The Company proposed to replace its three existing Energy Efficiency EAMs with two 
new EAMs that would provide incentives to the Company to direct a higher proportion of gas 
and electric energy efficiency spending to projects located within disadvantaged communities 
(“DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs”).  Tr. 1417-1418.  The minimum target for the DAC 
Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs would be aligned with the CLCPA target of 35% of spending 
on clean energy and energy efficiency programs being directed towards disadvantaged 
communities, and the midpoint and maximum targets would be set at 40% and 45%, 
respectively.  Tr. 1419.  Staff recommends the Commission reject the Company’s proposed DAC 


--- Page 276 ---
 
268 
 
Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs arguing that these EAMs are foreclosed by the Commission’s 
July 20, 2023 Order in Case 18-M-0084.172  Tr. 1719-1721.  UIU recommends these EAMs be 
rejected by the Commission for the same reason.  Tr. 507. 
Staff and UIU misinterpret the July 2023 Order and the structure and intent of the 
Company’s proposed DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs.  Staff and UIU point to language 
in the July 2023 Order that states the Commission will not consider energy efficiency or building 
electrification EAMs proposed or included as part of any new rate case filing or joint proposal 
submitted after the effective date of the July 2023 Order and until such time as the Commission 
determines an appropriate path forward.173  Tr. 1719, 505-506.  Staff and UIU, however, ignore 
the greater context of the Commission’s discussion on EAMs in the July 2023 Order.  The 
Commission’s concern regarding energy efficiency and building electrification EAMs was 
focused on the Share-the-Savings EAM metrics, which reward utilities for achieving a lower cost 
per unit of lifetime energy savings than an agreed upon baseline.174  The Commission observed 
that EAMs focused on cost savings do not fully align with deeper, more expensive energy 
savings measures, and the fact that the metrics are based on a predetermined baseline creates the 
potential for “undeserved utility shareholder awards.”175  Further, the Commission noted that 
uncertainty regarding future budgets and targets compounds the issue.  
The Company’s proposed DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs are distinguishable 
from the EAMs discussed in the July 2023 Order.  First, the Company’s EAMs are not premised 
 
172  Case 18-M-0084 – In the Matter of a Comprehensive Energy Efficiency Initative, Order Directing Energy 
Efficiency and Building Electrification Proposals (Jul. 20, 2023) (the “July 2023 Order”). 
173  It is worth noting that the Company proposed to replace its existing Share-the-Savings EAMs pursuant to the 
July 2023 Order.  
174  July 2023 Order at 85. 
175  Id. 


--- Page 277 ---
 
269 
 
on energy efficiency cost savings – the targets are simply based on the amount of spending in 
DACs.  The Company’s proposed targets are not impacted by the overall energy efficiency 
budgets or targets, and therefore are not affected by the lack of certainty mentioned by the 
Commission.176  While the proposed DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs involve the 
Company’s energy efficiency spending, the structure and intent of the EAMs is aligned with the 
CLCPA and its targets related to investment in disadvantaged communities.  Tr. 1418.  Given 
that the DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs are distinguishable from the EAMs foreclosed 
by the Commission in the July 2023 Order, they should be adopted. 
UIU also contends that the Company’s proposed DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs 
provide an incentive to the Company for “reaching goals that are established in law.”  Tr. 507.  
This argument is misguided.  The CLCPA does not mandate that Central Hudson direct 35% of 
energy efficiency spending towards disadvantaged communities.  Rather, the CLCPA includes a 
statewide target that does not directly apply to individual utilities or specific programs.  The 
Company’s proposed DAC Energy Efficiency Benefits EAMs would encourage the Company to 
take action that is beyond what is currently required by law.  Therefore, UIU’s argument should 
be disregarded. 
B. 
Electric and Gas Peak Reduction  
The Company proposed to maintain the structure of the existing Electric Peak Reduction 
EAM, with a few clarifying changes as provided in Exhibit 115 (EAMP-3).  Tr. 1423.  The 
Company did not propose any changes to the current structure, metrics or measurement for the 
existing Gas Peak Reduction EAM.  Tr. 1426.  The Company’s Electric Peak Reduction EAM 
provides incentives to the Company to reduce its New York State Independent System Operator 
 
176  Id. 


--- Page 278 ---
 
270 
 
(“NYISO”) Zone G-J Locality Peak.177  The Gas Peak Reduction EAM incentivizes the 
Company to achieve gas system peak reductions that provide additional system benefits and 
lower supply costs to customers.178  Staff recommended that both of these EAMs be 
discontinued.  Tr. 1728. 
According to Staff, these EAMs should be discontinued because they can result in 
financial gains that are not due to any of the Company’s actions, but instead due to unexpected 
downturns in the economy or other negative macroeconomic impacts.  Tr. 1725.  Staff has made 
similar arguments with respect to other utilities with peak reduction EAMs, such as Con Edison, 
KEDNY/KEDLI, NYSEG, and Orange & Rockland.  Id.  The Company disagrees with Staff’s 
recommendation for a number of reasons. 
First, the fact that the Company’s Electric and Gas Peak Reduction EAM performance 
may be influenced by external factors does not provide a basis for their discontinuance.  The 
Commission rejected similar arguments to exclude outcome-based metrics from EAM incentives 
in its Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility Revenue Model Policy Framework issued May 
19, 2016 in Case 14-M-0101.179  The REV Order provides that “[a]s a general matter, we reject 
the arguments of parties that would confine EAMs to items under direct control or strong 
influence of the utility, to the extent that ‘influence’ is interpreted as the implementation of 
specific approved programs.”180  The Commission specifically acknowledged that peak reduction 
 
177  2021 Rate Plan, Joint Proposal, Appendix W.

is appropriate for an outcome-based metric, as it can be influenced by a wide range of utility 
activities, including but not limited to participation in demand response programs.181 
The Company also disagrees that it cannot influence peak reduction.  As it relates to the 
Electric Peak Reduction EAM, the Company implements demand response programs like the 
Commercial System Relief Program and the Residential (EV) Managed Charging Program, 
which give incentives to customers to reduce their usage during peak times.  Tr. 1437.  The Staff 
EAM Panel acknowledged that there is a potential relationship between increased participation in 
these programs and the Company’s peak demand.  Tr. 1757-1758.  The Company can also 
influence gas peak reduction through communications to customers encouraging energy 
conservation.  Tr. 1437-1438.  Given that encouraged growth in these electric demand response 
programs and customer communications can influence the Company’s peak demand, the current 
Peak Reduction EAMs should be maintained, as proposed by the Company.     
The Company finds Staff’s argument with respect to the demand response performance 
of other utilities unavailing.  Staff points to the performance of other utilities, including Con

Reduction EAMs.  Tr. 1725.  Staff testified that it has recently observed dramatic peak reduction

peak reduction.182  Further, the Con Ed Order and NYSEG-RG&E Rate Order were adopted after

E. 
EV Adoption 
Central Hudson proposed to continue its EV Adoption EAM with updated targets and 
assumptions about per vehicle emissions reductions.  Tr. 1424.  To develop its proposed 
baseline, midpoint, and maximum targets, the Company leveraged the detailed electric vehicle 
forecast that was developed for the Company’s 2023 DSIP.  Id.  This forecast was developed 
through a multi-step methodology that involved an analysis of historic vehicle adoption data in 
the Company’s service territory.186  The Company then used this historical data to inform a 
forecast that predicted the future electric vehicle share in the Company’s service territory.187  The 
Company also developed a propensity model for each electric vehicle type, which was calibrated 
to the service territory forecast.188  This forecast, which is specific to the Company’s service 
territory and accounts for historical adoption curves, provides a reasonable basis for setting the 
EV Adoption EAM targets.  Tr. 1443. 
Staff recommended that the targets for the EV Adoption EAM should be modified.  Tr. 
1742.  In developing new targets, Staff used the analysis in Appendix G to the Climate Action

Council Final Scoping Plan to set the midpoint and maximum target.  Tr. 1761.  Specifically,

XIII. ENERGY AFFORDABILITY PROGRAM 
A. 
EAP Budget, Expenditures and Reconciliation 
The Company proposed Rate Year EAP budgets of $20.2 million for electric and $5.3 
million for gas.  Tr. 3005.  Due to increased Rate Year participation in the EAP, the Company 
proposed to reduce the gas bill discounts in order to remain under the 2% budget cap established 
in Case 14-M-0565.  Tr. 1853-1854.  To develop its Rate Year EAP budgets, the Company 
provided a projection and updated bill discounts to: 1) project a Rate Year rate allowance that 
estimates actual program costs to be incurred for the Rate Year when low income bill discounts 
are reset consistent with the base delivery rate change in these proceedings; and 2) provide an 
estimate of the low income bill discounts that will be in effect during the Rate Year.  Tr. 1914.  
The Company also proposed to continue the current reconciliation mechanism.193  Tr. 3005. 
Staff objected to the method the Company used to arrive at its Rate Year EAP budgets.  
Staff argued that the Company did not adhere to the Commission directives in Case 14-M-0565 
with respect to calculating the typical bill and program participation.  Tr. 4448.  Specifically, 
 
193  Staff agreed with the continuation of this mechanism.  Exhibit 304.

279 
 
Staff objected to the use of the proposed customer charge instead of the current customer charge 
and the use of projected participated levels rather than historical participation levels.  Id.  Staff 
noted that the Company’s approach was inconsistent with the EAP workbook filed by the 
Company on November 1, 2023 in Case 14-M-0565.  Id.  According to Staff, the appropriate 
Rate Year budgets are $12.7 million for electric and $3.5 million for gas.  Tr. 4449.

proceedings.  Tr. 1915.  That calculation will utilize a typical bill calculation that leverages the 
Rate Year proposed customer charge and volumetric rates as well as a historical three-year

XIV. REVENUE ALLOCATION AND RATE DESIGN 
A. 
ECOS Studies 
The Company conducted embedded cost of service (“ECOS”) studies to guide the 
Company in determining the earned rate of return of its electric and gas service classes and to 
evaluate the relationship between the costs to serve a customer class and the rates charged to that 
class.  The ECOS studies included a historic ECOS study for both the electric and gas business, 
which used actual data from CY 2021, and pro-forma ECOS studies for both the electric and gas 
business using forecast data from the Rate Year.  Tr. 1592, 1596.  The Company performed its 
four separate ECOS studies consistent with the methodology used in its prior rate cases, Cases 
20-E-0428 and 20-G-0429, as well as the Unbundling Track Guidelines set forth by the 
Commission in Case 00-M-0054.  Tr. 1592.  The ECOS studies followed the basic steps of 
functionalization, classification, and allocation of costs to the Company’s service classes, using 
the “Electric Utility Cost Allocation Manual” dated January 1992 by the National Association of 
Regulatory Utility Commissioners, a generally accepted authority on cost of service 
methodology.  Tr. 1597-1600.  Staff found that the results of the ECOS studies were reasonable, 
and generally consistent with Commission precedent and past Company practice.  Tr.  4252.  


--- Page 289 ---
 
281 
 
Other parties, however, recommended various changes to the Company’s methodology, which 
are addressed below.   
1. 
Test Year and Forecasting Assumptions 
Consistent with the Cost of Service Panel’s rebuttal testimony (Tr. 1646), the Company 
proposes that revised ECOS studies be submitted at the time of Brief on Exceptions to reflect the 
final recommended revenue requirements and any modifications to the historic or pro forma 
ECOS studies, as applicable, both as addressed in the Recommended Decision.  Further, the 
Company’s Brief on Exceptions will include the revenue allocation and rate design resulting 
from any revised ECOS studies.  Tr. 1646.  
2. 
Property Taxes 
MI recommends that property tax expenses be functionalized using net plant, rather than 
by land and land rights investments as the Company did in the electric ECOS studies.  Tr. 4205. 
MI argues that using land and land rights investments to functionalize property taxes assigns a 
disproportionate amount of taxes to the production and transmission functions as compared to 
other functions in the ECOS studies.  Tr. 4204.  Although the Company has functionalized 
property taxes using land and land rights investments for the electric ECOS studies in each of its 
last three rate proceedings, the Company’s Cost of Service Panel agrees that would be more 
appropriate to functionalize property taxes on net plant, as it does in its gas ECOS studies.  Tr. 
1635.  Nevertheless, the Company does not believe it is necessary to reflect this change in these 
rate proceedings, as the overall electric ECOS studies, and the resulting rate design, is 
reasonable.  Additionally, maintaining the Company’s ECOS methodology supports the concept 
of gradualism in designing rates, as it would mitigate large rate shifts for customers.  Tr. 1636; 
Ex 178 (COSP-7R).  


--- Page 290 ---
 
282 
 
3. 
Electric System Control/Load Dispatching 
MI also recommends that the Company functionalize and allocate electric system control, 
load dispatching, and other power supply expenses (FERC Account Nos. 556 and 557) as 
production labor expense, rather than the Company’s proposal to functionalize and classify these 
costs to production energy.  Tr. 4200.  MI argues that its functionalization is appropriate because 
labor comprises the vast majority of these expenses and because the Company classifies 
production labor expenses to both demand and energy.  Tr. 4202. 
The Company disagrees with MI, as system control and load dispatching expense is not 
entirely attributed to labor, as MI assumes.  Tr. 1637.  Further, the Company believes it is more 
appropriate to be consistent in functionalizing plant assets and the corresponding expense.  Id.  
The Cost of Service Panel, in its rebuttal testimony, acknowledged that there could be a stronger 
linkage between functionalization of production plant and production system control and load 
dispatch expense.  Tr. 1637-1638.  Should the Commission decide to direct a change in the 
allocation of production system control and load dispatching expense, the Cost of Service Panel 
suggests that modification should be made solely to the 70% functionalized to production and 
should be based on the classification of production plant, which is classified 92.5% to energy and 
7.5% to demand.  Tr. 1638.  This classification would follow the Panel’s functionalization and 
classification of transmission load dispatching expense, which is functionalized and classified on 
total transmission plant.  Id.  This alternate classification would increase rate base and decrease 
operating expense by less than $100,000 for S.C. 1 non-heating with a corresponding decrease to 
rate base and increase to operating expense for all other classes with the exception of S.C. 5 – 
Area Lighting.  The resulting impact on class rates of return would be negligible with the largest 
impact being to the S.C. 13 T and S.C. 9 traffic signal classes with a reduction in rate of return of 
8 BPs.  Tr. 1639.  


--- Page 291 ---
 
283 
 
4. 
Legacy Generation Fleet 
MI’s recommendation to remove legacy generation costs and revenues from the electric 
ECOS studies should be rejected.  MI opposes the inclusion of costs and revenues related to the 
Company’s hydroelectric generation facilities in the Company’s electric ECOS study.  Tr. 4208. 
MI argues that these costs and revenue are not appropriate to include in the electric ECOS 
studies because “[l]egacy generation is not a delivery service.”  Tr. 4208, lines 8-9.  As stated by 
the Company’s Cost of Service Panel, however, these costs are appropriate to include in the 
electric ECOS studies for multiple reasons.  First, the return on and of the Company’s investment 
in its legacy generation plant, as well as production related O&M costs are recovered through 
delivery rates.  Tr. 1640.  As these facilities are provided rate base treatment, it is reasonable and 
appropriate to include them in the ECOS studies.  Tr. 1640-1641.  Further, all customers benefit 
from the operation of these facilities, as any benefits or revenues from the facilities are shared 
with all delivery customers.  As such, the costs and benefits of these facilities should be reflected 
in the ECOS studies used to develop rate design.  Tr. 1640.  
Your Honors and the Commission should also reject MI’s alternative proposal to increase 
the hydroelectric revenue imputation from $3.9 million to $18.6 million.  Tr. 4208, 1641.  MI’s 
alternative proposal to increase the revenue imputation fails to recognize that the sales revenues 
already captured in the ECOS study represent pre-rate relief revenues intended to collect the 
entire revenue requirement, including hydroelectric facilities, for which rate base treatment and 
O&M recovery are provided.  Tr. 1642.  Further, MI’s alternative is based on a flawed 
assumption that all revenues in revenue Accounts 456.15, 447.10, 447.25, 447.30 and 447.40 are 
reflective of actual market revenues for all of the Company’s legacy generation.  Id.  In fact, 
several of these accounts are unrelated to the Company’s hydroelectric facilities.  For example, 
Account 447.10 includes sales to end use customers in adjacent service territories.  Tr. 1643.  


--- Page 292 ---
 
284 
 
Additionally, Account 447.25 includes NYISO energy market revenues that are unrelated to the 
Company’s hydroelectric facilities, as those facilities are not bid into the NYISO market.  Tr. 
1643.  Finally, Account 456.15 is largely composed of transmission contract revenues that are 
unrelated to the Company’s hydroelectric facilities.  Tr. 1643.  For the foregoing reasons, MI’s 
recommendations regarding the hydroelectric generation imputation should be rejected, and 
Your Honors and the Commission should adopt the imputation of $3.9 million in hydroelectric 
revenues proposed by the Company. 
5. 
Gas Peak-Day Sendout 
MI noted that the Company’s pro-forma ECOS continued to use 70 Heating Degree Days 
(“HDD”) in determining its gas peak day send out, rather than 73 HDD as agreed in the 
Company’s previous rate case.  In rebuttal testimony, the Company stated that it had updated its 
historic ECOS study to use 73 HDD, but acknowledged that it inadvertently failed to do so in the 
pro-forma ECOS study. Tr. 1644.  The Company agreed with the impact on peak day allocators 
presented by MI.  Tr. 1645; Exhibit 450 (JP-7). 
6. 
Net-Operating Loss Carryforward 
In conducting its ECOS studies, the Company functionalized Net-Operating Loss 
(“NOL”) carryforward from deferred income taxes on pre-tax operating income and taxable 
income.  MI opposes the Company’s functionalization approach for NOL, arguing that they 
should instead by functionalized based on previously allocated rate base.  Tr. 4203.  The 
Company maintains that it is appropriate to functionalize NOL on a pre-tax operating income 
basis.  Tr. 1633-1634, 1653.  MI’s proposal should be rejected because NOL carryforwards 
reflect the outcomes of previous tax calculations.  These calculations incorporate a diverse set of 
items and, as a result, the NOL “lacks the link to specific items necessary for a more granular 
functionalization.”  Tr. 1633, lines 20-21.  Further, MI’s proposal is inappropriate because it 


--- Page 293 ---
 
285 
 
focuses on only functionalization and ignores the impact on the allocation of these costs.  Tr. 
1633.  The Company analyzed the impact of MI’s approach on the allocation of NOL and found 
that the resulting impact on class rates of return is a decrease in return for S.C. No. 1 non-
heating, S.C. No. 2 non-demand and secondary demand, S.C. No. 13 substation and 
transmission, and S.C. No. 9.  Tr. 1634.  Staff also opposes MI’s proposed recommendation.  
Tr. 4288.  The Company supports its initial ECOS methodology, including the functionalization 
of NOL carryforward, and submits that MI’s proposed modification should be rejected.  Tr. 
1634.  
B. 
MCOS Studies 
The Company prepared electric and gas Marginal Cost of Service (“MCOS”) studies to 
be utilized in setting rates for the Excelsior Jobs Program (“EJP”) in the Company’s service 
territory.  Tr. 1613.  The Company followed the same general methodology in preparing its 
MCOS studies as it has in prior rate proceedings and proposed that the MCOS studies be used to 
develop EJP rates in this proceeding.  No party opposed the Company’s methodology in 
performing the MCOS studies; however, Staff opposed the use of the Company’s MCOS study 
to establish EJP rates.  Tr. 3632, 4275.  Instead, Staff recommends that the Company continue to 
use the interim system-wide $14.55 per kW per year estimate for marginal cost purposes 
pursuant to the Order Regarding Value Stack Compensation194 to develop its EJP rates until the 
Commission acts on the ongoing proceeding in Case 19-E-0283 and establishes a statewide 
MCOS methodology.  Tr. 3632, 4275.  Although the Company acknowledges that subsequent 
action in Case 19-E-0283 may potentially result in revised EJP rates, using the updated MCOS

study conducted by the Company in these cases is appropriate so that EJP rates reflect the most

C. 
Electric Loss Factor 
The Company’s Cost of Service Panel provided the Company’s Factor of Adjustment 
(“FOA”) for its Electric Loss Factor pursuant to the methodology established by the Commission 
in Case 09-E-0588 and continued in the Company’s subsequent electric rate cases, Case 14-E-

Loss Factor FOA is determined based on the electric loss factor calculated using the most recent 
36-month average based on the data available at the time of compliance.  Id.  Based upon an 
assumed June 2024 tariff filing in compliance with the Commission’s determination in these 
proceedings, the most recently available data, the 36 months ending May 2024, would be utilized 
to determine the final Electric Loss Factor FOA for each service class/sub-class.  The Company 
submits that data for the 36 months ending May 2024 should be adopted by Your Honors and the 
Commission as that time period is consistent with past Company practice and Commission 
precedent and has not been opposed by any party.  
D. 
Lost and Unaccounted For Gas 
The Company’s Cost of Service Panel provided the Company’s calculation of the FOA 
for Lost and Unaccounted For (“LAUF”) using the methodology adopted in Case 14-G-0319, as 
modified in Case 17-G-0460 and continued in Case 20-G-0429.  Tr. 1621.  Under this 
methodology, the FOA is calculated as a five-year average for the 12 months ending August 31st 
of each year, updated annually to be applicable for the period beginning with the first billing 
batch of November and ending with the final billing batch of the subsequent October.  Id.  The 
effective FOA at the time of filing was 1.0060, updated on November 1, 2022 based on the five-

year average ended August 31, 2022.195  Tr. 1622.  The Company submits that Your Honors and
