methodology currently employed by the Company as it is reasonable, is consistent with past 
Company practice and Commission precedent, and is not opposed by any party in this 
proceeding. 
E. 
Electric Revenue Allocation and Rate Design 
1. 
Revenue Allocation 
The Company allocated revenue among its electric and gas service classes using the same 
general approach taken in its prior rate cases, including the 2020 Rate Case.  In conducting the 
revenue allocation, the Company sought to bring the rates of return for its electric and gas 
service classes to within 15% of the system average rate of return.  Tr. 1830.  As it had done in 
the 2020 Rate Case, the Company implemented tolerance bands to mitigate bill impacts for 
customers.  Pursuant to the tolerance bands, service classes earning less than 85% of the system 
average rate of return were allocated a maximum increase of 1.25 times the system average rate 
of return and service classes earning more than 115% of the system average were allocated a 
minimum increase of 0.75 times the system average.  Id.  The Company referenced its Historic 
2021 and Pro-Forma ECOS studies (the “ECOS Studies”) in performing the revenue allocation.  
Tr. 1832.   
Consistent with the approach taken in prior proceedings, the Company allocated a system 
average increase to classes where the ECOS Studies indicated varying results in the class’s 
unitized rate of return.  Where the ECOS Studies did not indicate varying results for a service 
class, those classes with a unitized rate of return less than 85% of the system average received 
 
195  The effective FOA was subsequently updated to 1.0037 on November 1, 2023, based on the five-year average 
ended August 31, 2023. 


--- Page 296 ---
 
288 
 
1.25 times the overall system average and those classes with a unitized rate of return more than 
115% of the system average received 0.75 times the overall system average.  Tr. 1832.  The 
unitized rate of return varied for electric S.C. Nos. 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 8, 9 and 13 (Transmission) 
among the ECOS Studies; therefore, the Company allocated incremental revenues to those 
classes using the system average rate of return.  Tr. 1833.  The rate of return for S.C. No. 13 
(Substation) varied, but was consistently positive, exceeding the upper tolerance band level; 
therefore, S.C. No. 13 (Substation) was allocated incremental revenues at 0.75 times the overall 
system average.  Id.  This revenue allocation resulted in a revenue shortfall as compared to the 
rate increase revenue, which was allocated pro-rata among the service classes.  Tr. 1833-1834.    
Central Hudson updated its revenue allocation in its rebuttal testimony to reflect the 
Company’s October 2, 2023 Update and additional updates to the ECOS Studies and revenue 
requirements presented in the Company’s rebuttal testimony using the same methodology.  
Tr. 1876-1877.  The updated proposed electric revenue allocation is shown in Exhibit 204 (FRP-
1R).  The Company submits that its electric revenue allocation is reasonable and consistent with 
historic practice and, as such, should be adopted. 
The only party opposing the Company’s revenue allocation methodology is MI, which 
recommends two modifications: 1) using only the Pro-Forma ECOS study in developing the 
revenue allocation; and 2) not assigning any increase to classes currently earning rates of return 
that are more than 300 basis points above the system average.  The Company submits that both 
of MI’s proposed modifications be rejected and the Company’s revenue allocation be adopted.  
Tr. 1891.    
Central Hudson maintains it is appropriate to use both ECOS Studies in developing the 
revenue allocation because it allows the revenue allocation to reflect actual data and experience 


--- Page 297 ---
 
289 
 
while providing a useful point of comparison for the Pro-Forma ECOS study.  Relying solely on 
the Pro-Forma ECOS study, as MI suggests, introduces a greater potential for error because it 
reflects forecasts of revenue, expense, and rate base, as well as forecasted customer and demand 
allocators, rather than actual data.  Tr. 1892.  If the forecast of any of these items were subject to 
change, then the entire results of the study would change.  Id.  Although the Historic ECOS study 
can be dated, it provides a basis for what actually happened in a prior period and can be used as a 
tool to compare for reasonableness with the Pro-Forma ECOS study forecasts.  Tr. 1893.  The 
use of the Historic ECOS study is also consistent with the Company’s past practice in rate cases 
since 2009, when the Company adopted Staff’s recommendation to use both a historic and pro-
forma ECOS studies to establish the revenue allocation in Cases 09-E-0588 and 09-G-0589.  Id.  
There is no reason to depart from this methodology in these rate cases.  
The Company also opposes MI’s recommendation to not assign a revenue increase to 
classes providing returns more than 300 BPs above the system average rate of return.  MI’s 
proposal could cause material impacts in the revenue increase for other classes.  MI indicated 
that it had not analyzed these impacts to other classes in making this proposal.  Tr. 1893; Exhibit 
210 (FRP-8R).  As discussed in the Forecasting and Rates Panel’s Rebuttal Testimony, only one 
gas service class – S.C. 11 T – is providing returns 300 BPs above the system average rate of 
return under the Company’s revenue allocation methodology.  Tr. 1894.  As discussed above, the 
Company’s methodology takes steps to mitigate bill impacts to this over-returning class while 
managing bill impacts on all other service classes by allocating an increase of 0.75 times the 
overall system average increase.  Id.  MI’s proposal to adjust the entire revenue allocation 
methodology solely for the benefit of one service class is unreasonable and should be rejected. 


--- Page 298 ---
 
290 
 
2. 
Rate Design 
The Company proposed adjustments to minimum charges, volumetric rates, and demand 
rates for certain classes.  Tr. 1845-1848.  The Company proposed to implement a Low Power 
Attachment Program, which will allow customers to attach small power devices, such as security 
cameras, to existing poles in a cost-effective manner.  Tr. 1841-1845.  The Company also 
proposed updates for its billing services credit, the Energy Efficiency Tracker (“EET”) credit, 
and low income bill discounts.  Tr. 1852-1853.  The Company submits that its proposed rate 
design is reasonable and should be adopted. 
The Company’s proposed electric rate design appropriately moves rates towards the cost 
of service set forth in the Company’s ECOS Studies, while reflecting the principle of gradualism 
to mitigate bill impacts on customers.  Staff, PULP, and MI oppose the Company’s proposed 
minimum customer charges with respect to different service classes.  Walmart recommends that 
the Commission adopt the customer charges proposed by the Company for S.C. No. 2 Secondary 
and Primary Demand customers but assign all remaining increases from those customers to 
demand charges such that the delivery energy charges remain fixed.  Tr. 572, 1896, 1900.  The 
changes recommended by these parties fail to strike the appropriate balance between making 
progress towards the cost of service while mitigating bill impacts.  Therefore, the recommended 
changes by these parties should be rejected. 
Staff and PULP oppose the Company’s proposed $2.00 increase to customer charges for 
S.C. Nos. 1 and 6, and Staff opposes the proposed $2.00 increase for S.C. No. 2 Non-Demand.  
PULP recommends freezing residential electric customer charges at their current level.  Tr. 619.  
The Staff Rates Panel, meanwhile, recommends reducing the Company’s proposed customer 
charge increases from $2.00 to $0.50 for S.C. Nos. 1 and 6 and S.C. No. 2 Non-Demand.  
Tr. 4262.  Staff and PULP’s proposals should be rejected because they fail to make sufficient 


--- Page 299 ---
 
291 
 
progress toward cost-based rates.  Such failure to make progress is exacerbated by the fact that 
Staff’s proposal provides only minimal savings to customers.  Staff’s proposed customer charge 
increase for a customer using 530 kWh per month would only reduce a customer’s total bill by 
$0.36, or 0.24%.  Tr. 1898.  These minimal savings do not justify the lack of progress towards 
cost-based rates.  The Company submits that Your Honors and the Commission should adopt the 
Company’s proposed customer charges for these mass market customer classes, as they provide 
an appropriate balance of following cost causation principles, while mitigating bill impacts on 
customers.   
MI’s proposal to increase the S.C. No. 3 and S.C. No. 13 Substation and Transmission 
customer charges by 11% and 32%, respectively, above the Company’s proposed charges also 
fails to strike an appropriate balance and, therefore, should be rejected.  Tr. 1899.  Although 
MI’s proposed increases for these classes would move the classes closer to the cost of service, 
the overall bill impacts resulting from this change would severely harm smaller customers in 
these classes.  For example, under MI’s proposal, a small S.C. No. 13 T customer with 925 kW 
of demand would experience a delivery bill 20% higher than what it would be charged under the 
Company’s proposal.  Tr. 1899-1900.  As a result, MI’s proposal should be rejected and the 
Company’s more gradual movement to cost based rates be adopted in this instance.    
Walmart’s proposal to assign all remaining revenue increases after customer charges to 
demand charges for S.C. No. 2 Secondary and Primary Demand customers similarly fails to 
properly account for bill impacts for all customers in the class.  Although the Company agrees 
with the general principle of aligning costs and rates, other factors, such as bill impacts, must be 
considered in designing rates.  Tr. 1901.  Walmart’s proposal would harm customers with lower 
load factors, burdening them with a disproportionate amount of the rate increase.  Id.  In 


--- Page 300 ---
 
292 
 
addition, energy delivery charges already comprise a relatively small portion of the total delivery 
revenue requirement for these classes currently representing 8.5% and 4.7% of S.C. No. 2 
secondary and primary demand revenue requirements, respectively.  Id.  Therefore, to be mindful 
of customer bill impacts for customers of all load factors, Your Honors and the Commission 
should adopt the rate design proposed in the Company’s initial filing, which maintains the 
current ratio of demand to energy delivery charges for S.C. No. 2 Secondary and Primary 
Demand customers. 
F. 
Gas Revenue Allocation and Rate Design 
1. 
Revenue Allocation 
The Company follows the same general methodology for revenue allocation for both its 
electric and gas businesses.  For S.C. Nos. 1 and 12, S.C. Nos. 2, 6 and 13, and S.C. No. 11 and 
Distribution Large Mains, the unitized rate of return varied among the ECOS Studies; therefore, 
these classes were assigned a system average increase.  Tr. 1834.  S.C. No. 11 Distribution 
consistently fell below the lower tolerance level and was assigned the maximum increase of 1.25 
times the system average, and S.C. No. 11 Transmission consistently was above the upper 
tolerance level and was assigned 0.75 times the system average increase.  Id.  This revenue 
allocation resulted in a revenue surplus, which was allocated among the service classes pro-rata.  
Id.  
The Company updated its revenue allocation in its rebuttal testimony to reflect updates to 
the ECOS Studies and revenue requirements presented in the Company’s rebuttal testimony 
using the same methodology, as well as to make discrete adjustments based on identified issues.  
Tr. 1877-1878.  The updated proposed gas revenue allocation is shown in Exhibit 205 (FRP-2R).  
The Company submits that its gas revenue allocation is reasonable and consistent with historic 
practice and, as such, should be adopted. 


--- Page 301 ---
 
293 
 
The only party that opposes the Company’s gas revenue allocation methodology is MI, 
raising the same arguments as it did in opposing the Company’s electric revenue allocation.  For 
the reasons discussed in Section XIV.E.1, supra, MI’s arguments should be rejected and the 
Commission should adopt the Company’s proposed gas revenue allocation. 
2. 
Rate Design 
The Company proposed certain structural changes to its gas rate design, including the 
continued phase-out of declining block rates and the elimination of the high volume discount for 
S.C. No. 6 customers.  The Company also proposed adjustments to minimum charges, 
volumetric, and demand rates for certain classes.  Tr. 1850-1851.  The Company further 
proposed updates for its billing services credit, the EET credit, and low income bill discounts, as 
it did for electric customers.  Tr. 1852-1853.  The Company submits that its proposed rate design 
is reasonable and should be adopted. 
The Company’s proposed minimum customer charges appropriately move rates towards 
the cost of service set forth in the Company’s ECOS Studies, while mitigating bill impacts on 
customers.  Staff and PULP oppose the Company’s proposed minimum customer charges with 
respect to different service classes.  The changes recommended by these parties, however, fail to 
strike the appropriate balance between making progress towards moving rates closer to the cost 
of service while mitigating bill impacts.  
Similar to electric customer charges, Staff and PULP oppose the Company’s proposed 
$2.00 increase to customer charges for residential gas service classes, S.C. Nos. 1 and 12.  As 
with electric customer charges, PULP recommends freezing gas customer charges for these 
classes at their current level.  Tr. 619-620.  The Staff Rates Panel, meanwhile, recommends 
reducing the Company’s proposed increase from $2.00 to $1.00 for these classes.  Tr. 4266-
4267.  For the reasons discussed with respect to electric customer charges in Section XIV.A.2, 


--- Page 302 ---
 
294 
 
supra, Staff and PULP’s proposals fail to make as much meaningful progress as the Company’s 
proposal toward the cost of service for residential customers.  Further, when looking at total bill 
impacts, Staff’s proposed $1.00 increase fails to create savings for most residential customers.  
Tr. 1899.  An average gas customer with annual usage of 780 Ccf would experience savings 
under the Company’s proposal of increasing the customer charge by $2.00 when compared to 
Staff’s proposed $1.00 increase.  Id. 
Staff also recommends that the Company make a more gradual decrease to customer 
charges for S.C. No. 11 Transmission and S.C. No. 11 Large Distribution Mains.  Tr. 4267.  The 
Company disagrees with this recommendation, as the decrease proposed by the Company is in 
line with the findings of the Company’s ECOS studies and the customer charges for these service 
classes should follow cost causation principles to appropriately set rates.  Tr. 1900.  
The Company proposed to reduce volume discounts for gas customers in two ways:  1) 
by continuing the five-year phase out of its declining block rate structure for S.C. Nos. 1, 2, 6, 12 
and 13 that commenced in the previous rate plan; and 2) by phasing out the high volume 
discount for S.C. No. 6 customers over a two-year period and to eliminate the gas air 
conditioning discount due to lack of participation.  Tr. 1836-1838.  UIU agrees with the 
Company regarding removal of the gas air conditioning discount.  Tr. 516.  Staff supported the 
continued phase-out of the declining block rate structure but recommended that the phase out of 
the S.C. No. 6 high volume discount be extended to five years to mitigate bill impacts.  Tr. 4264, 
4266, 4268.  UIU supports the continued phase out of declining block rates but recommends that 
the Company implement a more gradual reduction in the Rate Year.  Tr. 516-517.  Your Honors 
and the Commission should reject these recommendations from UIU and Staff and adopt the 
Company’s rate design. 


--- Page 303 ---
 
295 
 
The elimination of volume and end-use-specific discounts such as declining block rates, 
the S.C. No. 6 high volume discount and the gas air conditioning discount furthers the goals of 
the CLCPA and New York State energy policy.  In the 2021 Rate Plan, the Commission found 
that the five-year phase-out of declining block rates supported energy efficiency and eliminates 
the inconsistency between the Company’s rate structure and its energy efficiency efforts.196  
Declining block structures also limit the ability of lower usage customers to achieve bill savings 
through energy efficiency.  Tr. 1839.  The elimination of volume discounts will also simplify 
customer bills, allowing customers greater insight into the incremental costs of increases in 
usage.  Tr. 1839-1840.   
Although UIU testified that it “commend[s] the Company for pursuing the elimination of 
declining block rates” (Tr. 523, line 13), it recommends that the slower movement toward 
flattening block rates for residential customers in S.C. Nos. 1 and 12 to mitigate bill impacts in 
this proceeding.  Tr.  523-524.  UIU’s concern over bill impacts for residential customers is 
overstated and does not justify slowing the progress toward flattening block rates.  For example, 
a large residential customer that uses 200 Ccf in a month would incur only approximately 2% 
greater charges under the Company’s proposal than under UIU’s more gradual phase-out.  
Residential customers at lower usage levels would experience even lesser impacts.  Most 
residential customers are unlikely to reach this level of usage, and consequently this level of bill 
impacts; only 2% of customers reach or exceed 200 Ccf during summer months.  Tr. 1903.  
These low bill impacts on a small number of high-use customers do not justify deviating from 
the phase-out of declining block rates established in the 2021 Rate Plan and the accompanying 
incentives for energy efficiency gains. 
 
196  2021 Rate Plan at 31, 41. 


--- Page 304 ---
 
296 
 
The Commission should also reject UIU’s recommendation to continue the tail gas block 
rate notification procedure adopted in the prior rate case.  Tr. 528.  Pursuant to the Joint Proposal 
adopted by the 2021 Rate Plan, the Company was required to mail a notification to each EAP gas 
customer who would experience a delivery bill impact of 9% or more as a result of the flattening 
of declining block rates.197  UIU acknowledged that the Company had no EAP customers that 
experienced bill impacts at or above the 9% threshold, and therefore was not required to send 
any notices to customers.  Tr. 528.  UIU nevertheless recommends that the Company continue to 
“monitor and inform EAP customers if changes in the tail block structure result in delivery bill 
percent changes that are higher than average customers.”  Tr. 528, lines 6-8.  This monitoring 
and notification requirement is unnecessary, creates an undue administrative burden for the 
Company and provides no benefit for customers.  As acknowledged by UIU, no customer met 
the notice threshold under the Company’s current rate plan.  Further, the impact of the 
Company’s rate design proposal, prior to any rate change, is 3.7% for the highest use customers, 
with customers at or below the average use level experiencing savings.  Tr. 1904.  A customer’s 
bill can fluctuate in a given month by 3.7% or more solely due to changing usage, supply or 
other factors.  Id.  For this reason, the Commission should reject UIU’s proposal and should not 
implement any notification procedure outside the Company’s gas rate design webpage.198 
The Commission should also reject Staff’s proposal to extend the phase-out of the S.C. 
No. 6 high volume discount from two to five years.  Staff testified that a more gradual phase out 
of the high volume discount would help to mitigate bill impacts for customers.  Tr. 4268.  Staff’s 
concern is overstated, as Staff’s extension of the phase out period would create only a modest

reduction in bill impacts for high volume customers than they would experience under the 
Company’s two-year phase-out, from 18.9% to 14.4%.  Tr. 1905.  This modest (approximately

subsidized by other customers and contrary to the mandates of the CLCPA.  Id.  
G. 
Danskammer Revenues 
Due to the uncertainty surrounding the future repowering and operation of Danskammer, 
the Commission should adopt the Company’s proposal to exclude Danskammer revenues from 
the revenue forecast and defer the actual revenues received from the facility for refund to 
customers through a bill credit.  In order to move forward with its repowering plans, 
Danskammer is currently pursuing various permits and approvals necessary for its operation, 
including a Title V Clean Air Act Permit (“Title V Permit”) from the New York State 
Department of Environmental Conservation (“DEC”) and New York State Siting Board (“Siting 
Board”) approval pursuant to Article 10 of the Public Service Law.  The DEC denied 
Danskammer’s request for a Title V Permit in October 2021, finding that the repowering plans 
were not consistent with the CLCPA.  A court subsequently upheld the DEC’s decision in June 
2022.199  The proceeding before the Siting Board, meanwhile, has been adjourned indefinitely 
since January 16, 2022 to allow Danskammer to continue to pursue its Title V Permit.200  
Although Danskammer continues to pursue a Title V Permit and Article 10 approval for the 
facility, the initial decisions by the DEC and a reviewing court as well as the lack of progress at 
the Siting Board create substantial uncertainty with respect to the future operation of the facility 
 
199  Danskammer Energy, LLC v. New York State Dept. of Envtl. Conservation, 76 Misc. 3d 196 (N.Y. Sup. Ct., 
Orange Cty., 2022). 
200  Case 18-F-0325 – Application of Danskammer Energy, LLC for a Certificate of Environmental Compatibility 
and Public Need Pursuant to Article 10 for Approval to Repower its Danskammer Generating Station Site 
Located in the Town of Newburgh, Orange County, Ruling Adjourning Schedule (January 26, 2022). 


--- Page 306 ---
 
298 
 
and the associated revenues Central Hudson may receive in the Rate Year.  Given this 
uncertainty, the Company’s proposal to exclude Danskammer revenues from the Company’s 
forecast and defer them for future credit to customers is appropriate and should be adopted.  
Staff’s recommendation to maintain the imputation of $1.5 million in Danskammer 
revenues adopted in the 2021 Rate Plan is unreasonable and should be rejected by Your Honors 
and the Commission.  Tr. 4234.  As an initial matter, Staff’s proposal effectively ignores the 
developments in Danskammer’s Title V Permit and Article 10 approval processes, which have 
become significantly more uncertain since the 2021 Rate Plan, as discussed above.  It is 
unreasonable to continue the status quo from the previous rate case given the significant change 
in circumstance since that time.    
Further, the Commission has found that, where substantial uncertainty exists with regard 
to a large revenue source, it is inappropriate to impute revenues into the revenue forecast.  In the 
2010 Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation (“Niagara Mohawk”) rate case, the Commission 
found that the prevailing circumstances were “too uncertain to impute to Niagara Mohawk a 
specific amount of merchant plant delivery revenues.”201  The Commission, therefore, required 
Niagara Mohawk to defer merchant plant delivery revenues for future credit to customers,202 
which is exactly what the Company proposes to do here with respect to Danskammer revenues.   
Finally, even if the Commission finds that imputation of Danskammer revenues is 
appropriate in this proceeding, the $1.5 million imputation recommended by Staff is 
inappropriate because Danskammer recently reduced its firm supply contracted volume, which 
 
201  Case 10-E-0050 – Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules, and Regulations of 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation for Electric Service, Order Establishing Rates for Electric Service at 21 
(January 24, 2011). 
202  Id. 


--- Page 307 ---
 
299 
 
results in a reduction in revenue from the facility.  Tr. 1825, 1895.  Continuing to impute 
revenues at the current level would ignore this known reduction in revenue.  Therefore, to the 
extent the Commission finds that imputing Danskammer revenues is necessary, the imputation 
for the Rate Year should be: 1) expressly conditioned on continuation of the current 
Danskammer deferral; and 2) limited to, at most, $1.0 million which is more reflective of the 
current Danskammer revenue level.  Tr. 1895.   
H. 
Bill Impacts 
The Company is cognizant of affordability concerns for its customers and went to great 
lengths in this filing to mitigate bill impacts on its electric and gas customers resulting from these 
rate proceedings.  As discussed by the Company’s Policy Panel, these mitigation actions 
included: re-prioritizing and delaying a meaningful portion of the Company’s capital plan; 
utilizing an ROE that is approximately 50 BPs below the average of the ROE range 
recommended by the Company’s expert; and implementing a productivity credit, despite 
challenges from inflation and the post COVID-19 labor market.  Tr. 3297-3298.  The Company 
also sought to implement regulatory assets and liabilities, where possible, in a manner that 
further softened bill impacts for customers.  Tr. 3298.  The Company notes that it is limited in its 
ability to implement mitigation measures in a one-year litigated rate case, as opposed to a multi-
year settlement.  The Company submits, however, that the overall bill impacts in this proceeding, 
as presented in Exhibit 208 (FRP-3R) and Exhibit 209 (FRP-4R) for electric and gas 
respectively, are reasonable and properly balance affordability with the Company’s need to 
maintain the safe and reliable operation of its systems while making continued progress towards 
the state’s climate and energy goals. 


--- Page 308 ---
300 
I.
Battery Storage Rates
The Commission should not make any findings regarding bulk battery storage rates, 
including those rates addressed in KCE’s testimony, that are subject to the jurisdiction of FERC.  
Based upon KCE’s response to interrogatory response CH to KCE (001) (Exhibit 210 (FRP-7R) 
pages 9-10), the Company understands that the rates applicable to the specific projects KCE is 
concerned about are Wholesale Distribution Service (“WDS”) rates.  Central Hudson’s proposed 
WDS tariff, which will apply to generation or storage resources interconnected to Central 
Hudson’s distribution system that seek to participate in the NYISO-administered wholesale 
markets, has been provided to the NYISO, in its role as the tariff administrator, for filing on 
behalf of Central Hudson as a new Rate Schedule in Section 6 of the NYISO Open Access 
Transmission Tariff.  The NYISO anticipates submitting the Rate Schedule to FERC by March 8, 
2024.  Once filed, the proposed WDS rates will be subject to FERC’s review and approval and 
therefore are outside of the scope of the Company’s rate proposals in this proceeding.  Tr. 1908.   
J.

In addition to corresponding tariff updates to implement provisions discussed herein, the 
Company proposed tariff revisions to remove the Power Purchase Agreement component of the 
Energy Cost Adjustment Mechanism from the tariff due to the agreement’s expiration.  Tr. 1863. 
No party opposed the Company’s proposal.  In addition, the Company has proposed various 
tariff modifications to the Billing for Service Supplied and Retail Access Program sections of the 
tariff to implement the Company’s monthly meter reading proposal described in the testimony of 
the Customer Experience Panel.  Tr. 1870.  Although the Company is expediting movement to 
monthly meter reading, the Company respectfully requests that any tariff compliance filings 
relating to monthly meter reading be made within 30 days after the Company has fully 


--- Page 309 ---
301 
implemented monthly meter reading across its service territory with such tariff amendments 
effective on 15 days’ notice.

For all the reasons expressed above, the positions expressed by Central Hudson herein
should be adopted and those positions of Staff and the other parties that conflict with the

Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation 
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
 
Support for Incremental Full-Time Equivalents (“FTEs”) 
 
 
I. 
Electric Capital and Operations Panel Incremental FTEs 
Assistant Distribution Operator (4) 
These FTEs were proposed to support the Company’s Grid Modernization (“Grid Mod”) 
initiative, which includes the Advanced Distribution Management System (“ADMS”).  Tr. 2057-
2058.  These FTEs are necessary for the expansion of Central Dispatch operation and the 
buildout of Distribution System Operations, which will be essential to ensure 24/7 monitoring 
and control with the ADMS.  Distribution Operators will be using the ADMS to optimize the 
electric distribution system.  Staff proposes eliminating these FTEs because the purpose of the 
ADMS is to automate the electric system, and thus, there should be less human intervention, not 
more.  Tr. 2585-2586.  However, Staff’s proposal would hinder the Company’s plan to transition 
the distribution operating authority from the five district offices to a single Distribution Control 
Center.  Tr. 2117.  While the ADMS will provide efficiencies, centralizing the distribution 
operating authority will require incremental staffing to perform daily functions currently 
performed by other Company employees, which will allow those FTEs to focus more on safety 
and field productivity.  Id.  Further, removal of these FTEs would require limiting shifts to one or 
two employees, which is not sufficient for a department that will be charged with performing 
distribution switching and issuing work permits across the entire Central Hudson service 
territory.  Tr. 2118.  Finally, Staff’s proposal would not allow for appropriate training for 
Distribution Operators, which is critical for the transition discussed above.  Tr. 2119.  For these 
reasons, Staff’s removal of these FTEs should be rejected. 


--- Page 315 ---
 Initial Brief 
Appendix 2 
Page 2 of 26 
 
 
I&C Technician (Substation) (3)1 
These FTEs are necessary to support additional work related to technology changes and 
to create technical proficiency considering the long progression and training required for the role.  
Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  While Staff generally agreed with the conceptual need for incremental 
I&C Technicians, Staff disagreed with these three positions, stating that the I&C Technician 
position is a relatively new job title and the Company did not adequately demonstrate the 
immediate need for the incremental FTEs for this position, which would “increase its current 
headcount by approximately 200 percent.”  Tr. 2596-2597.  While I&C Technician may be a new 
job title, the core responsibilities of this position are like that of the historical Communication 
Technician and Plant Technician, which became Substation Technicians.  Tr. 2129.  Therefore, 
Staff’s assertion regarding a 200% increase in headcount is a mischaracterization when viewed 
in context and based on workload.  Id.  These positions are necessary as building the expertise 
in-house ultimately is more cost-effective than contracting out work on fiber optic splicing.  Tr. 
2130. 
Assistant Engineer (Substation) (2) 
 
These FTEs are necessary to perform planning/scoping, design and project construction 
management to meet the demands of the work plan.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  Specifically, 
these roles are needed to address the persistent trend of missing substation design package 
deliverables.  Tr. 2121.  Staff disallows these FTEs because the Company’s proposal represents 
an increase in headcount of 100% and the Company can adequately address its current and future 
electric substation design workload without the FTEs.  Tr. 2590-2591.  Staff’s assertion that the 
 
1  
Staff’s Exhibit 302 (SAP-4 Corrected) reflects a removal of 3 I&C Technicians.  Staff’s EIOP testimony, 
however, explains that the Company asked for 4 in total (3 within the bridge period and 1 in RY2) and notes 
that 3 were denied but clearly stated that the one hired in 2023 was to remain.  Tr. 2595-2597. 


--- Page 316 ---
 Initial Brief 
Appendix 2 
Page 3 of 26 
 
 
Company’s proposal for this position is an increase in headcount of 100% is a 
mischaracterization, as the existing substation design engineers cover a variety of projects in 
varying complexity.  Tr. 2125.  The basis for these incremental positions is to allow focused 
attention to the capital construction projects by the engineering team and provide available 
resources to support the numerous developer-funded projects.  Id.  Accordingly, these positions 
are necessary for the Company to meet the demands of the work plan, and Staff’s reduction 
should be rejected. 
Financial Analyst - Capital 
 
Central Hudson requires a Financial Analyst - Capital to execute improvements related to 
Management Audit Recommendations and enhanced capital planning.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  
 
Staff claims that based on Recommendation 3.2 of the report Overland filed on 
November 15, 2023 in Case 21-M-0541, this role is not necessary, as analysis of the Company’s 
capital projects is a process that is already in place.  Tr. 878.  Staff states that Overland’s 
Recommendation 3.2 is intended to remedy deficiencies in that process.  Id.  However, while 
Overland’s report states that there is approximately 20 hours of additional monthly work that 
could be absorbed by the Company without this position, it does not provide any basis for this 
calculation of task time or how it arrived at this conclusion.  Tr. 2131-2132.  Also, Staff provided 
additional recommendations throughout its EIOP testimony that add further time requirements 
for the completion of the new processes, as well as greatly expand the work needed to analyze 
capital spending and create reports.  Tr. 2132.  The size of the Capital Program has more than 
doubled in the last 10 years, and yet the headcount of only one employee has remained the same.  
Id.  Thus, the addition of this position is critical for the Capital Program, especially in light of 
Staff’s proposals.   


--- Page 317 ---
 Initial Brief 
Appendix 2 
Page 4 of 26 
 
 
Assistant Engineer - Electric System Design Engineer (Grid Mod) (3)2 
These positions are necessary to supply enough capital work to meet the combined 
capital demands of the Five-Year Capital Plan, the increased number of developer-funded capital 
projects, and other corporate initiatives such as Grid Mod, Network Strategy, and 
Interconnections.  Tr. 2049.  The engineers will perform planning/scoping, design, and project 
construction management to meet the demands of the work plan as promised and on time.  
Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  
Staff rejected these FTEs on the basis that the Company’s overall request for five 
additional engineers would increase the headcount of Assistant Engineers (Grid Mod) by 166%, 
and the Company’s proposed Electric Substation Design project workload does not support the 
need for five additional engineers because this position has overlapping duties with the Assistant 
Engineer (Substation).  Tr. 2587-2588.  First, the Company has requested the minimum number 
of engineers required to meet the capital spending plan based on proforma historical data.  Tr.  
2050.  Second, Staff’s statement that there are overlapping duties among Assistant Engineers for 
Grid Mod and Substation is precisely why these additional FTEs are necessary: the sharing of 
Grid Mod project duties detracts from the ability of the other engineers to timely complete their 
capital improvement projects.  Thus, the Company needs designated Grid Mod engineers to 
allow for focused attention on the remaining construction projects by the rest of the engineering 
team and timely completion of work.  Tr. 2124.  
 
2  
Staff's Exhibit 302 (SAP-4 Corrected) reflects a removal of 3 assistant engineers for Grid Mod.  Staff’s EIOP 
testimony, however notes, “of the five Assistant Engineer (Grid Mod) FTEs requested by the Company, the 
Panel recommends allowing one.  The Company has already hired that one FTE for this position.” Tr. 2587. 


--- Page 318 ---
 Initial Brief 
Appendix 2 
Page 5 of 26 
 
 
Relay Technician (Substations) (2) 
The Company requires two relay technicians to support additional work related to 
technological changes and to help create technical proficiency, especially considering the long 
progression and training required for the role.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R). 
Staff disagreed with this proposal because it claimed that Central Hudson did not 
adequately demonstrate that there is an immediate need for this position, as this is a new job title 
and scope that was identified and created in 2022.  Tr. 2599.  Staff states that since the Company 
has been able to maintain its I&C and relay needs through 2022 with its historical substation 
technician FTE headcount, the addition of this position is unnecessary.  Id.   
The Company disagrees with Staff’s assertion that these FTEs are unnecessary.  First, the 
Relay Technician position is new in title only – the core responsibilities of this position are like 
that of the historical Relay Technician, which became the Substation Technicians.  Tr. 2130-
2131.  Further, consideration must be given to the additional time required to configure and test 
the relays due to the rapid evolution of the relays with far more complex versions.  Tr. 2131.  
The growing backlog of work presents a risk to reliability and compliance and having the ability 
to address the backlog is another primary reason for the requested FTEs.  Id.  In addition, adding 
these FTEs is important to stay ahead of the impending retirement of the current Chief.  Id.

requirements and ensuring items such as the Davis-Bacon Act (local prevailing wages) are

--- Page 325 ---
 Initial Brief 
Appendix 2

technology more than ever to enable operational work.  Tr. 1374.  The Management Audit report 
in Case 21-M-0541 called for the establishment of a Project Management Office for all major IT 
programs and recommended that all other IT projects have a Project Manager.  Tr. 1373.  The

contractors, and security and compliance risks.  Tr. 1375-1376.

important outreach activities in the communities Central Hudson serves.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-
2R).   
 
Staff disagrees with this proposal and recommends the Commission disallow the 
Consumer Outreach Supervisor position, stating that the Company has not provided sufficient

Business Analyst (2) 
 
The Company is in need of two business analysts to support the design and testing of 
IVR, script tools, billing payments, outages and notifications in support of enhanced customer 
experience.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R). 
Staff recommended removing funding for the entire IVR Modernization project, claiming 
that the Company did not identify any risks associated with outages or service interruptions if the 
current IVR is left in place.  Tr. 4434.  However, it is important to recognize that the 
manufacturer is requiring a technical upgrade to the IVR software in order for it to remain 
supported by the vendor.  Tr. 3085.  Additionally, the objectives of the IVR Modernization 
project are to upgrade the current IVR Platform from an on-premises solution to a cloud-based 
solution with disaster recovery capabilities, the ability to easily develop new flows with a web-
based drag and drop configuration tool, and a test environment without the need of a third-party 
vendor.  Id.  Finally, it should be noted that in the Billing Reporting Requirements Stipulation, 
Staff stipulated to allow work to commence on this key project in the Rate Year.  Exhibit 514.  
Given that Staff’s removal of these FTEs appears to have been predicated on their initial 
recommendation to disallow the IVR Modernization project, these two positions should now be

Appendix 2

included in the revenue requirement.  The requested FTEs are essential to support the IVR 
Modernization project, which will drastically enhance the customer experience.  
UX Analyst 
 
Central Hudson needs one User Experience Analyst to enhance digital user experience 
and develop customer facing digital applications.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  Staff recommended 
that the Commission deny the FTEs and associated costs for the entire Customer Web and 
Mobile Technology project, based on concerns that the project is premature and, further, that the 
Company did not issue an RFP or evaluate if an outside vendor would be needed.  Tr. 4436.  
Staff also raised a concern that Central Hudson did not perform a cost benefit analysis.  Id. 
 
However, Staff’s logic is flawed.  An RFP and external vendor quotes are not required 
here as the Mobile App and Website enhancements are completed by internal resources.  Tr. 
3086.  Further, it is extremely difficult to quantify the value of providing expected customer 
experiences which are commonplace throughout many industries and generally seen as a cost of 
doing business, and weigh such value against the relatively minimal customer costs to complete 
the project.  Id.  As this FTE would enhance the digital user experience for customers, it should 
be adopted. 
Business Analyst (Billing)  
 
The Company needs a Business Analyst to support investigations associated with 
collections calls in order to provide customers with greater certainty regarding the accuracy of 
their arrears balances.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  Staff has stated that while it is concerned with 
the Company’s level of residential customers in arrears, it believes the Company lacks a plan 
regarding restarting collections and associated service terminations, such that it could not 
evaluate the appropriate level of staffing.  Tr. 4403.  However, the Company has demonstrated

The Company requires an Associate Risk Administrator to support activities associated 
with Management Audit recommendations 2.4 and 2.5 to enhance Enterprise Risk Management 
(“ERM”) reports and perform a formal assessment of the ERM program using a recognized 
maturity model.  Exhibit 257 (WCBP-2R).  Staff recommended a reduction of 0.5 FTEs for this 
position and relied on Overland’s November 2023 report, which noted that the activities 
associated with this position were historically performed by a person on roughly a half-time 
basis.  Tr. 877-878.  The Company disagrees with the removal of 0.5 FTE, as implementation of 
Recommendations 2.4 and 2.5 will provide various benefits, including increased documentation,
